A database that cannot be reached, a login that fails, a table that is not there, a query that fails when it runs, or a row the service cannot use is a **refusal to start** with a message and a status: `20` (the database: cannot connect, cannot log in, the query failed, a row with an empty field or a byte that is not printable, or a table over the text the service reads, 16 KiB when this was written and 32 KiB since section 25.1) or `13` (a row that `endpoints.parse` refuses: an id of seven digits or more since section 25.1 (16 or more before it), a secret that is not `whsec_` and base64; the message names the row). An **empty table is not a refusal**: the service starts with no endpoints and keeps the events it is given, as it always did with no `endpoints.conf`. The start **waits while the database is silent**: the blocking client has no timeout, so a database that accepts and never answers holds the start until it is killed (a test says so). That is the cost of reading the list before listening; a timeout is a change to `lexsys-pg`. *(Corrected by section 37: the start does not wait. The service listens, takes events, reads the table when the database answers and refuses (status 20) only for a login the server refuses for good, or when `pg-start-wait-ms` passes. "The table is the truth" stands: nothing is delivered before it has been read.)*

**One parser.** The table is written into a buffer as the same text `endpoints.conf` is (`id host port secret`, a line a row) and given to `endpoints.parse`, so a row is judged by the rule that judges a line of the file: nothing about "what is a valid endpoint" exists twice. Between the database and the parser each field must be non-empty and printable (a byte of 33 to 126), because a newline in a host would otherwise be a second line, and so a second endpoint.

**Importing.** `--import-endpoints 1` (a setting, so also a line in the settings file) reads `endpoints.conf` of `--dir`, checks it with `endpoints.parse`, inserts every line in one transaction (`on conflict do nothing`) and exits, saying `imported A of N endpoints`. A line whose id is in the table already is **left as it is, not updated**: a command that silently rewrote a receiver's host or secret would be the worse failure, and changing a row is C3d's. A file with a bad line imports none of it (status 13); a database that refuses a row part-way adds nothing (status 20). It needs no `--port`.

**The secret is in the table, in the clear.** The service signs with the key a `whsec_...` secret decodes to, so it has to be able to read it back; a hash would not do. Anyone who can read the table can sign as the service, and the schema file says so. This is the same exposure as `endpoints.conf` has, moved to the database's permissions.

**What the tests check** (`tests/roster_test.py`, against a real PostgreSQL, SCRAM when `HOOKS_PG_PASSWORD` is set; 33 checks): the import (every row as written, a second import adds none, a changed line changes nothing, a bad line imports none of the file, no file, no host, no database, a trigger that refuses the second line leaves the table empty and the same file imports whole without it, `0` or `1` only, no port needed); the service delivering to the table's endpoints and **not** to a different one in the file, each signed with its **own secret from the table** (the receiver verifies the Standard Webhooks signature itself); a row removed and a restart (it is no longer delivered to); an empty table; a secret that is not base64, an id of 20, a host with a space, an empty host and 201 rows of 250-byte hosts (each refused with its own message, the last not a crash); no table, a view that prepares and then fails when read, no database, a wrong password; the file as before without `pg-host`; and a database that is silent. The history suite changed with it: a database that is not there at start is now a refusal (it was "starts, drops rows"), and its helper fills the table instead of the file.

**Mutants of the new code: 19 run, 15 killed, 4 survive.** Killed, among others: a space or an empty field allowed through, no overflow guard on the buffer, every row counted as added, no transaction around the import, a missing table reported as a failed login, a failed query read as an empty table, the length of the text lost, the table never used, the row number wrong or the status of a bad row, no import without a port, the import flag never set. Four survive and none is a gap in the tests: the import's own check of the SQL error in the reply is redundant with the status the client already gives (a failed statement answers a nonzero status), a `commit` after a failed statement is a rollback in PostgreSQL, so never sending `rollback` changes nothing, the default password `-` is exercised only under a server that trusts the connection (as in section C3a), and accepting a seven-digit id changes nothing because the parser then refuses any id of 16 or more (it refused them as ids above 15 when this was written; section 25.1 made it six digits).

**Found along the way.**

* **`history.login` answered 6 for a table that was not there, and `roster` called it "cannot log in".** Preparing the statements is part of the login there, so a missing `endpoints` table looked like a wrong password. It answers a status of its own now (`prepare_failed`, 16) and the message says the query failed. A side effect: **every** user of the database now needs both tables, `attempts` and `endpoints`, even one that only wants the history, because all four statements are prepared on every connection; `sql/schema.sql` makes both.
* **`import` is a keyword in lex-sys**, and so is `connect` (a builtin): the module's functions are `copy_in` and `open_db`.
* **My first test of "a query that fails when it runs" did not test that.** It used a view that raises when selected, which could not be inserted into, so the *prepare* of `add_endpoint` failed and the service refused for that reason; the mutant that ignores a failed reply survived. An updatable view whose filter raises prepares and then fails, and kills it.
* **A test raced itself**: the stage with no database reused a directory that held an event from the stage before, so the file's endpoint was sent two events and `wait_for` returned at the first. It uses a fresh directory.
* **The service now needs the database before it listens**, which changes what `listening` on stderr means for a harness: it still means the service is up, but the start can take as long as the database takes to answer.

**What is next.** C3d: `POST /endpoints` and the rest, taking effect without a restart. That is the slice that has to change a delivery state of fixed tables while it runs (the table is six integers an endpoint, sized at start), and it needs a design of its own before any code.

## 25. C3d: managing endpoints without a restart (design, written before any code)

*Nothing in this section is built. It is the design, the gates and the questions, so that they are fixed before the code and the code can be held to them. Where a decision is marked **to confirm** it changes behaviour a person may rely on.*

**What is wanted.** `POST /endpoints` (register a URL and the event types it wants; the answer carries the secret, once), `GET /endpoints/:id`, `PATCH /endpoints/:id`, `DELETE /endpoints/:id`, taking effect **without a restart**, with the database as the owner of the configuration (section 3) and the log as the owner of delivery state. (Event types: section 5 says an endpoint "wants" some; nothing in the service filters by type yet, so **filtering is not in this slice** and the column is not added.)

**What the code does today that this has to be built on** (read from the source, not assumed):

1. **State is keyed by the endpoint's number**, 0 to 15: the cursor `cur[e]`, a window of 1,024 cells for each number, `flying[e]`, one bit in `c_disabled`. The outcome log records the number, and the history table's key is `(endpoint, event, replay, attempt)`. The number is both the endpoint's *identity* and its *slot*.
2. **The table of endpoints** (host, port, key) is positional, six integers an endpoint, with the host and the key in one 16 KiB blob that is filled once at start. There is no way to remove or grow an entry.
3. **A new endpoint starts at event 0**: its cursor is 0, so it is sent every event still in the log (and an endpoint more than 1,024 events behind is not served until it catches up). That is right for the file, where "the endpoint was always there", and wrong for an endpoint created today.
4. **`request_for` takes the key when an attempt starts**, so a changed key is used by the next attempt and not by one already on the wire.
5. **Nothing in the service is authenticated.** Every route can be called by anyone who can reach the port.

**Decisions.**

**D1. Identity and slot are different things (to confirm).** Reusing a number after a delete makes three things wrong at once: the old endpoint's outcome records in the log replay into the new one's state, its rows in `attempts` collide with the new one's (`on conflict do nothing` would silently drop the new one's), and a late attempt of the old endpoint could be recorded against the new one. So: an endpoint has an **id** (a database sequence, a `bigint`, never reused, what the API and the history use) and a **slot** (0 to `max_endpoints() - 1`, what the state arrays use, reused). The mapping slot → id is **in the log**: a record `endpoint created (slot, id, start cursor)` and `endpoint removed (slot)`, replayed in order at start, so a slot's outcome records belong to the endpoint created before them. `max_endpoints()` rises from 16 to **62** (the disabled set is one integer's bits; the cells for 62 slots are 62 × 1,024 × 3 × 8 bytes = 1.5 MB). The history's `endpoint` column becomes the id (`bigint`); the rows already written are of ids 0 to 15, which is what the importer will make the first ids, so nothing is rewritten.

**D2. Where a new endpoint starts (to confirm).** An endpoint created through the API starts **from now**: its start cursor is the last event id when it is created, carried in the `created` record, so it gets events from then on and not the log's past. The request may say `"from": "start"` to be sent the log's backlog (limited by the window: it is served as it catches up, as today). *An endpoint that is in the table at start with no `created` record* (the rows C3c imports, or a row somebody inserted with `psql`) **was to keep today's behaviour and start at 0**, and a record is written for it, so that the next start does not do it again. *(Superseded by section 25.3: it starts at the slowest cursor of the others, 0 if there are none, because starting at 0 stalls them.)* That keeps C3c's meaning and makes the new behaviour opt-out only for the API, which is the one place a person has said "create".

**D3. The two stores, and the order that makes a crash harmless.** A change is: (1) the database transaction (insert, update or delete the row), then (2) on its commit, the log record and the change to the tables in memory, answered after the record is flushed. A crash between 1 and 2 leaves the row without a record, which is exactly the state D2 describes for a row from `psql`: it is found at the next start and handled by the same rule, **there is no second recovery path**. For a delete the order is the same and the crash leaves a slot with a `created` record whose row is gone: at start, a slot whose id is not in the table is **removed** (a `removed` record is written), again by a rule that already has to exist. The database is the owner; the log never invents an endpoint.

**D4. One change at a time.** A change holds the connection (`server.hold`, as `GET /events/:id/attempts` does) while its transaction runs on the pool; a second change while one is waiting is `409`. A change the database does not answer in five seconds is a `504` and the transaction is **not** assumed to have failed: the next start reconciles (D3) and `GET /endpoints/:id` says what the service believes. The service never applies a change to memory before the database has said commit.

**D5. What a change does to what is in flight.**
* *Create*: nothing to wait for. The slot is zeroed (`cur` set to the start cursor, its cells cleared), the table entry appended, `c_endpoints` raised.
* *Patch of host or port*: the next attempt uses it; an attempt on the wire finishes against the old address. The blob entry is rewritten in place if it fits, else appended; the blob is compacted when it is full (the entries are short and few, so this is a copy, done between turns).
* *Patch of the secret*: replaces it, and the next attempt, **including a retry of an event first attempted under the old secret**, is signed with the new one. A receiver that has not been given the new secret refuses until it has. Standard Webhooks lets a message carry **two** signatures for exactly this rotation (`v1,a v1,b`); sending both for a fixed period is the better design and is **not in this slice** (it needs the old key kept, a time, and a test against the reference library's multi-signature verification). The API says so rather than hiding it.
* *Delete*: **no new attempt** is started for the slot from the moment it is applied; attempts on the wire are allowed to finish and their outcome is recorded (and written to the history) as for any endpoint; waiting replays for the slot are dropped, each with a record; the cells and the cursor are discarded when `flying[slot]` is 0 and the slot is only then reusable (a `removed` record is written at that moment, not at the request: when `flying[slot]` is already 0 at the request, that moment is the request, and section 25.5 says what the code does in each case). Rows in `attempts` are kept: they are for people.

**D6. The secret.** `POST /endpoints` generates it (`whsec_` and the base64 of 24 bytes from `/dev/urandom`, the nonce's source) unless the request names one; it is in the `201` once and **no read returns it again** (`GET` answers the id, the address, whether it is disabled and the cursor, never the secret). It is stored in the clear in the table, for the reason C3c gave.

**D7. Authority (to confirm, and the part most likely to be wrong).** An endpoint is an address the service will `POST` to, chosen by whoever can call this API: with no authentication that is a request-forgery tool for anything the service can reach, and secrets are returned on create. So **every route that changes an endpoint requires a bearer token, `--admin-token` (a setting, so a line in the settings file, not a flag by habit), and with no token set they answer `403`**: a service that was not given one is read-only, which is the safe default, and an existing deployment is not changed by this slice. The comparison is constant-time. This does not make `POST /events` or the existing `POST /endpoints/:id/enable` authenticated: that is a separate decision and is listed below. Not in this slice (built in section 26): an allow-list or deny-list of destination hosts (private ranges, the metadata address); it is the real defence against request forgery from a stolen token and is the first thing to add after it, and the doc for the endpoint says that the token is the only barrier until then.

**D8. Validation** is the file's (`endpoints.parse`: a host with no whitespace, a port 1 to 65535, a secret that is `whsec_` and base64) plus a body limit and a count limit (`409` at 62 live endpoints). Errors are `problem+json`, as `lexsys-web` does; `lexsys-schema` is not used here because the three fields are checked by the parser that already judges the file, and a second description of what is valid is the thing C3c removed.

**The slices, in order, each with its own tests and mutants:**

1. **State and recovery, no API.** The slot/id split, `max_endpoints()` 62, the `created`/`removed` records, the start-time reconcile of D2 and D3, the history's `bigint` id. Gate: every older suite unchanged; a log written by the current binary (with no `created` records) is read by the new one and yields the same state; a kill between the database and the log in each direction, by hand-made tables and logs.
2. **`POST /endpoints` and `GET /endpoints/:id`, with the token.** Gate: an endpoint created while 1,000 events are in the log gets none of them, and gets event 1,001; `"from":"start"` gets the backlog; the secret is in one answer and no other; no token is `403`; a wrong token is `401`; the transaction fails (a trigger) and nothing changed in memory or the log; a kill after the commit and before the record, and the next start has the endpoint.
3. **`PATCH`.** Gate: a host change reaches the next attempt and not the one on the wire; a secret change signs the next attempt and the receiver verifies it with the **new** secret and refuses the old; a patch that the database refuses changes nothing.
4. **`DELETE`.** Gate: no attempt starts after it; one on the wire finishes and is recorded; replays for it are dropped; the slot is reused by a new endpoint that gets **none** of the old one's cursor, window or outcome records, across a restart (the case D1 exists for); the history keeps the old rows and the new endpoint's rows do not collide with them.

**Open questions, for a person.**

* **D1 and D2 are behaviour.** Is "an endpoint created through the API starts from now" what you want, with `"from":"start"` for the backlog? And is it right that a row that appears in the table without the API keeps starting at 0?
* **D7.** Is a bearer token, read-only without one, the authority you want for the management routes? Should `POST /events` and `/endpoints/:id/enable` be put behind a token too (that would break every client that does not send one, which is why it is not assumed)?
* **62 endpoints** is a limit of this design (the disabled set is an integer). Is it enough?
* **Secret rotation** with two signatures is deferred: is a PATCH that replaces the secret acceptable for a first version, with the receiver refusing in between?

**A bug found while planning slice 1, and fixed first (it is older than this section).** The delivery state's offsets table (`offs`: where each event of the window starts in the events log, 1,024 entries) began at 161 and the cells at 145 + 1,024 = 1,169, **16 integers inside it**. An event whose number is 1,008 to 1,023 modulo 1,024 had its offset overwritten when the first cells of endpoint 0 were written (they sit at 1,169 and up), so a *retry* of such an event read the log at a wrong offset and the event was not delivered; and as it could not become final, the cursor stayed below it and **the endpoint stopped being served once it was a window (1,024 events) behind**. Reproduced on the binary of the previous merge with a receiver that fails event 1,011 once: 1,011 is never delivered and, with 2,300 events, 2,035 and everything after it is not either. The first window is the one a test that posts fewer than about 1,000 events never leaves, which is why nothing earlier saw it, and the failure needs an event to fail *and* be retried in those 16 places, which is why production would see it rarely and badly. The fix is `off_cells() = off_offs() + span`; `tests/layout_test.py` posts 2,300 events, fails 1,011 and 2,035 once each and requires every event delivered with its own body (it fails on the previous binary and passes on this one). Slice 1 below rewrites the layout as a chain of computed offsets so that a region cannot be placed by hand again.

### 25.1 Slice 1, built: endpoint ids and slots

*Written after the code and its tests; where it differs from the plan above it says so.*

**What exists now.** An endpoint has an **id** (0 to 999999: what the table, the API and the history call it) and a **slot** (0 to 61: its place in the arrays of the delivery state). `state.max_endpoints()` is 62 and the layout is a chain of computed offsets. The log says which endpoint has which slot with two new kinds of record in `delivery.seg`: `created` (kind 10: slot, id, the cursor the slot starts at) and `removed` (kind 11: slot). `state.reset` is what recovery does for either: the slot's window is empty and its cursor is the record's. Nothing creates a `removed` record yet except the one rule below, and in this slice `created` always started at 0 (section 25.3 changed that: it starts where the slowest endpoint is); **no API exists in this slice**; what it changes is what a table with an id above 15 or 62 rows means, and the ground the next slices stand on.

**How a start decides the slots** (`scan_slots`, `match_slots`, the replay, then `place_new` (section 25.3 moved the placing after the replay; it was `assign_slots` before it); all before the service listens):

1. *Pass one* reads the outcome log for the slot map. `created` gives a slot its endpoint's id, `removed` frees it, and a **slot first met in an ordinary outcome belongs to the endpoint with that id** (that is what the number in an outcome was in every log written before this): so a log from the previous version needs no migration and **no record is written for the endpoints it already knows** (a test removes the slot records from a log the service wrote and starts on the result: no event is sent again, the cursors are where they were). An outcome for a slot that was freed is stale and does not give it back.
2. *Each endpoint of the table* takes the slot that has its id. One that has none takes the slot with its own number if that is free (so `0..15` stay where they were, and a person reading the log sees what they expect), else the lowest free slot, and a `created` record (flushed, before the first outcome) says so, with the cursor it starts at (25.3: the slowest cursor of the endpoints the log knows, 0 if there are none).
3. *No free slot* (62 are taken): an endpoint that is in the log and **not in the table** is **dormant**, and the lowest dormant slot is freed with a `removed` record and given to the newcomer. A slot of an endpoint that **is** in the table is never taken (a mutant that did takes it, and a test with 62 slots, one of them live, kills it).
4. *Pass two* is the replay of section 15, with two additions: `created` and `removed` reset the slot (window, cursor, disabled bit, waiting replays), so a second endpoint in a slot inherits nothing from the first, and the records of a slot that no table endpoint has are skipped as before.

**A refinement of D3 that the code needed.** D3 said a slot whose id is not in the table at start is *removed*. That is destructive on a mistake: a table restored from a backup, or emptied by a bad migration, would free every slot and, when the rows came back, send every event again from the start. The code does the milder thing: an endpoint missing at start is **dormant**, its slot and its state kept (as the log always kept them: nothing is trimmed), so **an endpoint that comes back resumes where it was** (a test removes one for three events and restores it: it gets exactly those three). Its slot is freed only when someone else needs it, and from then on it is a new endpoint when it returns (it starts at the slowest cursor, 0 before 25.3: a test says so too). A DELETE (slice 4, section 25.5) writes `removed` itself, when the slot is free: at once if no attempt of the endpoint is on the wire, else when the last one has ended.

**What changed for a person.**

* An endpoint id may be up to six digits and a table may hold 62 endpoints (it was 16). The API takes the id: `POST /endpoints/:id/enable`, `POST /events/:id/replay/:endpoint`, and `GET /endpoints` says it. An id that is not a number is a `400`, one that is not there a `404` (a number of 16 or more was a `400`).
* `attempts.endpoint` is a `bigint` and the history says the **id**, not the slot (for ids 0 to 15 they were the same number). `sql/schema.sql` widens an existing table's column; `endpoints.id` is an `integer`.
* The text the service reads (the file, or the table written as one) may be 32 KiB (it was 16): 62 endpoints at most 253 bytes of host each do not fit in 16.
* `delivery.seg` has records of kinds 10 and 11, which a reader written before this change refuses: **a log written by this version cannot be read by the previous one.** (Starting the previous binary on it stops with the status for an unknown record; nothing is lost.)

**What the tests check** (`tests/slots_test.py`, new; the log is read and written by the test's own code, so the record format is checked and not only the service's agreement with itself): the `created` records of a first start, in order, and none from a restart; the legacy log; ids 20 and 999999 (slot 20 for its own number, slot 0 for the other), delivery, restart, the API by id (200, 404, 400), replay by id; dormant, then back; 62 slots taken and two newcomers (the lowest two freed, in that order, nothing else touched); a live endpoint's slot not taken; a hand-written log in which endpoint 7's slot was disabled, had a replay waiting, delivered five events, was removed, had a stale `failed` arrive, and was given to endpoint 9, which must get all five events once each, not be disabled and have no replay; a `created` that starts at 3; and the history under an id of 999999. The older suites are unchanged except for the refusals that changed meaning (`400` to `404`) and the reader of `attempt_test`, which now skips the slot records.

**Mutants of the new code: 21 run, 19 killed, 2 survive and are equivalent.** Killed, among others: `created` mapping a slot to itself, `removed` not freeing, a stale record bringing a freed slot back (found by a case I added after it first survived: stale deliveries after the last `removed`), no preference for the id's own slot, the first pass matching nothing, reclaim taking a live endpoint's slot or a slot with id 0 counted free, the start cursor of `created` ignored, `reset` keeping the disabled bit or the waiting replays or doing nothing, the sequence number not advanced after pass one (the slot records are then refused), the slot records not flushed, enable, replay and `GET /endpoints` taking or saying the slot instead of the id, the history saying the slot, the cells overlapping the offsets again, and 16 for 62. Two survive: the first pass reads the table's slot field, which holds the id until it is assigned (the same number), and a log with the same id in two slots, which nothing the service writes can make, resolves to the last instead of the first. Three first results were not mutants killed but a flaky test: the check that endpoint 0 "gets 6 to 8" compared lists and the events arrive in any order; it compares sorted lists now.

**Found along the way.**

* **The offsets table overlapped the cells** (above): found because the layout had to be rewritten, fixed first.
* **A region of `main` has 64 KiB and the text is 32 KiB**, so a second copy of it (the scratch table `endpoints.parse` is checked into) does not fit: the first version trapped (`SIGILL`, status -4 in the test) in both the import and the start. The check is on the heap now (`parse_check`).
* **`prepare` now writes** (the slot records), so its effect row has `file_write`, and the lex-sys checker, which refuses a declared effect it does not perform and one it performs and does not declare, said so.
* **A test of mine expected the slot records to be written back for a legacy log.** They are not, and need not be: that is the design (an endpoint known by its outcomes already has its slot). The test now says that.

### 25.2 Slice 2, built: `POST /endpoints` and `GET /endpoints/:id`

*Written after the code and its tests.*

**What exists now.** `POST /endpoints` creates an endpoint and `GET /endpoints/:id` reads one (the same four fields as the list: id, port, cursor, disabled; never the host, never the secret). It needs a database and an `admin-token` (a setting: 8 to 255 visible characters, in the settings file by preference; `GET /config` does not show it). The request is `{"host", "port"}` and optionally `"secret"` (`whsec_` and base64; the service makes one if it is left out: `whsec_` and the base64 of 24 bytes from `/dev/urandom`) and `"from": "now"`.

**Who may call it (D7).** No `admin-token` configured: `403`, whatever the request carries, so a service that was not given one is read-only. A missing token, the wrong one, a prefix of it, more than it, a token over 255 bytes, another scheme, two `Authorization` headers or the token in the query: `401` with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer`. The scheme word is not case sensitive and the token is; the comparison looks at all 255 positions whichever byte differs first. The existing routes (`POST /events`, `POST /endpoints/:id/enable`, the reads) are **not** behind the token: that is the open question of section 25 and was left as recommended, which is to change nothing for an existing client in this slice. **The token is the only barrier**: an endpoint is an address the service will `POST` to, so a holder of the token can make it send requests to any host it can reach (an allow-list of destinations is the next thing to add, and is not here).

**What a request may say.** The host: a string of 1 to 253 printable characters with no space. The port: an integer from 1 to 65535. The secret, if given, must decode as the base64 `endpoints.parse` accepts (it is judged by the same function, **before** the database is touched: a mutant that skipped it wrote a row with an unusable secret, and a row like that is a refusal to start). `from` may be `"now"` or absent: **`"from": "start"` is refused** (`400`), which D2 had promised and this slice does not do; see the finding below. Other members are ignored.

**The order (D3, D4).** The request is judged in `handle` and kept (`manage.ls`: the block `mg` of the delivery state); `run` makes the secret if there is none, sends `insert into endpoints ... returning id` to the database on the pool, and **holds the connection**. The id is `greatest(nextval('endpoint_ids'), max(id) + 1)`: a sequence, so an id is never given twice even after its row is deleted, and one past any row inserted by hand. *(That was not quite true, and section 25.5 corrects it: a row inserted by hand is not taken from the sequence, so deleting the highest such row and creating an endpoint gave its id again. `DELETE` now moves the sequence past the id it deletes, in the same statement; a row deleted with `psql` can still have its id given again.)* When the database answers, and only then, `finish_create` takes a slot (an endpoint of that id that the log remembers is freed first: it is a different endpoint), writes `created(slot, id, start)` with `start` the id of the last event, flushes, resets the slot's window to that cursor, appends to the table and answers `201 {"id","host","port","secret","from","cursor"}`: **the secret is in this answer and in no other**. One change at a time: a second request while one waits is `409`, as is a 63rd endpoint. A refused insert (a trigger raising) is a `503` and **nothing changes in the service or the log**; a database that does not answer in five seconds is a `504`, the service does not know the endpoint, and the row, which may still commit, is an endpoint at the next start. As a row the service did not create, it **starts at the beginning of the log** (section 25.1's rule), which is the finding below.

**A bug the first test of an id the log remembers found.** `prepare` read the slot map and the log's last sequence number only when there were endpoints at start. A service that started with none and created one later had a slot map of zeros (every slot "taken by endpoint 0") and appended its `created` record with sequence 0 into a log whose last record was higher: the log refused it, a `503` for a good request. The map and the sequence are read always now (`scan_slots` before the `if n > 0`).

**Finding: a row added by hand to a service with a long log stalls every other endpoint, and it is older than this slice.** Measured on this branch's binary with the default schedule: endpoint A has delivered 3,000 events; endpoint B's row is inserted by hand (its receiver is down) and the service is restarted; B starts at 0, so the slowest cursor is 0 and the scan of the events log is limited to 1,024 events past it; **A is sent none of the next ten events** (B's cursor was 0 and A's 3,000 when the probe ended), and would not be until B reaches within a window of A, which for a receiver that is down is as long as its retry schedule: up to a day. (With a schedule of 100 ms B dead-letters 3,000 events in seconds and nothing is seen, which is why a test with a scaled schedule does not show it.) The same happens with a line added to `endpoints.conf`. It is why `POST /endpoints` starts from now and refuses `"from": "start"`: a backfill is not a request this design can honour without the others paying for it. **The rule that fixes the rows too is built in section 25.3.**

**What the tests check** (`tests/manage_test.py`, against a real PostgreSQL; the receivers verify the Standard Webhooks signature themselves): who may call (13 cases); what a request may say (18 refusals, an accepted unknown member); the answer's shape, the secret's form, the row equal to the answer, the secret that came with the request kept as it came, and the secret, the host and the token in no read; the endpoint starting from now (five events already in the log, none sent to it, the sixth sent and signed with its secret, and the old endpoint sent nothing twice), the first endpoint of a service that had none; a restart (cursors, nothing sent again, the `created` records with their slot, id and starting cursor); ids (0, 1, 2; after the highest row is deleted 3; after a row inserted by hand 51; an id of 100, whose slot is not 100, read by its id); an id the log remembers (the old endpoint 7 would have had events 4 and 5, the new one gets neither, and the old slot was freed); a database that refuses; two requests at once; a database that takes seven seconds; 62 endpoints and a 63rd; no database named; an `admin-token` of 7 characters or with a space refused at start.

**Mutants of the new code: 25 run, 25 killed.** Three of them are killed only in combination, which is the honest way to say it: the length of the given token not being compared, the loop over the longest token there can be, and the 255-byte limit each survive alone, because each is covered by another (a different length makes a byte differ or the length differ); the mutant that removes the length comparison **and** loops only over the given bytes lets a prefix of the token in, and the test of a prefix kills it. One mutant that removed the first-endpoint seek of the scan position did not build (an unused variable) and is equivalent: the scan then starts from the beginning and reaches the same place. Four more (the `WWW-Authenticate` header, the id-as-slot read of `GET /endpoints/:id`, a remembered id resuming, the slot map read only when there are endpoints) first survived or were found by a later test and killed by cases added for them, as the section 25.1 mutants were.

### 25.3 A row nobody recorded starts where the slowest endpoint is

*Section 30 removed the shared scan this rule protected; the rule is kept as a policy, and `lowmark` is now `slowest_cursor`. What follows is as it was written.*

**The rule (decision 1 of 25.2, built).** At start, an endpoint of the table that the log has no slot for (a row inserted by hand, a line added to `endpoints.conf`, the first start of a service with a history of events) is placed **after** the replay, when the cursors are known: it takes a slot as before, and starts at the **slowest cursor of the endpoints the log does know** (`lowmark`; 0 if there are none, which is the first start of everything and keeps C3c's meaning). The cursor is written in its `created` record, so the next start reads it and does nothing. Why that cursor and not "now": it is the furthest back an endpoint can start without widening the window the others are held to (the scan of the events log is limited to 1,024 events past the slowest cursor), so it can never stall them; an endpoint that starts behind the others is the group's own slowest member, which is where a down receiver ends up anyway. The cost, said plainly: such an endpoint is sent the backlog the *slowest* endpoint still has (up to 1,024 events), not the whole log and not only the future. To get the future only, create the endpoint with `POST /endpoints` (`"from": "now"`).

Code: `assign_slots` is two functions, `match_slots` (the table's endpoints take the slots the log says; an unknown one keeps -1, and `lowmark` skips it) before the replay, and `place_new` (`take_slot`, the `created` record, `state.reset` to the cursor) after it.

**Tests (`tests/slots_test.py`, 8 and 8b; 4 and 5 changed meaning).** 8: slot 0 at cursor 3 (receiver down), slot 1 at cursor 8, a new endpoint 12: it gets events 4 to 8 once each, the one that had them all gets none, and the `created` record says slot 12, id 12, start 3. 8b is the stall: endpoint A alone delivers 1,300 events; a second row is added whose receiver is down, with a retry schedule of an hour; the next event reaches A, and B's `created` says start 1,300. **Mutant: starting at 0 fails 8b (A is sent nothing, cursors `{0: 1300, 1: 0}`), 8, 4 and 5**, which is the stall reproduced and then removed. Tests 4 and 5 said a new endpoint gets the whole log; they now say it gets the log from the slowest cursor. (Test 4 also waits for the cursors before stopping: it had been stopping the service between a delivery and its record, which is an at-least-once repeat and not a bug.) All other suites unchanged and green.

**Found along the way.**

* **`fs_read` inside a condition is a compiler bug**: `if fs_read(fs, "/dev/urandom", raw) != 24 {` failed code generation (`cannot determine the scalar kind of FileOp`); binding the result first works. (For lex-sys, not fixed here.)
* **`http.reason` has no phrase for 409, 504 or 507** (the status line says `Unknown`); the codes are right and a client reads the code. (lex-sys.)
* **The pending-change block is 368 integers, a byte to an integer**, because the delivery state is integers; it is the price of one array. A `Bytes` region in the state would remove it.
* **Every user of the database now needs the `endpoint_ids` sequence**, because all five statements are prepared on every connection and one names it: an existing deployment must re-run `sql/schema.sql` (it is idempotent) before the new binary, or the service refuses to start with "the query failed".

### 25.4 Slice 3, built: `PATCH /endpoints/:id`

**What it does.** Changes the host, the port or the secret of an endpoint, without a restart, behind the admin token (the same `403`/`401` as `POST`). The body names any of `host`, `port`, `secret` (a secret to use: `whsec_` and base64) and `rotate` (`true`: the service makes one, as `POST` does when it is not given one; refused together with `secret`); an unknown member is ignored, a body that names none of the four is a `400`. The host is judged by the same rule as in `POST` (section 26). The answer is `200 {"id","host","port"}`, and **`"secret"` when the change brought or made one: it is in that answer and in no other**.

**How it is applied (D3, D4, D5).** The request is judged in `handle` and kept in the block `mg` of the delivery state (which gained three integers: what it is, the id it is for, and the members it names); `run` sends **one statement**, `update endpoints set host, port[, secret] where id = $1 returning id`, to the database and holds the connection. The members the request did not name are filled in from the endpoint as it is now, so one statement serves. Memory changes **only when the database says commit** (`finish_patch`): the host, port and key of the endpoint's entry are replaced; a statement that returns no row (the row is gone) is a `404` and nothing changes; one the database refuses is a `503` and nothing changes; one it does not answer in five seconds is a `504`, the row may have changed, and the next start reads the table, so there is no second recovery path (D3). One change at a time: a `PATCH` or a `POST` while one waits is a `409`.

**What it does to what is in flight (D5).** The next attempt uses the new address and key, **a retry of an event first tried under the old secret included**: `request_for` reads the key when an attempt starts, and the address is read when it connects. An attempt already on the wire finishes against what it began with (a test holds a receiver's answer for 1.2 s, moves the endpoint meanwhile, and the held attempt ends delivered at the old address without the new one being sent that event). **There is one signature.** After a secret change a receiver that has not been given the new secret refuses until it has; Standard Webhooks lets a message carry two (`v1,a v1,b`) for exactly this and sending both for a period is the better design: not built, because it needs the old key kept, a time, and a test against the reference library's multi-signature check. The README and the product page say so.

**The blob (`endpoints.replace`, `endpoints.compact`).** The hosts and keys live in one blob of 32 KiB. A change appends the new bytes after the used ones and leaves the old ones behind; when the blob is full, `compact` moves everything to the front in the order of the table (through a scratch copy), and if the new bytes fit only without the endpoint's own old ones, those are taken out too. If they do not fit however it is packed (not reachable with 62 endpoints and a host of at most 253 bytes, but the code does not assume it) the answer is a `507` that says the row was stored and the service must be restarted, and nothing in memory has changed.

**Tests (`tests/patch_test.py`, 11 stages against a real PostgreSQL; the receivers verify the signature themselves, under the old and the new secret).** Who may call it (a missing, wrong or truncated token, an unknown id, an id that is not a number, no token configured, no database), 19 bodies that are not a change (each a `400` with a reason, the row unchanged), an unknown member ignored; a move while an attempt is on the wire; a secret that is changed, verified under the new and not the old, and in no read; a retry signed with the new secret; `rotate` (24 bytes of base64, in the row, in the answer, verifying, a second one different); a trigger that refuses the update (`503`, the old address and secret still in force, and the next change works); a row deleted by hand (`404`); a trigger that sleeps (`409` for a second `PATCH` and for a `POST`, and the first completes); a restart (the cursor kept, the new address and secret in force, nothing sent again); the destination rule (a loopback host and a name are a `400`, the row unchanged; a public one is a `200`; a change that names only the port keeps the host; a `POST` after `PATCH`es still creates); and 400 changes of the host between a 253-byte name and an address, with the other endpoint intact and the last change in force, which fills the blob several times. **Mutants of the new code: 13 run, 13 killed.** The port bound (65536 allowed); `rotate` with a `secret` allowed; the destination rule skipped by `PATCH`; the `mg` kind not reset by `POST` (a `POST` after a `PATCH` was then a `PATCH`); the port, and the host, not filled in from the endpoint when the request did not name them; the key never replaced; a waiting change not refused; the unknown-id check and the token check skipped; a row that is gone applied; `compact` not copying back; `replace` not releasing the endpoint's own bytes. **One survived the end-to-end test at first and is the reason for its shape:** with `compact` not copying back, stage 11 still passed, because the endpoint that moved had the length it had been created with when the blob filled, so the other endpoint's bytes were at the place the broken compaction put them by coincidence. The test now creates the moving endpoint with a host of 13 bytes, which is neither of the two lengths it is changed to, and the mutant fails it. (`replace` not releasing its own bytes is killed by the unit test only: with 62 endpoints and hosts of at most 253 bytes the blob cannot be that full, so the case is not reachable from the API.) Unit tests (`tests/endpoints_test.ls`): `replace` changes one endpoint and leaves the others, `compact` packs without changing what any endpoint says, `replace` compacts when full, takes the endpoint's own old bytes out when only that makes room, and refuses without changing anything when it cannot.

### 25.5 Slice 4, built: `DELETE /endpoints/:id`

*Written with the code and its tests. Where the plan above said something the code had to change, this section says so, and the plan is corrected in place where it was a claim (D1's "never reused" and the slice 4 gate).*

**What it does.** Removes an endpoint, without a restart, behind the admin token (the same `403`/`401` as `POST` and `PATCH`, then `503` with no database, `400` for an id that is not a number, `404` for an id the service does not have, `409` while another change waits for the database). The answer is `200 {"id":N,"deleted":true,"draining":false}`. Every refusal leaves the endpoint, its row, the log and the counters as they were.

**How it is applied (D3, D4).** The request is judged in `handle` and kept in the block `mg` as kind 2 (`manage.mg_kind()`: 0 a new endpoint, 1 a change, 2 a delete; the pending change is the same block, so *one change at a time* is the same `409`); `run` sends one statement, `with gone as (delete from endpoints where id = $1::int returning id) select id, setval('endpoint_ids', greatest(nextval('endpoint_ids'), id), true) from gone`, and holds the connection. **The service changes only when the database says commit** (`finish_delete`): first the records, flushed (a `replay_dead` for each replay that waits, and the `removed` record if no attempt is on the wire), then memory. A statement the database refuses is a `503` and nothing changes; one it does not answer in five seconds is a `504`, the row may still go, and the next start reconciles it by the rule that already exists (the endpoint's row is gone, so its slot is dormant: D3). There is no second recovery path.

**What it does to what is in flight (D5, as built).**

* **No new attempt starts.** The endpoint leaves the table in the same call that commits, so `start_attempts` (which walks the table) never sees it again; its waiting window attempts and their retries simply stop.
* **An attempt on the wire finishes and is recorded** exactly as for any endpoint: an outcome record in the log, a row in `attempts` under the endpoint's **id**, the counters. (It needed a fix: the id for the history was looked up in the table, and the table no longer has the endpoint. `ident_of_slot` reads the slot map now, which holds the id until the slot is freed.) A failed attempt is recorded as failed, with the time of a next attempt, and **no next attempt happens**: so a history row of outcome 2 ("will be tried again") for a deleted endpoint is the honest record of that attempt and not a promise; the same for a replay (`replay_failed`, and then a `replay_dead` that ends it). A `410` on such an attempt is a dead letter and does not disable the deleted endpoint (there is nothing left to disable).
* **Waiting replays are dropped**, each with a `replay_dead` record (flushed before the answer): a record kind that recovery already reads as "this replay is over", so no new kind and no reader that does not know it. A replay that is on the wire is not dropped: it finishes, and is recorded.
* **The slot is freed when nothing of it is on the wire** (`flying[slot] == 0`), and **then** the `removed` record is written (not at the request): at once if nothing was on the wire, else in the turn in which the last attempt ends (`finish_drains`, after `settle` and `sweep`). Until then the slot is **draining**: one bit of `c_draining`, not free (its id is still in the slot map) and not dormant (its id is not in the table either, which is what a dormant slot looks like, so `take_slot` skips it explicitly). When it is freed the window, the cursor, the disabled bit and any replay entry of the slot are reset, as `created` and `removed` make a restart do.
* **`POST /endpoints` while a slot drains** does not get it. With a slot free it gets that one; with all 62 taken (live and draining together) it is a `409` *before* anything is written to the database (a row the service has nowhere to put would be an endpoint at the next start and not now): "every slot is taken: a deleted endpoint is still finishing an attempt". That window is at most the attempt's deadline (2 s by default). A dormant slot (a row removed while the service was down) is still reclaimed when it is needed, as in 25.1, but never a draining one.
* `GET /endpoints` and `GET /endpoints/:id` stop showing the endpoint **at once** (decision: not marked "draining": a deleted endpoint is not an endpoint, and the number of slots that are draining is `draining` in `/stats`). `POST /endpoints/:id/enable`, a replay to it and a `PATCH` of it are `404`.

**The table has an entry removed (`endpoints.remove`), and no index is kept across turns.** Removing entry `i` moves the later entries down one place, clears the vacated row, and zeroes the removed entry's host and key in the blob (so a deleted endpoint's signing key is not left in memory); the bytes of every other entry stay where they are, so their `host_start`/`key_start` stay right, and `blob_used` shrinks if the removed one was last (what is left in the middle is room that `compact` gives back). I went through every place that holds a **table index** or the **count**: `start_one` and `start_replay` take `i` and read the table in the same call; `start_attempts` fixes `count` and `i` for one turn and a delete is applied only between turns (in the answer of the pool, outside `delivery_turn`), and with `count == 0` its loops do not run (the `% count` is not reached); `finish_attempt`, `finish_replay`, `settle`, `sweep` and the attempts in flight are keyed by **slot** (`attempt.endpoint_of`), never by index; `rp_*` hold the slot; `fill_change` and `finish_patch` look the index up by id when they run, and no change can be waiting while a delete is applied (D4). The three places that did **not** survive an endpoint leaving, found by reading and fixed: the id for the history (above); `run` skipped `delivery_turn` when there were no endpoints, so the last endpoint deleted with an attempt on the wire would never have had that attempt settled or timed out (the guard is `count > 0 || draining != 0`); and `take_slot` took a draining slot for a dormant one.

**`lowmark`, the scan and the slowest endpoint.** `lowmark` is the least cursor of the table's endpoints, and `extend_scan` reads the events log up to `lowmark + 1,024`. When an endpoint leaves, `lowmark` can only rise (the endpoint is no longer in the minimum), except that with none left it is 0; neither moves the scan backwards, because `c_scanned` only grows, and the first `POST` after the last endpoint left sets the scan position itself (`count == 0`, as in 25.2). When the endpoint that left was the slowest, the others are no longer held to its window: `extend_scan` reads further, the offsets ring entries it overwrites are those of events at or below the new `lowmark`, which are final for every endpoint that is left (a draining endpoint's attempt on the wire has its request already), and the others are served the events beyond the old window in the next turn. A test measures it (stage 14): the fast endpoint, held to 1,024 events by a slow one that is down, gets events 1,025 to 1,300 once the slow one is deleted, each once.

**A row that was already gone (decision, to confirm).** The statement returns no row when somebody deleted the row with `psql`. The service **removes the endpoint from memory all the same** and answers `200` with `"row":"was already gone"`, and writes the records as for any delete. The alternative, a `404` with memory unchanged as `PATCH` does, leaves the endpoint delivering to a receiver the database says does not exist, with no way to remove it short of a restart, and a retried `DELETE` that fails forever. For a `PATCH` there is nothing to apply, so `404` is right; for a delete the database already says what the caller wants, and the service is the one that is wrong. (After a restart the two are the same: the row is gone, so the endpoint is dormant.) A second `DELETE` is a `404`.

**The id is not given again.** The plan (D1) and 25.2 said so because the id comes from a sequence. It is true for the ids `POST` makes. It was **not** true for a row inserted by hand: `greatest(nextval('endpoint_ids'), max(id) + 1)` is `max(id) + 1` when the sequence is behind, so deleting the highest hand-made row and creating an endpoint gave its id again, and its rows in `attempts` would have collided with the old ones (`on conflict do nothing` drops the new one's). The delete statement moves the sequence past the id it deletes (`setval(..., greatest(nextval(...), id), true)`, which uses the same privileges as `nextval`, and burns a number, so ids are not dense after a delete). A row deleted with `psql` can still have its id given again; that is outside what the service sees.

**What a restart finds, and what I verified.** The row is gone, so the endpoint is not in the table and its slot is dormant (25.1): (1) deleted with nothing on the wire: `removed` is in the log, the slot is free, a restart writes nothing and the endpoint is not back (stage 2); (2) killed with an attempt on the wire (no `removed`): 61 endpoints, slot dormant, the log unchanged, and a `POST` with every slot taken reclaims it with `removed` and then `created`, in that order (stage 12); (3) the same with the row put back by hand before the restart: it is the same endpoint resuming in its slot, and the replays that were dropped stay dropped, because their `replay_dead` records were written first (stage 12b: without them `stats.replays` is 1 after the restart); (4) a row put back after `removed` is a *new* endpoint: not disabled, no replay, starting at the slowest cursor (stage 4); (5) a slot reused by a new endpoint, then a restart, then a row added by hand taking a freed slot: none of them inherits a cursor, a window cell, a disabled bit or a replay (stage 8, with 1,040 events so that the cells the old endpoint left above its cursor would have swallowed events 1,027 and 1,028); (6) a `504`: the row goes later, nothing changed in memory, the next start does not have the endpoint (stage 15).

**What the tests check** (`tests/delete_test.py`, against a real PostgreSQL; the receivers verify the signature themselves; the log is read with the test's own reader; 173 checks, about 50 s): the refusals (a 401 with `WWW-Authenticate`, a prefix of the token, an id with a suffix or a negative one, the method listed in `Allow`) and that each leaves the endpoint, the row and the log as they were; the delete, and no event after it reaching the receiver, the listing, the row, the `removed` record, the history rows kept, a restart; an attempt held for 1.4 s on the wire: delivered and recorded in log and history under its id, `removed` after it and not at the request, a `POST` in the window getting another slot, then the freed one; the same for an attempt that fails (recorded, not tried again), for the only endpoint (the loop keeps settling), for a replay (delivered; failed and then dropped) and for a `410`; replays that wait, dropped with their records in order before `removed`; a trigger that refuses (`503`, nothing changed, the log byte for byte the same, the next delete works); a row already gone; one change at a time in both directions (a slow delete trigger and a slow insert trigger); slot reuse across restarts with the log's story of the slot read back, 1,040 events, a hand-added row taking the freed slot; the history of the old and the new endpoint side by side, and a replay of an event both had; ids; 62 endpoints (`409` at 62, delete and create, 70 times with the live number never above 62, a restart, and one event reaching all 62, each signed with its own key); a kill with an attempt on the wire, with 21 dormant slots beside a draining one; a stream of 400 events during which an endpoint is deleted (nothing reaches it more than a quarter of a second after the answer, the other loses none); the slowest endpoint deleted; and a database that takes 6.5 s. Unit tests (`tests/endpoints_test.ls`): `remove` of the first, middle, last and only entry, the other entries unchanged, the vacated row cleared, the removed key zeroed, an index that is not there refused with nothing changed, and `append` and `compact` after a `remove`.

**Mutants of the new code: 38 run, 35 killed, 3 survive (two equivalent, one that cannot fire).** Killed: the `removed` record written at the request with an attempt on the wire; the slot reusable while `flying > 0` (never draining); the slot freed while attempts are still on the wire (the drain ignoring `flying`); the `removed` record of a drained slot naming another slot; the draining bit never set, and never cleared; a freed slot keeping its id (it then looks dormant, not free); waiting replays not dropped (no record, kept in memory), dropped in the log and not in memory, dropped with the wrong event or the wrong kind, an in-flight replay dropped with the waiting ones; a failed replay of a deleted endpoint kept to be retried; a `410` on the wire disabling a deleted endpoint; the history's id read from the table (the deleted endpoint is -1); the loop not running delivery with no endpoint but a draining one; memory changed although the database refused; a row already gone answered `404` with memory left alone; the sequence not moved past a deleted id; the count not decremented; a draining slot taken as a dormant one (`take_slot`); `POST` not counting draining slots; the auth skipped (twice: the `403` and the `401`), the busy check, the "no database" check, the `404`, the `400`; kind 2 not sent as a delete; the answer always saying `draining: false` and `/stats` always saying 0; and in `endpoints.remove` an index shift that stops one short, nothing shifting, the key not zeroed and the bound off by one (these four by the unit tests first; the first two were run again with only the end-to-end test and are killed there too, by stage 2). **Survivors:** (1) *a freed slot not resetting its window* is **equivalent**: nothing reads the window of a free slot, `finish_create` and `place_new` reset the window of the slot they hand out, and recovery resets it at a `removed` record; the call stays so that memory after a delete is what recovery would build. (2) *A freed slot not clearing its replay entries* is **equivalent** for the same kind of reason: by then every entry of the slot has been dropped (waiting ones at the delete) or has ended (an in-flight one delivers, or fails and is dropped). (3) *The database's answer naming another endpoint being accepted* cannot fire: `returning id` of `where id = $1` is that id (the same defensive check `PATCH` has). **One survived at first and is the reason for a test's shape:** an in-flight replay dropped along with the waiting ones wrote a spurious `replay_dead` before the real outcome, and stage 3d only checked that `replay_delivered` was recorded; it now asserts the exact sequence of the slot's records (`created`, `delivered`, `replay`, `replay_delivered`, `removed`). **Two of the first kills were false**: they were killed by a check of mine that was racy (stage 8 posted five events at once, and the fifth could be attempted before the `410` of the fourth disabled the endpoint, so "the deleted endpoint's receiver saw nothing after the delete" failed on the *unmutated* binary one run in seven); the stage posts the fifth after the `410` now, the full set was kept, and the two mutants whose only failure was that check were run again: one is the survivor (2) above, the other is killed by stage 12. Two more were not counted when first written: one did not build (the compiler's own check that a declared effect is performed: `finish_drains` no longer wrote) and was redone as naming another slot, and one string did not match and was redone.

**Found along the way.**

* **Three places read the table for something that outlives an entry** (above): the id for the history, the guard that skipped delivery with no endpoints, and the dormant test in `take_slot`. None was in a test of the plan; each is now in one.
* **The id was reusable**: the claim in D1 and 25.2 was true of the sequence and not of the ids (above).
* **The delete needs `setval`, which needs `UPDATE` on the sequence**: the same privilege `nextval` already needed; a role that can create endpoints can delete them.
* **`c_disabled` is a bit per slot, up to 62**, and its comment still said "per endpoint id (0 to 15)": corrected. (One survivor of slice 2's review, not of this one.)
* **A slot that is draining makes `POST` a `409` at 62 endpoints for up to the deadline.** That is the price of "the slot is reusable only when `flying[slot] == 0`"; the alternative is to let the new endpoint wait for the slot, which holds a request on something other than the database and is not in D4.

## 26. Where a delivery may go (SSRF)

> **Changed by [section 40](#40-https-endpoints-names-and-the-destination-rule-at-every-attempt-docsproductionmd-p17).** This section is the rule as it was first built, for addresses. Where it says a name is refused, section 40 replaces that: a name is accepted at
> the write as a name, resolved by the service at every attempt, every address of the answer judged by the ranges below, and the connection made to the address that was judged. The ranges, the places the rule applies, the redirects and the
> default are as written here; the paragraphs that said what names could not do are corrected in place.

**The risk.** An endpoint is an address the service will `POST` to from inside the operator's network. Whoever can name an address, through `POST /endpoints` (admin token), a row in the table, or a line in `endpoints.conf`, can make the service call something only it can reach: a database on `10.0.0.5`, the cloud metadata service on `169.254.169.254`, an admin port on `127.0.0.1`. What comes back is not read by the caller, but a `POST` to an internal service is an action, the status is kept in the history, and a `2xx` versus a `4xx` is a probe of what is there.

**The rule (`src/destination.ls`).** Unless `allow-private-hosts` is `1`, the host of an endpoint must be:

1. an **IPv4 literal**: four decimal numbers of 0 to 255, no leading zero, nothing else. A name was refused (until section 40) because the service could not know what it resolves to: `tcp_connect_start` calls `getaddrinfo`, so a check before the call and the connection after it can disagree (a name can change in between: DNS rebinding), and the call blocks the loop (design section 16). It is accepted now as a name (`destination.name_ok`), resolved by the service itself on the poller and judged at every attempt, which closes both gaps (40.5). The spellings a resolver accepts and a person does not expect (`127.1`, `2130706433`, `0x7f.0.0.1`, `0177.0.0.1`, `127.0.0.01`) are refused for the same reason, and IPv6 is refused because the rule is written for IPv4 (what the check does not understand it does not allow; an IPv6 literal could name `::1` or an IPv4-mapped loopback). A connection to an IP literal is made without a lookup, so **there is no gap between what was checked and what is connected to**;
2. **public**: outside 0/8, 10/8, 100.64/10 (carrier-grade NAT), 127/8, 169.254/16 (link-local, which holds the metadata address), 172.16/12, 192.0.0/24, 192.0.2/24, 192.88.99/24, 192.168/16, 198.18/15, 198.51.100/24, 203.0.113/24, 224/4 (multicast) and 240/4 (reserved, which holds the broadcast address).

It applies at **every place an endpoint comes from**, with the same function (`endpoints.parse` for the file and the table, `manage.parse_create` for `POST /endpoints`): a request is a `400` that says why and **stores nothing** (no row, no record), and a file or a table that has a bad host **stops the start** with status 13 and the line (`--import-endpoints 1` refuses it too, and imports nothing). "Refuse, don't downgrade": a row that is not allowed is not skipped, because an endpoint that silently does not receive is a worse failure than a service that does not start.

**Redirects.** A `3xx` is not followed (the service has no code that follows one); only a `2xx` is a delivery, so a redirect is a failed attempt, retried like any other, and the receiver it points to is never called. A test pins it, because an allow-list that a redirect walks around is not one.

**The default is the safe one, and that changed what the demos need.** `allow-private-hosts` is `0`, so a receiver on `127.0.0.1` needs `--allow-private-hosts 1` (the README's demo and every test pass it). An existing deployment whose endpoints are on a private network stops at start with a message that names the setting; that is the intended failure. `GET /config` reports the setting.

**What this does not do.** It is a deny-list of ranges, not an allow-list of the customer's hosts: any public address is allowed, and so is any name that resolves only to one. It does not limit the **port** (a public host's port 22 or 6379 can be named), it does not pin an endpoint to a pre-approved host, and a public address that is itself a proxy to the inside is outside what a service can know. Names were not supported at all while the policy was on, which was a real cost: a customer's endpoint at `hooks.example.com` had to be given as its address, and broke when the address changed. Section 40 built what this paragraph named as the next piece: a resolver of our own, which resolves, checks the answer against the same ranges, and connects to the address it checked.

**Tests.** (As first built; the changes of section 40 are marked CHANGED in the files and listed in 40.8.) `tests/destination_test.ls` (unit): the dotted-quad reader on 4 good and 22 bad texts, each range at both of its edges (25 inside, 25 outside), names never *literal*-allowed, and the file parser's `open` flag. `tests/ssrf_test.py` (end to end, against PostgreSQL): 41 refused hosts each stop an `endpoints.conf` start with status 13 and the line, 23 public hosts that are the edges of every range are accepted and listed, `allow-private-hosts 1` accepts private addresses and names, the same 41 are each a `400` from `POST /endpoints` with nothing stored (the message says "public IPv4"), a public host is a `201`, a table row with a private host stops the start and `--import-endpoints` refuses, and a `302` is retried as a failure and never followed. `tests/config_test.py` reads the setting back from the file and from the flag, refuses `yes`, and checks that a private host in the file with the default stops the start.

**Mutants of the new code: 10 run, 10 killed.** Leading zeros accepted; the 169.254 range moved; 172.16/12 losing 31; 100.64/10 off by one; 224/4 losing 224; names allowed; `POST /endpoints` skipping the rule; the file and the table skipping it; the file's message path judged open; the flag ignored. One (leading zeros accepted) is killed by the unit test only, because every end-to-end case that has a leading zero is also a private address and would be refused anyway; the unit test is where the property is pinned.

## 27. Inbound gateway

*Design only; nothing is built.* [`docs/inbound-gateway.md`](inbound-gateway.md) designs the service in reverse: public URLs per source (Stripe, GitHub, Shopify, Slack, a generic HMAC) that verify the provider's signature, store the exact raw body in `events.seg`, answer `2xx` only after the flush, and forward byte for byte through the existing delivery, retry, idempotency and replay machinery. It also records two statements in this file and in the README that its measurements found false: section 17's claim that a request over the server's input limit is answered by closing the connection rather than with `413` (the pinned `http-server` answers `413`), and the README's "over 65,500 bytes" (the limit is 65,499).

## 28. A bug found by measuring: starts beyond the 64 connections were recorded as failed attempts

**Found** while measuring what one core does (`scripts/bench/run.py`, a load generator and a sink in C): with ten endpoints beside a healthy sink, 3 of 5 runs of 50,000 deliveries had failed attempts (13, 39 and 79), although the sink answered every request it received (its count was exactly 50,000). One endpoint never had any (it cannot have more than 8 in flight, which is under 64). A first guess, that the load generator exhausted the ephemeral ports, was wrong: 25,000 deliveries, under the range, failed too (31 in two runs of three).

**Cause (read from `start_attempts`, `start_one`, `attempt.begin`).** A turn starts at most `most_starts()` (16) attempts, and an endpoint has at most `per_endpoint()` (8) in flight, but **nothing limited the service's connections**: `attempt.slots()` is 64 and ten endpoints can want 80. A start beyond the 64th reached `attempt.begin`, which answers "no connection" when `held >= slots()`; `start_one` then recorded that as an outcome like any other: **a failed attempt**. It uses a step of the retry schedule for a receiver that was never called. With the default schedule the next try is 5 s later, then 5 min, 30 min, ... and after the last one the event is a dead letter for that endpoint. So a burst over nine or more endpoints could delay deliveries by seconds to minutes and, repeated, dead-letter an event nobody ever refused. The README said "up to 64 delivery attempts are in flight at once (8 per endpoint)", which read as a queue; it was a cliff.

**Measured, before and after** (`tests/saturation_test.py`: ten endpoints share one healthy receiver that holds each request for 60 to 400 ms, 60 events, 600 deliveries):

| | attempts | delivered | failed |
|---|---|---|---|
| before the fix (30 events, 300 deliveries; the first version of the test) | 536 | 175 in a minute | 361 |
| after the fix (600 deliveries) | 600 | 600 | 0 |

and the benchmark's ten-endpoint case, three runs after the fix: 50,000 attempts, 50,000 delivered, 0 failed each time.

**The fix.** A turn starts no more attempts than there are free connections: `state.starts_allowed(held, slots, most)`, a pure function (`min(most, slots - held)`, never negative), used by `start_attempts`; the replays that follow in the same turn share what is left of the budget. `attempt.begin`'s check stays as the last defence and should no longer be reachable.

**What the tests do and do not pin.** The end-to-end test fails on the old binary and passes on the new, and it checks that the receiver really saw about 64 at once (otherwise it proves nothing). It does **not** kill a cap that is off by one to three **inside the band where 49 to 51 connections are in use at the start of a turn**: two such mutants of the inline version survived five runs each, with random hold times, because that state is rare. So the rule was extracted into `state.starts_allowed` and **unit-tested at its boundaries** (0, 47, 48, 49, 50, 51, 63, 64 and 70 connections in use): six mutants of it (off by +1, off by -1, no clamp at zero, applied too late, applied one late, no cap at all) are all killed.

**What it does not fix.** Events that earlier versions failed spuriously were retried by the schedule and are not "lost", but the delay was real and nothing recorded why: a failed attempt carries no reason in the log (the history table has the status), so a spurious failure cannot be told from a refusal after the fact. Recording the cause of a failed attempt is a separate change and is listed in the README's limits.

## 29. Correction: one unreachable endpoint stops every endpoint after 1,024 events

**What was claimed.** The README and the product page said a slow, silent or unreachable endpoint "costs the others almost nothing" (sections 14 to 16, `tests/isolation_test.py`). **That is true for latency and false for progress**, and the second matters more.

**Measured** (the binary of section 28; `python3 scripts/bench/stall_probe.py` reproduces it: two endpoints, one dead, 3,000 events posted): the healthy endpoint's cursor stopped at **1,024**; delivered 1,024 of 3,000; the dead endpoint's cursor stayed at 0 and its attempts went through the retry schedule. The healthy endpoint received nothing more for as long as the dead one stayed at 0, which with the default schedule is hours to a day.

**Cause (read from `extend_scan`, `lowmark`).** The events log is scanned forward only as far as the **slowest cursor plus the window** (`lowmark(dv) + state.span()`, 1,024 events), because one ring of offsets (`offs`, indexed by `id % span`) is shared by all endpoints. An event beyond that is not scanned, so no endpoint can start an attempt for it, however healthy that endpoint is. Section 25.2 measured the same mechanism for a row added by hand and section 25.3 fixed *that entry point* (a new endpoint starts at the slowest cursor); it did not touch the general case, which is any endpoint that is down.

**Why the tests did not see it.** The isolation test posts 80 events (about 20 a second) and a dead endpoint costs the others nothing within the first 1,024. The chaos and delivery tests use schedules of 100 to 250 ms, so a dead endpoint dead-letters its events in seconds and its cursor moves on. Only a long schedule and more than 1,024 events show it, which is the production case.

**Consequence.** The service is not production-ready: **one customer's broken receiver halts delivery to every other customer** once about a thousand events have accumulated. The fix is the first item of `docs/production.md`.

*Closed by section 31: what is measured above is the binary of section 28. Nothing in this section is true of the service any more except the measurement.*

## 30. Signing the largest events trapped; HMAC moves to lex-sys's `std.hmac`

**Found while building lex-sys's `std.hmac` (lex-sys `docs/hkdf.md` §2, PR #229).** `src/sign.ls`'s `hmac_sha256` put `ipad || id.timestamp.payload` in a heap box, because an arena is 64 KiB, and then handed it to `crypto.sha256`. That function built its padded copy of the whole message in an arena. Ingest accepts an event of up to 65,498 bytes (section 27), so **any accepted event of 65,446 bytes or more (65,451 with a one-digit id) trapped the service when its delivery was signed** (`SIGILL`, status -4 from `build/sign_probe`, with the compiler pinned before this change; the reference library's signature from the new one). The event is already in the log by then, so it was very likely a crash on every restart as well, though that was not run end to end.

**The fix is lex-sys's**, not this repository's. Its SHA-2 now streams over a small state and copies nothing the size of the message, so moving the compiler pin is what removes the trap. **The change here:** `sign.hmac_sha256` is deleted, `signature` calls `hmac.sha256` from `std.hmac`, and the compiler pin moves to a lex-sys commit that has it. This moves the one HMAC implementation out of this repository, as lex-sys issue #201 asks. `tests/sign_test.py` now signs payloads of 65,400 to 70,000 bytes against the reference library, through the range that trapped.

**Cost:** lex-sys measures its `hmac.sha256` at 2.6 µs on 64 bytes, 9.9 µs on 1 KiB and 120 µs on 16 KiB, against 21.8, 32.7 and 184 µs for this file's old HMAC over the old SHA-256 (lex-sys `docs/hkdf.md` §6).

## 31. A scan position per endpoint, and a circuit breaker (`docs/production.md` 0.1; closes section 29)

*Written with the code, like sections 20 and 22: the rule was taken from production.md, the gates were written with the tests and not before them, and the mutants were run once the tests passed.*

**What was wrong.** One ring of event offsets (`offs`, 1,024 entries) and one scan position (`c_scanned`, `c_offset`) served every endpoint, so the events log was read forward only as far as `lowmark(dv) + span()`: 1,024 events past the **slowest** cursor. An endpoint that stayed at 0 held every other endpoint to events 1 to 1,024 (section 29, `scripts/bench/stall_probe.py`: cursors `(0, 1024), (1, 0)` of 3,000 events).

**What it is now.**

* **A scan position per slot.** `scan_id(e)` is the last event of the events log that endpoint has looked at and `scan_off(e)` the offset where the record after it starts; `offs_at(e, id)` is a ring of 1,024 offsets *for that slot*, like its cells. Endpoint `e` considers the events `cur[e] + 1` to `cur[e] + 1,024` and no others: **its own window is the only bound**, and the events beyond it wait in the log, on disk, not in memory.
* **A fresh event is read once, by the endpoint that starts its first attempt.** `start_attempts` walks an endpoint's window from its cursor as before; when it reaches an id above `scan_id(e)` it reads the log at `scan_off(e)` (`scan_next`), notes where the event starts in the endpoint's ring, moves the position past it and starts the attempt on the record it has just read (`start_one(..., loaded)`). The shared scan read every event once and every attempt read it again; now the first attempt's read is the scan's. A retry reads the log at the ring's offset, as it did. An event an endpoint has not reached is not read at all, and an endpoint that has caught up asks the log for nothing (`read_at` answers "not durable yet" without a system call).
* **Where each endpoint stands at start** (`seek_slots`, after the replay and `place_new`): at the first record above its own cursor, found for all endpoints in one pass over the log that ends at the record after the largest cursor. A cursor at or past the last record stands at the end. `scan_next` passes over records below the id it wants, so a position that is too early costs a read each and is never wrong; the mutant that puts it one record *too late* is the one that loses an event (below). A **new endpoint made by `POST /endpoints`** stands at `(last event, end of the log)` with no read at all (it used to need `seek_after`, a read of the whole log from the start, for the first endpoint).
* **`lowmark` is gone as a bound.** The function is `slowest_cursor` and decides only where an endpoint the log has never seen starts (section 25.3: the slowest cursor). The reason for that rule (the newcomer could not widen the one window everybody was held to) is gone with the window; the rule stays because it is what a person was told and the tests pin it: a new row is sent the backlog of the slowest endpoint, not the whole log.
* **Order.** An endpoint's events are first-attempted in id order, so within its window they are sent in order, retries aside (section 4). A dead endpoint that is revived later is sent its events 1 to 1,024 by their retries as they fall due and 1,025 and above as its window moves up, **never an event more than 1,024 past what it has been sent in order**; `tests/scan_test.py` checks that on the order the receiver saw.
* **Memory.** The offsets are 62 x 1,024 integers instead of 1,024, plus two integers and one more (the run of failures, below) a slot: **62,650 integers more, 501,200 bytes**, on a delivery state of 258,541 integers, 2,068,328 bytes (the cells are 190,464 integers and the in-flight flags 63,488). It is one heap array laid out as a chain of computed offsets, so nothing overlaps by construction (`tests/layout_test.py` still passes); the 64 KiB regions are not involved (the state is on the heap).
