**Where it is recorded, and why in both places.** Production.md allowed a new `delivery.seg` kind or the history column. Both, because they answer different questions:

* **A record of kind 14 in `delivery.seg`** (`state.reason()`): the endpoint, the event, the attempt's number, and the reason in the fifth field (plus 256 for a replay's attempt), written *right after* the `failed`, `dead`, `replay_failed` or `replay_dead` record it explains and in the same flush. The 40-byte outcome record is unchanged (kind 14 is a sixth value of its first field, as 12 and 13 were). The log is the truth about delivery and is there without a database: it is what survives a restart (the last reason of each endpoint is read back at start, `hooks_endpoint_last_failure`), what `scripts/logcheck.py` counts by reason, and what a post-mortem has when the history has holes. A crash between the two records loses the reason and keeps the outcome, never the reverse.
* **The column `attempts.reason`** (`smallint not null default 0`, `sql/schema.sql`, the queries regenerated by `pgen`): `GET /events/:id/attempts` answers `"reason":"connect_refused"` and the like (`none` for a delivery, `unrecorded` for a failed row written before the column). The `status` column keeps its coarse values (`reason.legacy_status` maps every new code to the -1, -2, -3 or -4 it would have been), so `history_test.py` passes as it was.

**Compatibility.** A `delivery.seg` with a kind-14 record is refused by the previous version (status 15): a rollback needs the backup of 5. The database needs `psql -f sql/schema.sql` first; a binary that has the column in its queries and a table that lacks it is a refusal (status 20), as for every earlier column.

### 34.4 Stopping

`SIGTERM` or `SIGINT`: stop accepting, stop starting attempts, let the attempts on the wire finish for at most `stop-deadline-ms` (5000), flush, close the logs, exit 0. A second signal ends the process at once.

**How a lex-sys program learns of a signal.** *(Section 36 replaced all of this paragraph's mechanism with lex-sys's signals capability; what follows is what 34 built and measured, kept because the decision and its cost are the record.)* It had no builtin for it (searched: `std/`, `docs/`, the builtin table; `docs/server.md` has the one signal use there is, `signal(SIGPIPE, SIG_IGN)` through `Ffi`). `Ffi("libc")` reaches libc, and a handler is out: a callback must have an empty effect row and scalar parameters (`function-values.md`), so it could not write to a pipe or set a flag the loop reads. So the program installs no handler: at the start of the loop it **blocks** both signals (`sigblock`), and once a turn asks which are pending (`sigpending`: a system call, 50 ms of latency at most because the wait is 50 ms). When it sees one it sets `SIG_IGN`, then `SIG_DFL`, on both (ignoring a pending signal throws it away), and unblocks them (`sigsetmask(0)`): from that instant the next signal kills the process by its default action. That is the whole of "a second signal exits at once", and it needs no code to run in the dying process. `sigblock` takes an integer mask; the POSIX `sigprocmask` takes two pointers, and a lex-sys slice crosses as a pointer *and* a length, which would put the length where the second pointer goes (`strings.md` section 6), so the old BSD call it is. Four libc functions, which with `statx` of the production profile (section 33.5) are the only foreign authority the service holds. **This changes the authority report, and the owner should weigh it:** `lex-sys authority` opens with `UNBOUNDED: this program calls foreign code, and a library is not an authority domain` (`docs/under-a-grant.md` in lex-sys), lists `ffi("libc")` among the labels and `sigblock`, `signal`, `sigpending`, `sigsetmask` (and `statx`) as foreign symbols; without the signal functions the service would release its `Ffi` at once and the report would have neither, unless `statx` is there. The signal calls are confined to `src/ops.ls` and the calls in `run`; without them the service cannot learn of a signal, so cannot drain. They are in systemd's `@system-service` (`rt_sigaction`, `rt_sigpending`, `rt_sigprocmask`, `unlink`; checked with `systemd-analyze syscall-filter`, and `strace` on a run shows exactly those); `systemd-analyze verify` and `security --offline` on the unit (exposure 1.2). A signal that arrives before the loop starts (the logs are being read) has its default action and ends the process at once, which is safe.

**The drain.** At the first signal the loop prints `hooks: stopping on SIGTERM: ...`, sets the stop flag and a deadline, and from then on: a request that writes and has passed the credential check (section 33.1) is answered `503 {"error":"the service is stopping"}` and the connection closes (the check is the method, not a list of routes, so a route added later is covered); `start_attempts` starts nothing (new, retry or replay); the cron tick starts no cycle; `/readyz` is 503 `stopping`, `/metrics` says `hooks_stopping 1`; reads still answer, so a drain can be watched. The loop ends at the end of a turn in which no attempt is on the wire and the history has nothing queued or in flight (a database that is not live counts as nothing), or when the deadline has passed. Then it flushes both logs again, removes the probe files, says `hooks: stopped: ...`, and `main` closes the logs and returns 0. Attempts the deadline cut off are not recorded and are made again at the next start (at least once, as after a crash); the message counts them.

**The listening socket stays open until the process exits.** This is the one thing of the item that is not as asked ("stop accepting new connections"): a connection that arrives during the drain is accepted by the server library and answered `503` with `Connection: close`. Closing the socket needs the `Listener` handle, which `main` lends to `run` for the whole loop and the server library takes by reference at every `wait`; `listener_close` consumes it. Handing `run` the listener, creating the server before `run`, and swapping in another listener for `wait` after the close compiles (a spike did it), but it moves the whole body of `run` one indentation level and every other change to the loop with it; and the server library offers no way to stop accepting while serving the connections it has. See 34.9.

### 34.5 Refusing corruption (production 0.5)

**Nothing is changed until everything is judged.** `logguard.preflight` opens `events.seg` and `delivery.seg` read-only, inspects each with `lexsys-log`'s own scanner (`segment.scan`, so the same length, CRC and increasing-id rules; a test compares it with a separate reader), and judges the pair against what each log would hold *after* the cut it would be given. Only then does `main` open the logs for appending, and `logguard.recover_known` apply the cut that was judged (the three steps of `log.recover`: truncate, sync; the log is not read again). So a refusal, whichever it is, leaves the directory exactly as it was (the tests compare every byte of both files and the file list), and `--repair-logs` cannot cut a log and then refuse the start. `lexsys-log` is unchanged and no patch to it is needed; `docs/lexsys-log-recover.patch` does not exist. (What would make the duplication unnecessary is a `recover` that takes the policy; see 34.9.)

**(a) The pair, status 18.** The largest event id `delivery.seg` refers to (`event` of a delivered, failed, dead, replay or reason record; the starting cursor of a `created` record) must not exceed the last id of `events.seg`. If it does, the service refuses, saying both numbers and what to do. It is judged after the cuts: an `events.seg` that `--repair-logs` would shorten below what `delivery.seg` knows is refused (18) and **nothing is cut**; repairing events whose deliveries are recorded means moving `delivery.seg` aside, and every event that is left is then delivered again. `--repair-logs` never lifts 18.

**(b) Damage in the middle, status 19.** After the last whole record the scan stops; what follows is **a torn tail** or **damage**:

* damage if **a record that validates starts anywhere after the first bad place** (every offset is a candidate, `find_valid`), or if the bytes there **parse as two or more records of plausible length one after the other** (`chain`; the checksum is not asked: a flipped byte in a record leaves its length good);
* otherwise a torn tail, of any size: an unfinished record, a few bytes of a header, a page of zeros, garbage, a spoiled last record, a spoiled record and a part of the next.

The reason for the rule: what a crash can leave in the unsynced tail is a prefix of the truth with at most garbage after it, and **nothing that validates after garbage**; what bit rot or a bad restore leaves in the middle has intact records after it, and cutting there throws away records that are whole. Using the number of bytes (the first version of `logcheck.py` allowed one record's length) is wrong both ways: the shim of `tests/fsync_shim.c` zeroes up to 64 bytes at the end of the file, which can spoil a whole record and part of the one before it. A torn tail is cut as before, and **said, after `listening`** (the tests read the first line of stderr and wait for `listening`): `hooks: events.seg: cut a torn tail of N bytes at byte X (an unfinished write); M records are whole`.

Damage is refused (19) with the log named, where it is whole to (`whole for K records (up to byte X)`), how many bytes cutting would take, how many intact records start among them (and where), and how to go on. `--repair-logs 1` cuts at X **after copying the bytes after X to `<log>.cut-X`** (written, synced, closed; if that file exists, or cannot be written, nothing is cut and the start is refused again), says `hooks: --repair-logs: events.seg: cut at byte X: N bytes gone, among them M intact records; K records are whole. The bytes are kept in events.seg.cut-X`, and goes on. It is a setting (`repair-logs`, in the file or on the command line); `GET /config` shows it, and the sample file says to give it once on the command line.

**One rule, two implementations.** `scripts/logcheck.py` has the same rule (`classify_tail`), and `tests/corrupt_test.py` stage E gives 153 corruptions (150 random, 3 directed) of a small log (flips, truncations, zero runs, garbage, holes, duplicated records, a flipped byte near the end) to `logcheck.classify_tail` and then to the service, which must refuse exactly the ones the reference calls damage and cut to exactly the same byte the others.

**The known false positive.** A crash that persists a later page of the unsynced tail and not an earlier one leaves intact records after a hole. They are unacknowledged (nothing after a flush's boundary was), the rule cannot tell, and the start is refused (19): an operator step (`--repair-logs 1`) and no loss. The opposite mistake cost 250 events. None of the power-cut tests produced it (the shim only truncates and zeroes the end): `chaos.py` (105 kills as power cuts), `delivery.py` (101), `scan_test` (12), `backup_test` stage B (13), `schedules_test` (6 rounds and 35 more) all restart after cuts, and none was refused (the one run of the whole suite on the final code).

**Cost.** `segment.scan` is `log.recover`'s scan, done once, so `events.seg` is read as many times as before; `delivery.seg` is read once more, for the pair (`last_reference`). A start on a 241 MB `events.seg` of a million events took 4.8 to 5.2 s against 4.7 to 5.5 s before (three runs each, `listening` as the end), and on 200,000 events with a 15 MB `delivery.seg` 3.0 to 3.4 s against 3.3 to 3.7 s.

### 34.6 Tests

Every harness is new except `backup_test.py`, `delete_test.py`, `config_test.*` and `state_test.ls`, which changed (below). The helper `tests/opslib.py` has a service whose stderr is kept, receivers that fail in a chosen way on raw sockets (`ok`, a status, `silent`, `slow`, `reset` with an `RST`, `close`, `garbage`), an independent reader of both logs, and the strict parser of the Prometheus text. **No test waits for the clock to say something happened**: each step is taken when the service or a receiver has been seen to do what the step needs (a receiver has the request, a line is on stderr), and the only durations checked are the ones the contract is about (how long a drain may take, that it waits for its deadline).

| file | checks | what |
|---|---|---|
| `tests/ops_test.ls`, `reason_test.ls` (unit) | 13 then (12 now), 6 | the counters, the readiness order, the probe's schedule, the stop deadline, the text of `/metrics` (a family once, a series per reason, a row per endpoint, 62 endpoints under 40 KB); every code an attempt can end with has its reason, the numbers are pinned (they are on disk), the legacy status |
| `tests/metrics_test.py` | 60 | stage 1, a known workload (43 events one at a time, three with keys and repeated, five refused, three endpoints: ok, 500, nothing listening): every number against `/stats`, the logs read with another reader, and the arithmetic; stage 2 lag, retries, and the last failure across a restart; stage 3 a `410` and the breaker; stage 4 62 endpoints; stage 5 the history against the rows of the table (with a database) |
| `tests/reason_test.py` | 36 | ten receivers, one event: the reason in `/attempts`, `/metrics`, the table's column and the log (a record of kind 14 right after its outcome), the coarse status kept, a replay's reason (+256), the last failure after a stop and a start, and ended by a delivery |
| `tests/ready_test.py` | 28 | `/readyz` ready; a proxy cuts the database (503 `database`, delivery unaffected, no reconnect: `INFO`, 200 after a restart); a 256 KiB tmpfs fills through the service (503 `events_log`, `/healthz` still 200, a restart finds every event) and is filled by another process (503 `data_dir`, 200 again with no restart) |
| `tests/stop_test.py` | 54 | SIGTERM and SIGINT idle; with 8 attempts on the wire and 192 events waiting (503 on writes, `/readyz`, `/metrics`, `/healthz`, no ninth request ever, the 8 recorded, exit 0, the next start delivers the rest and repeats none); the deadline (exits 0 after about it, leaving the attempts, which are repeated) and a deadline of 0; a second signal in three combinations (killed by that signal); under load (four clients posting, a slow receiver, SIGTERM: exit 0 in under 5.5 s, every acknowledged event in the log, every event reached each receiver **exactly once** across the stop and the next start) |
| `tests/corrupt_test.py` | 113 | A the pair (an older `events.seg`, a missing one, a `created` cursor beyond, a reason record beyond: status 18, files untouched; exactly the last event starts; `--repair-logs` does not lift it); B seven kinds of damage (status 19, the numbers in the message, files untouched); C eight torn tails (cut, said after `listening`, the next id continues, the next start silent); D `--repair-logs` (the cut kept byte for byte in `<log>.cut-X`, the report, a refusal when the file exists, `delivery.seg`, a clean log, the settings file; and a repair that would make the pair disagree is refused 18 with nothing cut); E 153 corruptions (150 random, 3 directed) against `logcheck.classify_tail` |

*Existing tests that had to change, and why.* `delete_test.py` stages 3e and 3f read the sequence of kinds in `delivery.seg` and now expect the reason record between `replay_failed` and `replay_dead`, and after `dead`. `state_test.ls` used 14 as "one past the last kind"; it is 15 now. `config_test.*` name two more settings in `GET /config`. `backup_test.py` stage C item 9 was *information* (what the service does with the refused pair: "NEVER DELIVERED"); it is a check now, for the pair (18) and for the flipped byte (19), given to the service itself. `scripts/logcheck.py` knows kind 14 (without it `backup_test` stage A failed: "226 records are not outcomes this version writes", which is also what the previous version would say of a log of this one).

### 34.7 Mutants

39 mutants of the new code, one at a time on a saved copy of the file (built, the test that should notice run, the file restored from the copy and compared; `mutate.py` is not kept in the repository, the list is here): **38 killed, 1 equivalent.**

| area | mutants (killed by) |
|---|---|
| reasons | a refused port classed as `connect_error`; a deadline while connecting classed as `no_response`; a reset as `closed_early`; a bad status line as `closed_early`; `410` as `status_4xx` (unit test); the history `status` of a refused port not the coarse -1; the reason record never written; a replay's reason without the +256; recovery ignoring the reason record; the history row without a reason (`reason_test`, `metrics_test`) |
| metrics | every refusal counted under 400; a key repeat counted as accepted; group commits of the events log not looked at; an endpoint's lag taken as its cursor; retries waiting counting events never attempted (`metrics_test`); a stopped service not saying `stopping` (`stop_test`) |
| readiness | ignoring the database; ignoring a broken events log; a probe that always passes (`ready_test`) |
| stopping | a write taken while stopping; attempts started while stopping; the second signal not ending the process; a drain that never gives up at the deadline; one that exits at once; SIGINT not looked for; SIGINT not held back (`stop_test`) |
| the pair and damage | a pair that refers to exactly the last event refused (`>=` for `>`); a `created` record's cursor, and a reason record, not counted as references; no search for intact records after the damage; no look at records in a row; damage in `delivery.seg` not looked for; the cut bytes not kept; the cut one byte too late; a torn tail cut without a word (`corrupt_test`) |
| the setting and the reference | `stop-deadline-ms` defaulting to 0 (`config_test.py`); `scripts/logcheck.py` without kind 14 (`backup_test`); `logcheck.py` ignoring records in a row (`corrupt_test` stage E) |

**Equivalent:** `recover_known` cutting damage without `--repair-logs`: `preflight` has already refused it, so the guard in `recover_known` is defence in depth that no input reaches.

Two things the first round taught. The mutant for logcheck's "records in a row" **survived** the random corpus (a random corruption rarely spoils several whole records at the end with nothing valid after them): stage E now begins with three directed cases (the last 2, 3 and 5 records spoiled), and the mutant is killed. And two mutants did not build because the compiler holds a function's effect row to what its body does (`note_reason` declaring `file_write` but never writing: "a row is exact or it is decoration"); they were rewritten to stay inside the row.

### 34.8 Measured

*Hot path* (`scripts/bench/run.py`, three runs each, base = the commit before this one, interleaved, a machine shared with other work, medians): CPU a delivery with 1 endpoint 85 us base, 91 us here (ranges 75 to 86 and 74 to 92); 10 endpoints 70 and 73 us; CPU an event on ingest 11 and 12 us; requests a second on ingest 40,793 and 40,484. The extra work is a `sigpending` call, a clock read and two comparisons a turn, and three adds a delivery. **Within noise, with a bias of a few percent that this machine cannot resolve.**

*Start time* (to `listening`, three runs each, base first): a 241 MB `events.seg` of a million events 4.7 to 5.5 s and 4.8 to 5.2 s; 200,000 events with a 15 MB `delivery.seg` 3.3 to 3.7 s and 3.0 to 3.4 s. The scan that judges the logs is the scan `log.recover` did.

*The suite, combined with section 33*: every step of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (34 test steps, including the formatting check, shellcheck, and `FULL=1` for the idempotency and schedules tests) on one build of the two features together, against a PostgreSQL of this machine's own: all green, after the one change in `tests/authz_test.py` that 33.8 names (236 checks there, 21 routes, 525 requests in the matrix, 746 answers searched for a token). *The suite before they were combined*: every step of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` as it was before the credentials of section 33 were combined with this one, against a PostgreSQL of this machine's own, in order, on the final code: all green (the steps in the list: `fmt`, the unit tests, `cron`, `sign`, `config`, `history`, `roster`, `layout`, `manage`, `slots`, `patch`, `delete`, `saturation`, `ssrf`, `replay`, `gone`, `attempt`, `retry`, `isolation`, `scan`, `breaker`, `chaos` (105 kills as power cuts), `backup` (13 kills, 12 online backups), `shellcheck`, `delivery` (101 kills), `idempotency` and `schedules` with `FULL=1`, and the five new ones).

### 34.10 Not verified

The stop under a real systemd or in the container (the image was not rebuilt; `systemd-analyze verify` complains only that `/opt/hooks/bin/hooks` is not there, `security --offline` says 1.2, the syscalls were read with `strace` and `systemd-analyze syscall-filter`). Anything on macOS (the signal functions and the bit positions are the same by the headers, not run). `connect_timeout` needs a host where `10.255.255.1` swallows the SYN (this one does; the test checks and skips otherwise), and `send_timeout` has no test (34.3). The tmpfs part of `ready_test.py` needs root or passwordless `sudo` and is skipped without. The false positive of 34.5 is argued, not produced. The listening socket stays open in the drain (34.4). A reconnect to the database does not exist (34.9). The process was not measured over days.

### 34.9 What the compiler and the libraries lacked

* **The LLVM backend (the default since #127) cannot compile a comparison with the result of `fs_write`.** Found writing the directory probe; the Cranelift backend builds the same file. Minimal reproducer (`lex-sys build repro.ls --std`; add `--backend cranelift` and it builds):

  ```
  edition 5;
  import std.io;
  fn wrote[&f](fs: &f Fs("")) -> [fs_write("")] bool {
      return fs_write(fs, "/tmp/x", "1") == 1;
  }
  fn main(world: World) -> [] int {
      let Split { io, ffi, fs, heap, args, net, clock } = split(world);
      var ok = 1;
      borrow fs as &f in { if wrote(f) { ok = 0; } }
      release(ffi); release(fs); release(net); release(clock); release(args); release(io); release(heap);
      return ok;
  }
  // error: the compiler failed to generate code for `wrote`; this is a bug in lex-sys, not in the program
  //        (cannot determine the scalar kind of `FileOp { write: true, prefix: "", args: [Load(Slot(0)), Bytes("/tmp/x"), Bytes("1")] }` here)
  ```

  The workaround (`ops.ls`, `probe_write`) is to bind the result first: `let wrote = fs_write(..); return wrote == 1;`.
* **No builtin for signals.** *(Fixed in lex-sys by #244, adopted in section 36; this item is the record of the gap.)* `Ffi("libc")` and four functions did it (34.4). A `std.signal` that says "SIGTERM or SIGINT is pending" and consumes it, behind a capability of its own, would remove four of the five foreign symbols the service holds (the fifth is `statx`, section 33.5: with a file-mode builtin too the `Ffi`, and `UNBOUNDED` in the authority report, would go) and the BSD `sigblock` (the POSIX call takes two pointers, and a slice crosses as a pointer and a length: `strings.md` section 6). On macOS the mask bits and `sigpending`'s buffer are the same, but **nothing here was run on macOS**.
* **A `Listener` cannot be closed from inside the loop that serves on it.** `server.wait` takes `&!Listener` on every turn, `listener_close` consumes it, and the `http.server` package has no "stop accepting" (a `server.stop_accepting(srv)` that closes or shuts down the listener it was opened on, or a `server.open` that takes the listener by value, would do). Without it the drain leaves the socket open (34.4).
* **The history pool cannot reopen a connection without blocking the loop.** *(Closed: `lexsys-pg` PR #12, adopted in section 37.)* `pg.login` is a blocking exchange (SCRAM is several round trips) on a `Conn` with no timeout, and `tcp_connect` blocks until the kernel gives up (minutes, for an address that does not answer); the only non-blocking dial is `tcp_connect_start`. So a running service cannot reconnect without a stall that would stop ingest and delivery, which is why a lost database connection is not reopened (it was documented as a limit before this section) and why `/readyz` says `database` until a restart. A `pg.pool.connect_start` that logs in under the poller would fix it. The test records the behaviour (`INFO 2.`), not as a check, so that implementing the reconnect does not need a test to be changed.
* **`lexsys-log` has no recovery policy.** `log.recover` cuts whatever follows the last whole record. A `recover(rw, window, max_len, policy)` that returns the verdict and cuts only what the caller allows, or a `log.inspect` that does not cut, would remove the 30 lines of `logguard.recover_known` that repeat `recover`'s truncate-and-sync. No patch was needed to do the work; `segment.scan` and `record.check` are public, which is what this relies on.
* **A `&static [byte]` cannot be compared with `==`** (`int`, `byte`, `bool`, `float` and `c_ptr` can): the tests that name a reason compare with a loop. Known (`strings.md`); it cost a rewritten assertion.
## 35. Event types, secret rotation with two signatures, custom headers (`docs/production.md` P1.1, P1.3, P1.5)

*Written with the code, like sections 20, 22 and 31: the rules were taken from production.md and `docs/inbound-gateway.md` (D10, D11), the gates were written with the tests, and the mutants were run once the tests passed.*

### 35.1 What was decided

| | decision |
|---|---|
| **What an event's type is** | the string `"type"` of its JSON body. `POST /events` already requires one and already parses the body once to check it (`invalid_event`), so no header convention is needed and no second parse is paid: the type is taken from that parse, decoded (`"escA"` is `escA`), and stored. A type that is empty, over 128 bytes or has a control character is stored as *no type*. |
| **Where it is stored** | a named pair `typ` in the event's record, right after `event`: `event`, `typ`, then `key` and `t` for a keyed event. D11's mechanism, without D11's source and header pairs (no inbound gateway yet). The reader finds pairs **by name, never by count** (`rebuild`, `filter.type_in`). |
| **Old logs** | a record of one pair (`event`) or three (`event`, `key`, `t`) is read as it always was and has no type; it goes only to an endpoint with no list or one that lists `*`. Nothing is rewritten. |
| **The other way** | a log with a `typ` pair is refused by the previous binary at start (`events.seg` status 16, nothing lost): one-way, like the slot records (25.1). `tests/idempotency_test.py` pinned the record's shape (`event, key, t`; `event` alone) and now pins the new one: **the only existing assertions changed besides the whole-object reads**. |
| **Patterns** | exact, `prefix.*` (the types that begin `prefix.`), `*` (everything, an untyped event too); at most 16, 128 bytes each, 512 in all; visible ASCII, no space, no comma; a `*` anywhere else is refused. D11 said "no patterns in the first version"; the trailing `.*` is the one the task asked for and costs a compare of the prefix. |
| **Where the decision is made** | in `start_attempts`, at the one place an endpoint first looks at an event: right after `scan_next` has read the record into the window. If the endpoint has a list and the record's type is not wanted, `state.apply(..., delivered, id, 0, 0)` makes the cell final **in memory** and the cursor slides over it. No record is written (D10 chose the same, with a sparse `advance` record as an optimisation that was not built: see 35.3), no attempt, no history row, no budget spent. The check is `len(list) == 0` for an endpoint with no list and a compare of at most 128 bytes against at most 16 patterns otherwise: the record is not parsed per endpoint. |
| **Rotation** | `endpoints.secret_old` and `secret_old_until` (Unix ms). `PATCH` with a new secret (or `rotate`) and `keep_old_ms` (0 to 30 days) or `keep_old: true` (the setting `rotation-grace-ms`, default 24 h) sets them in the same statement that sets the new secret, so the database never has one without the other. `keep_old_ms` alone moves or ends the period. A new secret without a period clears them. While `now < until` every attempt signs twice, `v1,<new> v1,<old>`. |
| **Headers** | `endpoints.headers`: `Name:value` pairs, a value percent-encoded; at most 8, 2,048 bytes as sent; the delivery's own headers and the connection's can never be set; CR, LF and NUL never. Values are never returned. |

### 35.2 The record, and why not a header

D11 listed a header as the cheap place to read a type *for the providers that put one there*; an event posted to `/events` has no such header, and a client that sets one would have to say the same thing twice (the body's `type` is mandatory). The header would also be a second source of truth that can disagree with the body. The body's `type` is the only one, and it is read in the one pass that already existed.

A record is now `28 + (13 + body) + (11 + type) [+ key and time]`: **a typed event is 11 bytes and the type's length smaller than the largest untyped one**, so the largest accepted body is 65,499 minus those (65,487 with a one-character type), `413` beyond. The README says so.

### 35.3 Where an unwanted event goes, and what a restart does

The cursor of an endpoint is the largest id such that every event up to it is final. An unwanted event is final at once, so it can only wait for an earlier event that is not (a retrying one): it holds a cell of the window meanwhile and nothing else. A dead endpoint subscribed to a rare type passes over the thousands of events it does not want with no attempt, stops at the rare one it cannot deliver, and passes over 1,023 more behind it (its window) while that one retries (`tests/filter_test.py` 3).

**No record, so a restart decides again.** The skip is not written. After a restart an endpoint reads the events log from the first record above its recorded cursor (`seek_slots`) and decides each again under the list **then in force**. Three consequences, all tested (stage 9): an event that has a trace in the delivery log above the cursor (an attempt made, or delivered) is **never** passed over by the second look (`unwanted` leaves it alone), so a `PATCH` that narrows a list does not lose a retry or repeat a delivery; an event that was passed over before the restart and is not yet behind the cursor is decided again, and sent if the list has grown meanwhile (a widening reaches it after a restart and not before: said in the README); the cost of a restart is a read per event between the cursor and the end of the log for an endpoint whose events are mostly unwanted. D10's sparse `advance` record would bound that, and it is not built because a new kind of record in `delivery.seg` makes an older binary refuse the log (status 15) and the retention work (production 0.2) is about to decide what that log's snapshot contains; the cost is a re-walk of at most the unrecorded run, measured in 35.8.

A disabled endpoint (a `410`, the breaker) is not scanned, so it passes over nothing until it is enabled. A turn passes over at most 4,096 events in all (`most_skips`), so a backlog of millions of unwanted events cannot hold the loop; the next turn goes on (the loop wakes at least every 50 ms).

**Replay.** A replay to every endpoint goes to those whose list wants the event's type (read from the record `find_offset` found); a replay to one named endpoint is the person asking, and goes whatever the list says.

### 35.4 Rotation

The period is counted from the commit, in memory **and in the row**. The request keeps the length (`keep_old_ms`), not an end time. The table in memory takes `now + length` when the database answers commit; the row is given `secret_old_until = <the database's clock when the statement runs> + length` by the statement itself (`patch_endpoint`: `(extract(epoch from clock_timestamp()) * 1000)::bigint + $7`), which is the commit's moment to within a millisecond. Counting from the request, as the first version did, let a change that waited for the database lose its overlap waiting: the soak found it twice. First as a rotation held 4 s by an unreachable database that kept the previous secret 4 s less than asked (fixed by taking the end in memory from the commit), then, in the second run, as 64 deliveries with one signature after a `kill -9` 2.7 s after such a rotation was answered: the restart reads the row, and the row still held the time as of when the change was sent, earlier than the commit by the wait. The row's time is now taken by the database, so a restart agrees with the live service. **The cost is a second clock for this one field**: after a restart the row's time is compared with the service's clock, so the service and its database should be kept in step (NTP); a database clock behind by *s* shortens the overlap after a restart by *s*, one ahead lengthens it, and nothing else is affected. `rotation_test` stage 11 holds a `PATCH` behind a proxy and checks the row as well as the answer. Time is the Unix clock (`clock_unix_ms`), as the breaker's: a clock set forward ends an overlap early, one set back extends it. The period is kept as an absolute time, so it survives a restart as the row has it, and a row whose time has passed is read as no overlap (the expired key is not even kept in memory). The previous secret stays in the row after the period until the next `PATCH` of the endpoint (or `{"keep_old_ms": 0}`): the table is a secret already (24.1), and the runbook says so. An attempt on the wire when the period ends finishes with the request it began with; the next attempt, a retry of the same event included, is signed afresh (stage 4 of `tests/rotation_test.py`: an event tried inside the period carries two signatures, its retry after it one).

The cost is a second HMAC per attempt while the period lasts (about 100 µs for an event of a few KB, the same as the first).

### 35.5 Custom headers

The spec (`hdrs.ls`) is the one form: the `headers` column, the `headers=` word of `endpoints.conf` and the text the roster hands the parser are the same string, so one function judges all three and the API cannot store what a restart would refuse. A value is percent-encoded because the roster's text is whitespace separated and the secrets are credentials the table must not be able to be used to smuggle a line through: `%0d%0a` in a stored spec decodes to a CR and an LF, which `decode` refuses, so a hand-edited row is a refusal to start (naming the row), not a header. A literal `%0d%0a` typed as a value is `%250d%250a` in the spec and goes out as those characters.

They go after `webhook-signature` and before `Content-Length`. The request buffer of an attempt slot was 66,560 bytes, 1,061 over the largest body; with 2,048 bytes of headers and a second signature it no longer fitted, and a request that does not fit is a failed attempt (`no_send`) that retries to a dead letter. `attempt.req_max()` is 70,656 now (64 slots: 4.52 MB, up from 4.26) and the largest event with the largest headers and two signatures is delivered (`tests/headers_test.py` 7; with the old size it is not: mutant 35).

### 35.6 Where it lives in memory

Not in the host/key `blob`: the endpoints table's variable-size bytes are compacted when it fills (25.4), and three more variable regions per endpoint would have made that code, which has its own unit tests, several times more complex. Each endpoint has a **row of fixed size** in the delivery state (`epx.ls`, 2,660 integers: 512 for the list, 2,048 for the headers as sent, 96 for the previous key, and the lengths), indexed like the endpoints table and shifted down by a delete the same way (`epx.drop_row`, which clears the removed row first: it held credentials). 62 rows are 1.3 MB of the delivery state, touched only where an endpoint has something. The change a `POST` or `PATCH` waits to apply is in a block of its own (`xg`, 4,616 integers), as `mg` is for the address.

### 35.7 Schema, upgrade, files

`sql/schema.sql` adds four columns to `endpoints`, each `not null default`, with `alter table ... add column if not exists`, so `psql -f sql/schema.sql` upgrades a database made by the previous schema (tested: `tests/filter_test.py` and the others all `reset_db` with the new file over a database that has the old table; `tests/roster_test.py` inserts rows by position, four values, which still works). A new binary on an old schema is a refusal at start (status 20, "the query failed"). `queries.ls` is regenerated by `pgen` from `sql/queries.sql`: `endpoints_all` reads the four columns, `add_endpoint` and `create_endpoint` write them, `patch_endpoint` is the one statement that changes any of them (`coalesce` for what the request does not name, one `case` for the previous secret).

`endpoints.conf` and the roster's text: `<id> <host> <port> <secret> [types=...] [headers=...] [old=<secret>@<until>]`. A line without the words is what it always was; a word that is none of these, or twice, or bad, is a bad line. `--import-endpoints` carries them.

### 35.8 Tests, mutants, measurements

**Unit tests** (`lex-sys test`, 9 sets, 103 tests; new: `filter` 12, `hdrs` 12, `epx` 6, and 2 in `endpoints`, 1 in `config`): the patterns and every kind of bad one, the match rules at their edges (a type shorter than the prefix, `user` against `user.*`, a case difference, an untyped event against `*`), the 128/129-byte and 512/513-byte limits, the type found in all four record shapes, every forbidden name in any case and near misses, every control character in a value, `%0d%0a`/`%00`/`%7f`/`%01`/`%1f` in a spec, 8 and 9 headers, 2,048 and 2,049 bytes on the wire, the wire form byte for byte, the rows of `epx` shifted by a delete, the optional words of a line.

**Harnesses** (new; they share `tests/endpoint_kit.py`; each needs the database; the second and third need `pip install standardwebhooks`):

* `tests/filter_test.py` (62 checks, 80 s): the rules against a model written apart from the service (19 event types against 8 lists, a type of 128 and of 129 bytes, one with a control character, one written with a JSON escape, an empty one); every cursor ends at the last event and the history has no row for an event passed over; **3,000 events of which almost none are wanted**: both cursors at 3,000 (the window is 1,024) with the wanted ones delivered in order and `attempts` equal to them (3,000 events posted in 1.7 to 2.2 s, final at both endpoints before the last post returned); **a dead endpoint** that lists a rare type passes over 2,500 events with no attempt, holds at the rare one it cannot deliver and passes over exactly 1,023 more (its window) while it retries, and goes to the end when it is up; **`kill -9` under filtering driven by the receivers' progress** (a kill when 100 more deliveries have arrived since the last start; 39 to 40 kills in a run, 1,167 to 1,275 repeated attempts against a bound of 16 an endpoint a kill): every wanted event arrives at least once, none that is not wanted ever does, every cursor ends at 1,800; a **log written by the test's own record writer with the shapes of before the type existed** (one pair, and three with a key whose time is now): read, untyped, sent only to the endpoints that take everything, the old keys still answer (`old-3` is event 3), a restart reads a log of records of one, two, three and four pairs; the API (16 ways a list can be wrong at `PATCH` and at `POST`, `[]` and `null`, a change reaching only the events not yet looked at); replay to all and to one; `endpoints.conf` with `types=` without a database, `--import-endpoints`, and four bad lists refused at start naming the line; **a narrowed list and a restart** (stage 9: the retries and the delivered are neither lost nor repeated, and what was passed over in memory is decided again under the list then in force).
* `tests/rotation_test.py` (64 checks, 25 s), with the reference library: one signature before; two after `PATCH {"secret", "keep_old_ms": 4000}`, the new first, each the signature the library itself computes, verified by a receiver that knows only the old secret, only the new one, and not by a third; one again when `GET` says the period is over; a retry after the period is signed with the new secret only (same `webhook-id`); `kill -9` in the period keeps it, a row whose time passed while the service was down is no overlap, a period that ended while it was stopped is over at the start; `keep_old: true` is `rotation-grace-ms` (the default a day, then 2,500 ms from the flag); `rotate: true` with a period; a period alone with nothing to keep is a 400, `{keep_old_ms: 0}` ends it, a second rotation keeps only the secret it replaced, a new secret without a period ends a running one; ten wrong periods; neither secret in any read; a replay in the period carries both; `old=` in `endpoints.conf` and in the table; and a previous secret belongs to its endpoint after a delete shifts the rows; and a change the database held 2.5 s (a proxy that holds every byte) keeps its whole period after the answer (the build before the fix fails both of that stage's checks).
* `tests/headers_test.py` (87 checks, 11 s): headers on the wire, name and value byte for byte (every visible ASCII character in one value), in the order after `webhook-signature`, on a retry and on a replay, the signature still verifying with the library; PATCH replaces the set; no value in any read, answer, history row or stderr line; **15 forbidden names in 4 cases each at `POST` and at `PATCH` (120 requests), all refused with a reason**, and four table rows with one refused at start; **20 injection attempts** (CR, LF, CRLF, NUL, SOH, US, tab, DEL, a JSON escape, a whole request in a value, a non-ASCII value, CRLF/NUL/colon/space/comma in a name) and the same bytes written raw in the JSON text, each a 400, and a literal `%0d%0a` sent as the text it is; 12 hand-written table rows with a CR, an LF, a NUL, a bad escape or an empty item refused at start; the limits at both sides of each; **the largest event (65,487 bytes) with 2,048 bytes of headers and two signatures delivered in one attempt**; headers kept across `kill -9` and each endpoint's own after a `DELETE` shifts the rows; `endpoints.conf`, import, and seven bad `headers=` words.

**Existing tests that had to change, and why.** Three assertions pinned the record's shape and now pin the new one (`tests/idempotency_test.py`: `event, key, t` is `event, typ, key, t` and `event` alone is `event, typ`); five compared a whole object (`GET /endpoints` in `manage_test` and `delete_test`, four places; `GET /config` in `config_test`) and name the new members; `tests/backup_test.py` puts a list, a header and an expired previous secret in a row and compares the table row for row with all eight columns after the restore. The rest of the suite runs unchanged.

**Mutants: 40 of the new code, 38 killed, 2 survive and are equivalent by design** (apply to a saved copy, build, run the tests that should kill it, restore from the copy, `cmp`). Killed: the match rule loosened four ways (an exact pattern matching a longer type, a prefix pattern dropping its dot, `*` not everything, any-of becoming all-of); the type never found, a long or control-character type stored; the skip not counted, not made final, the cap at 0, an event with an attempt behind it or a final one passed over by the second look; replay to all ignoring the lists (queued for them) and replay to a named endpoint obeying them; `rebuild` not stepping over `typ` (the service does not start), the type written after the key; the second signature made with the new key, the old signature first, the previous secret never expiring, not kept in memory, the SQL forgetting it, the table's `old=` not read at start, `keep_old` with no period, a period alone accepted with nothing to keep, a new secret without a period leaving the previous one, the default grace changed; a forbidden name allowed, a control character allowed in a value (two ways), a percent-encoded CR/LF allowed, nine headers, a name twice, headers not sent, `GET` showing values, a delete not shifting the rows, `PATCH` ignoring headers, the old request buffer size (the largest event with the largest headers fails), the `headers=` word of a line dropped, the import losing the lists. **The two survivors are redundancy**: the API not judging a header value itself (`hdrs.check_spec` over the whole spec, run after it, refuses the same bytes), and `finish_create` not clearing the row it fills (`drop_row` has cleared the freed row already). Six first survived and were fixed in the tests or the mutant: the replay mutant that changed only the capacity count (now the loop that queues), `rebuild` mutated where the code is equivalent (now the step over the pair), a mutation of the SQL file instead of the generated `queries.ls`, two mutations that still refused CR and LF (the tests now refuse each control character: `0x01`, `0x08`, `0x1f`).

**Measured** (`scripts/bench/run.py`; this machine, 4 cores shared with three other agents running their own suites, load average 5 to 11 throughout, so every figure is noisy; base is the commit before, built by the same compiler, runs alternated in order over six rounds, medians):

| | base | this change |
|---|---|---|
| ingest only, 64 connections: CPU an event | 10 to 12 us | 10 to 13 us (a type pair of 11 bytes plus the type) |
| 1 endpoint: CPU a delivery | 84 us (78 to 103) | 85 us (78 to 98) |
| 1 endpoint: deliveries a second | 4,730 | 4,385 |
| 10 endpoints: CPU a delivery | 69 us (62 to 88) | 72 us (64 to 80) |
| 10 endpoints: deliveries a second | 7,841 | 8,170 |
| 10 endpoints, each with a list that wants the type (new only, 4 runs) | | 63 to 78 us a delivery, as without a list |
| 10 endpoints, each with a list that **does not** want the type: 50,000 (endpoint, event) pairs passed over (new only, 4 runs) | | 0.17 to 0.19 s of CPU in all, ingest of the 5,000 events included: about **2.4 us a pair** against 70 us for a delivery |

In the first, unalternated run the new binary looked 10% slower for one endpoint (74 to 89 us); the run order (the new binary always second, while the load was rising) explained it, and alternating the order removed it. **No cost to a delivery is visible above the noise of this machine**; the reading of the code agrees (one length read of the endpoint's row for a list that is empty, a second HMAC only while a rotation overlaps, a copy of at most 2,048 bytes of headers when it has any). The state is 1.5 MB larger (`xt` and `xg`, lazily touched) and each attempt slot's request buffer 4 KiB larger (64 slots: 256 KB).

### 35.9 Not verified, open, and what the compiler lacked

* **Not verified:** the pinned compiler itself in CI (the compiler used is a local build of the commit `lex-sys.toml` pins, which `lex-sys build` accepted); a log of millions of events with an endpoint that wants almost none of them (the cost of a restart there is one read per event above its cursor; measured only as 2.4 us a pair in a running service); a clock that jumps during a rotation; a table whose text is near 32 KiB (the estimate in `POST /endpoints` is not exact: it counts decoded bytes and a guess at the rest, and the real limit is the roster's text, so a hand-edited table can still refuse the start, with the same message as a table that is too large today); two services on one database.
* **Open:** no `advance` record (35.3); a change of `types` and a restart decide differently for an event that was passed over but is not yet behind the cursor (documented, tested); the previous secret stays in the row until the next `PATCH` of the endpoint; `GET /events/:id` does not show the type; the inbound gateway's record (`docs/inbound-gateway.md` 5.3) lists `src` and `hdr` before `typ`, and `filter.type_in` reads the second pair, so the gateway has to write `typ` second or `type_in` has to look for the pair by name past the others (a few lines, when it is built).
* **What the compiler lacked or let through:** (1) `fn use2(x: &!a [byte], y: &b [byte])` called as `use2(buf, buf[8..16])` compiles and runs: an exclusive borrow and a shared one of the same array in one call are accepted. I had assumed it was refused and wrote around it with a region in `handle`; it is not needed, and it may be a hole in the borrow rules (reproducer: `region a { let buf = alloc_slice[a](16, byte_of(7)); use2(buf, buf[8..16]); }`). (2) A failed `test.assert_eq` traps (`SIGILL`) with no line or values, as section 32.9 says; three of my expectations were wrong (a length counted by hand, a stale byte in a reused buffer, an offset off by one) and each took a probe test to find.

**The suite, before the merge with sections 33 and 34.** Every step of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` as it was then, in order, against this build and a PostgreSQL of its own, passed: 30 steps (`fmt --check src`, the unit tests, `cron_test`, `sign_test`, `config`, `history`, `roster`, `layout`, `manage`, `slots`, `patch`, `delete`, `saturation`, `ssrf`, `replay`, `gone`, `attempt`, `retry`, `isolation`, `scan`, `breaker`, `chaos` (2,000 events, 101 kills as power cuts), `backup`, `shellcheck`, `delivery` (101 kills), `idempotency` and `schedules` with `FULL=1`, and the three new ones). One run of `delete_test` failed once with a refused connection after a restart of the service in its stage 8 and passed in all five reruns; I could not reproduce it and have no explanation (a service of this test killed from outside is the likeliest, the machine being shared). The `schedules` step failed once in the last full run and passed when run again alone: services of mine that an aborted mutant run had left running against the same database (they did not have `cron-seconds`, so they parked the test's six-field schedule) were the cause; the harness is fixed, which now kills what it started when a check fails with `FAIL_FAST`.

### 35.10 Together with sections 33 and 34

This section was written on the commit before the credentials (33) and the operating features (34) and then rebased onto both. Where the three met, none gave way:

* **The settings table.** `config.ls` has 23 integers: 17 and 18 are `stop-deadline-ms` and `repair-logs` (34), 19 to 21 the lengths of the ingest and read tokens and `production` (33), and **22 is `rotation-grace-ms`**. `tests/config_test.py` reads back every side's settings given at once, from a file and from flags, and refuses the bad values of this section's setting like the others.
* **The delivery state.** The tail of the state is now: the history ring (`off_hq`), the counters of `ops.ls` (`off_ops`), the rows of the extras (`off_xt`), the change that waits (`off_xg`) and the extras' counters and the type being accepted (`off_ex`), each starting where the last ends, and `dv_size` ends at the last (`tests/layout_test.py` checks that no region overlaps another).
* **Credentials.** No route was added. `POST` and `PATCH /endpoints` take `types`, `headers` and `keep_old_ms` under the admin token as before; `GET /endpoints` and `GET /endpoints/:id` are read scope and show the patterns, the header **names** and `secret_old_until`, never a value or a secret (`tests/authz_test.py` runs against the merged routes; its request bodies are valid under the new parsing). `POST /events/:id/replay` is admin scope, which is why the three harnesses of this section send the admin token to it; before, replay needed none.
* **The logs.** Event types add a pair to `events.seg` records (35.1) and no kind to `delivery.seg`, whose kinds 1 to 14 are those of section 34 (14: why an attempt failed). The corruption check of section 34.5 looks at the frames of a record (length, checksum, the pairs filling it) and not at their names, so it takes typed records; `scripts/logcheck.py` now also checks that each event record is one of the four shapes (`event`; `event`, `typ`; `event`, `key`, `t`; `event`, `typ`, `key`, `t`) and counts the typed ones, and `tests/backup_test.py` checks that a backup of typed events passes it, restores and starts.
* **The database.** `attempts.reason` (34.3) and the four columns of `endpoints` (35.7) are both in `sql/schema.sql`; `src/queries.ls` regenerated from `sql/queries.sql` with `pgen` against a database that has both is identical to the one in the tree but for its first line, so the statements of both sides are in it.
* **Exit statuses.** This section adds none. The refusals of an endpoints table or file that names a bad list or header are the existing 13 (the file) and 20 (the database); 18 and 19 are section 34's, 30 to 35 section 33's.
* **The authority report.** The service held `Ffi("libc")` for five functions then (four signal functions in `ops.ls`, `statx` in `perm.ls`; the four are gone, section 36), so `lex-sys authority` opened with `UNBOUNDED`. This section calls no foreign code.
* **CI.** The three harnesses of this section were not steps of the workflow; they are now, after `delete`.


## 36. Signals as a capability: the four libc functions are gone

Section 34.4 learned of a stop through `Ffi("libc")`: `sigblock`, `sigsetmask`, `sigpending` and `signal`, and 34.9 asked lex-sys for "a `std.signal` behind a capability of its own". lex-sys has it (lex-sys#244, `docs/signals.md` there, edition 6). This section is the move.

**What changed.** The project's compiler pin moved to `4c27593f` (`lex-sys.toml`), which has the signals capability (`32bad9c`) and the report that names foreign symbols (`unbounded_by`, lex-sys#247, `docs/foreign-authority.md` there) on top of it; ( the CI workflow and the Dockerfile read the pin from that file, and the compiler's cache key in CI is that pin, so no other place names the revision). `src/hooks.ls` and `src/ops.ls` are `edition 6;`, every other file stays edition 5 (a program mixes editions; `Split` only gained a field at 6). `main` destructures `signals`, narrows it to `"INT,TERM"` and claims both signals (`signals_watch`) just before it calls `run`: after the recovery of the logs, which is where `sigblock` was called before, so a stop during the recovery still has its default action, and before any thread could exist (the service spawns none, and a claim is refused with `EBUSY` while one is live). `run` takes the claim, registers it in the poller of the server (`poller_add_signals`, token `first_token + attempt.slots() + 16`, clear of the attempts', the pool's and the connections'), and looks at it once a turn (`signals_pending`, which never waits). The drain is unchanged. The first signal closes the claim (`signals_close`), which restores the default action: the next `SIGTERM` or `SIGINT` ends the process at once, which is "a second signal ends it at once" exactly as before, and the logic is one function, `ops.look`.

**Why an enum.** A claim is a linear value, and a loop body may not consume a value declared outside it (`docs/linearity-and-effects.md` 4.3), so the claim cannot be closed from inside the loop. It travels in `ops.Held { Live(SignalWatch), Closed }`: `ops.look(held)` takes it by value and answers it again, `Closed` once a signal was seen. Nothing in lex-sys needed to change for that; the shape is awkward but small (about 40 lines in `ops.ls`).

**What the authority report says.** Before (the previous pin, `lex-sys authority` over the program): `UNBOUNDED`; 15 labels, among them `ffi("libc")  <- unbounded`; foreign symbols `sigblock`, `signal`, `sigpending`, `sigsetmask`, `statx`; `signals` never touched. After, with the compiler of the new pin: `"bounded": false` with `"unbounded_by": ["libc:statx"]`, the same 15 labels plus `signals("INT,TERM")` and `signals_read` (both bounded), and `"foreign_symbols": ["statx"]`. So the service has **no `unsafe` and one foreign symbol, libc's `statx`** (`src/perm.ls`, the production profile's mode check; lex-sys has no file-mode builtin: lex-sys#243), and that one symbol is the whole of the reason the report is not `bounded`. `src/perm.ls`'s declaration already met the rule that a foreign function borrows exactly one `Ffi` naming one library (`foreign-declaration`), so it needed no change, and `log`, `pg`, `pool` and `server` at their pins build unchanged.

The report is committed as `docs/authority.json` and a CI step runs `scripts/check-authority.sh`, which regenerates it and fails on any difference: a new foreign symbol is exactly one added line in `unbounded_by` (`scope:symbol`), a new capability an added label, and either is a red diff that a person approves by committing the new file. The file leaves out the list of provably pure functions and the three counts (`folded_operators`, `folded_calls`, `functions`), which change with every function and say nothing about authority; everything else in the report, and any field a later compiler adds, is pinned, and the script refuses a compiler whose report has no `unbounded_by`. Mutant: an `extern fn getpid` called from `perm.ls` turns the step red with `+ "libc:getpid"` in the diff.

**A stop is noticed at once.** The old loop looked once a turn, and a turn waits at most 50 ms. Now the claim is readable in the poller, so `poller_wait` returns when the signal arrives. Measured as the time from `send_signal` in a test process to the line `hooks: stopping on` arriving on the pipe of stderr (so it includes the pipe and the reading thread, which are hundreds of microseconds), 60 starts each, an idle service, the signal sent at a random point of the loop's wait, alternating `SIGTERM` and `SIGINT`:

| | min | median | p90 | max |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| before (four libc functions) | 1.1 ms | 25.0 ms | 45.6 ms | 49.5 ms |
| after (the capability) | 0.09 ms | 0.24 ms | 0.54 ms | 3.2 ms |

**Behaviour that differs.** (1) A service started with `SIGINT` ignored (a background job, `nohup`) now drains on the first `SIGINT`; the signal is ignored again afterwards (the claim never touched the disposition), so a second `SIGINT` does nothing and `SIGTERM` ends it. (2) If the claim is refused (`EMFILE`, or `EBUSY`, which nothing can cause today) the service says so and exits with status **5** instead of running without hearing a stop. (3) The system calls: `rt_sigprocmask` and `signalfd4` once, no `rt_sigaction`; `signalfd4` is in `@signal`, inside `@system-service`.

**Compiler and libraries.** `log`, `pg`, `pool` and `server` at the pins of `lex-sys.toml` build and pass their users' tests under the new compiler (checked at `32bad9c` and at `4c27593f`); no library pin moved. Found: `lex-sys authority` has no project mode (`lex-sys build` and `test` read `lex-sys.toml`; `authority` takes a file list), so the script builds the list from the `hooks` program of the project file and the libraries that `lex-sys install` writes to `build/deps`.

**A test that was racing.** `tests/stop_test.py` stage 3 (`--stop-deadline-ms 0`) failed 3 runs in 15 with the new binary and none in 15 with the old: the service now stops within a fraction of a millisecond, and the harness read its stderr from a thread that had not yet taken the last lines when `wait_exit` returned (the failure showed only `listening`). The old loop's wait hid it. `Svc.wait_exit` in `tests/opslib.py` now joins that reader thread once the process has exited (its end of the pipe is closed, so the join returns at once); no check changed, and the stage failed 0 in 30 runs afterwards.

**The suite.** Every test step of `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, in its order, with `FULL=1` for idempotency and schedules, against a PostgreSQL of this machine's own and the compiler of the pin: 38 steps, all green (formatting, the authority pin, the unit tests, `cron`, `sign`, `config`, `authz`, `history`, `roster`, `production`, `layout`, `manage`, `slots`, `patch`, `delete`, `filter`, `rotation`, `headers`, `saturation`, `ssrf`, `replay`, `gone`, `attempt`, `retry`, `isolation`, `scan`, `breaker`, `chaos`, `backup`, `shellcheck`, `delivery`, `idempotency`, `schedules`, `stop`, `corrupt`, `metrics` and `ready`, `reason`). The same harnesses again as the unprivileged user `nobody` from a copy of the tree: 34 steps, all green (the tmpfs part of `ready_test.py` needs root and says so; the formatting, the install, the authority step and the unit tests were run as root only). `stop_test.py` alone, 10 runs each as root and as `nobody`: 0 failures.

## 37. A database that comes back, and a start that does not wait for it (`docs/production.md` 0.4 caveat 3; closes the last item of 34.9)

*Written with the code and its tests, from what they did. `lexsys-pg` PR #12 (`7a0acd7`) made its pool reconnect without waiting (`docs/reconnect.md` there); this is the service using it.*

### 37.1 What was wrong

A database connection that was lost was never opened again. Until the service was restarted: `/readyz` stayed 503 (`database`), every attempt's history row was dropped (`history_dropped`), `POST /endpoints` and the schedules' routes answered 503, and the cron tick stopped. And the start waited for the database: the endpoints table was read, with a blocking client that has no timeout, before the service listened, so a database that was silent held the start for ever. Both came from one thing: a login and a connect block, and the loop is the one thread that serves every request.

### 37.2 What the loop does now

* **One pool of two connections, configured at the start and made in the background.** `history.configure` gives the pool the login (user, password, database), the statements of `queries.ls` (`prepare_script`, which `pgen` writes; `queries.ls` was regenerated), the waits between attempts, the time an attempt and a request may take, and a 32-byte seed read from `/dev/urandom` for the SCRAM nonces. Nothing is dialed at the start.
* **Each turn:** the sleep bound is the smaller of 50 ms and `pool.next_wake` (a backoff that ends, a login with a key to derive, an attempt or a request that runs out of time; the poller wakes the loop for the rest); then the turn's `pump`s, then `pool.revive` (tick, `tcp_connect_start` for each connection that is due, `adopt`), then the answers (`next_done`). `revive` takes the pool by value, so the borrow blocks of that part of `run` are split around it.
* **Why `revive` and not `tick`, a dial of our own and `adopt`.** The pool never holds a `Net`, so that a program with a narrowed network keeps its report (reconnect.md 2.4). This service holds the whole network already, for the receivers (`net_out("")` in `docs/authority.json`), so the one row `revive` names is a row it has: **the authority report did not change** (`scripts/check-authority.sh` is green, `docs/authority.json` untouched). The seed is read with the `fs_read("")` the service already performs for the SCRAM nonce of the old blocking login and for the secrets it makes. No new capability, no new foreign symbol.
* **What is counted:** `pool.live`, `connecting`, `reconnects`, `failures`, `losses` are copied into the history block each turn (`history.sync`), where `/stats` and `/metrics` read them (`hooks_database_*`, 37.6).
* **Said on stderr:** a line when the number of live connections changes (`hooks: the database: N of 2 connections live`), not one per attempt.

### 37.3 The start with the database away (the decision)

**The question is a safety one.** Until section 37 the endpoints table was read before the service listened, and "the table is the truth" (24.1) was kept by refusing to start. With a start that does not wait, there is a time in which the service is up and does not know who to deliver to. What must never happen is an event treated as final for an endpoint that the service has not loaded. Three designs were weighed.

| | what it does | the problem |
|---|---|---|
| A. refuse (what was) | the start waits, then ends with 20 | it waits (the thing to remove); a supervisor restarts it in a loop while the database boots |
| B. wait before listening, bounded | the start waits up to N seconds | still a start that waits; the port is closed meanwhile, so a producer cannot even store an event |
| **C. listen, take events, deliver nothing, read the table when it can** | the port is open at once; the table is read the first time a connection is live | needs a late load that is the same code as the start's, and a rule for the time before it |

**C was built.** Why taking events meanwhile is safe: an event is durable the moment it is flushed to the events log, and delivery is *by cursor*: an endpoint's cursor says every event up to it is final, and it only moves when an attempt's outcome is recorded. Nothing is delivered while the service has no endpoints (`delivery_turn` runs only when `c_endpoints > 0`), so no outcome is recorded and no cursor moves. When the table is read, `load_late` runs the **same code as a start that read it** (`settle_endpoints`: each row takes the slot the log gave it, the outcomes of earlier runs are replayed so the cursors are where they were, rows the log has not seen are placed at the slowest cursor of the others (25.3), and each endpoint's place in the events log is found). An event taken meanwhile is above every cursor, so it is sent to every endpoint, once. A row new to the log starts at the slowest cursor of the endpoints the log knows, 0 if there are none: the backlog taken while the service was blind is sent to it, the same rule as a restart with a row added by hand. `tests/pgre_test.py` 5a, 5c and 12 check it: five events taken with the database away are delivered once each after it comes; with a log that had cursors, the three delivered before are not sent again and the three taken meanwhile are sent once.

**What is 503 until the table is read:** the routes that list, change, enable or replay endpoints (`GET /endpoints`, `GET/PATCH/DELETE /endpoints/:id`, `POST /endpoints`, `POST /endpoints/:id/enable`, `POST /events/:id/replay[/:endpoint]`): `{"error":"the endpoints are not loaded yet: the database has not answered since the service started"}`. An empty list would be a lie, and a replay to an unknown id a 404 about something that exists. `POST /events`, `GET /events/:id`, the history, the schedules, `/healthz`, `/stats`, `/config` and `/metrics` work. `/readyz` is 503 `database` until a connection is live **and** the table is read (`history.serving`): there is a window of milliseconds, and in a test with a view that sleeps two seconds a long one, in which a connection is live and the endpoints are not.

**When the service ends (statuses as before):**

* **at once**, with 20, for a refusal that waiting does not mend: the server refuses the login with SQLSTATE class 28 (a wrong password, a role that does not exist, no `pg_hba` line) or `3D000` (no such database), a login this client cannot do (MD5, a SCRAM failure on this side), a statement it will not prepare (a table is missing), or an answer it refuses. A refusal that is only for now (`57P03` the system is starting up, `53300` too many connections) is waited out like a connection that fails. `dbup.verdict` is that rule, as a function, with unit tests.
* **after `pg-start-wait-ms`** (30 s; 0 never), with 20, `cannot connect` or `the database did not answer in time`: the connection cannot be made or the server says nothing. 30 s is long enough for a database that boots beside the service and short enough that a misconfigured host is not "up" for ever.
* with 13 (a row the parser refuses) or 20 (a field with an unprintable byte, a table too large) **when the table is read**, as before, with the same words; the status is the process's exit status, so a supervisor sees what it saw.
* **never** once the table has been read. A service that has delivered is not ended by the database.

Both the refusals and `listening` kept their meaning where a test could rely on it, except `listening`: it now means *the port is open*. A harness that wants "ready for requests" waits for `hooks: endpoints loaded: N` (the test harnesses do: `tests/pgwait.py`, `opslib.Service.start`) or for `/readyz`.

**Not done, on purpose:** the table is read **once**. A reconnect does not read it again, and so a row added or changed behind the service's back is seen at the next start, as it always was, and a change whose outcome the service did not learn (37.4) is not in the running service until then. A re-read on reconnect would be a diff of the table against the state (new rows placed, vanished rows made dormant, changed rows patched), which is the three slices of 25 done a second time from the other side; it is the next piece if it is wanted. **Retention (section 38) waits for the table.** The cursors are meaningless until `endpoints_loaded` is 1, so `rt_floor` answers 0 (nothing is final) and `rt_maintain` takes no step but sealing a segment: a drop rests on the cursors, and a snapshot is built from them, so with the table empty it would have replaced the outcomes log by nothing. `compact-now` with a database named does not read the table (it does not wait for the database), so it refuses with status 44 and changes nothing; `tests/pgre_test.py` stage 15 checks both. **(Corrected by section 41.8: `compact-now` with a database now reads the table through the pool code, waiting at most `pg-start-wait-ms`, and does its pass; it ends with status 20 and changes nothing only if it cannot. The refusal with status 44 described here is gone.)**

### 37.4 What each user of the pool does when it is away

| user | no connection is live | the connection is lost with the request on it |
|---|---|---|
| history rows | wait in the ring of 256 (`history_queue`); the 257th is dropped and counted (`history_dropped`). They are written when a connection is live | the rows on the wire are counted `failed`: whether they were written is not known. They are not sent again (the insert is `on conflict do nothing`, so it could be; the pool does not say which requests it had sent, 37.9) |
| `POST/PATCH/DELETE /endpoints`, `POST/PATCH/DELETE /schedules` | `503` at once, "cannot be stored now": nothing was sent | `504`, "the connection to the database was lost while the change was being stored; the change may have been stored". **Outcome unknown, not refused.** Nothing in memory changes. The row may exist: the running service does not know it until a restart; a retry of a `POST` can make a second one |
| `GET /schedules[/:id]`, `GET /events/:id/attempts` | `503` | `503` (a read: nothing to be unsure of) |
| the cron tick | not started (no cycle, no error counted) | the select: the cycle is over, counted an error, the next one asks again. An update: the fire's event is in the log under `cron:<id>:<second>` already (the key is in the index), the row is still due, the next cycle sends the update again and no event is made twice |
| the read of the table | asked as soon as a connection is live | asked again |

**The request deadline.** A request the service holds for a client is answered 504 after 5 s whatever the pool does (as before). The pool's own `pg-request-ms` (10 s) is the bound on a connection that says nothing while a request is on it: past it the connection is given up (status 12) and every request on it is answered; it is longer than the 5 s so that the service's answer is the one clients see for a slow query, and the pool's is the one for a link that is dead.

### 37.5 The history queue

While no connection is live the rows wait in the ring that already existed (256 rows of nine integers). That is a bounded queue with the old counters: `history_queue` rows waiting, `history_dropped` rows that did not fit, `history_failed` rows that were on the wire when the connection went or that the server refused, `history_written`. Every attempt that ends is a row and a row is in exactly one of these; `tests/pgre_test.py` stage 2 checks that after an outage `attempts = written + failed + dropped` with the queue empty, and that the table has at least `written` and at most `written + failed` rows. At 60 attempts a second an outage of three seconds queued 180 rows and dropped none; at 100 a second, five seconds, the queue filled (256) and 380 rows were dropped and counted. A stop with the database away does not wait for the queue (`busy` looks at it only when a connection is live).

### 37.6 Settings, and what is counted

| setting | default | why |
|---|---|---|
| `pg-backoff-min-ms`, `pg-backoff-max-ms` | 100, 5000 | the pool's own suggestion (reconnect.md 2.1): 100, 200, 400 ... 5 s, per connection. A database that boots is found within a second or two; one that is gone is asked twice a second at most for each connection, then every five seconds. A connection that was live for a second is replaced at once, whatever these say. The longest may not be below the first (refused at start: `pg_status`; it depends on two settings, so it is judged when all are in) |
| `pg-attempt-ms` | 5000 | dial, login and the 15 statements; a server that accepts and says nothing costs this much per attempt, not a stall (the loop turns meanwhile) |
| `pg-request-ms` | 10000 | twice the 5 s after which the service answers a held request itself (37.4); `0` never, and then a link that dies without a reset is found only by a request that waits for the kernel (minutes) |
| `pg-start-wait-ms` | 30000 | 37.3 |

`/config` shows all five. `/stats`: `endpoints_loaded`, `history_queue`, `database_reconnects`, `database_failures`, `database_losses` (and the old `history_*`). `/metrics`: `hooks_database_connecting`, `hooks_database_reconnects_total`, `hooks_database_connect_failures_total`, `hooks_database_connection_losses_total`, `hooks_endpoints_loaded`, beside `hooks_history_connections` (live) and the history's rows. All since the start.

### 37.7 Tests

`tests/pgre_test.py` (new; 14 stages, about 130 checks) puts `tests/pgproxy.py` between the service and PostgreSQL. The proxy now has: `cut` (a FIN, new connections closed), `refuse` (new ones closed), `freeze` (nothing read and nothing lost: the bytes wait in the kernel, as after a pulled and replugged cable), `hold` (read and thrown away, as before), `blackhole` (open connections freeze and the proxy stops accepting with its queue full, so a SYN is dropped and the dial waits for the kernel), and `kill_backends` (`pg_terminate_backend` for the backends of the proxy's own connections only, found by their source ports, or, where something between the proxy and the server makes new connections (CI's `postgres:16` service container: its port mapping re-originates every connection, so the server never sees our ports and the first CI run ended 0 backends in stages 4, 6, 7, 9 and 13), by the address and the start time of the backend: in this database, from the address the test's own `psql` has, started within a quarter of a second of a connection the proxy made). Nothing stops or restarts the server, except stage 14, which needs commands for a server that is the test's own.

| stage | what it checks |
|---|---|
| 1 | idle, cut, restored: `/readyz` 503 `database` within a second or two, 200 again by itself (the same process; 1.66 s after the proxy came back), the counters, the endpoints answered from memory meanwhile, an event after it delivered and its row written |
| 2 | 60 events a second through a 3 s outage, and 100 a second through a 5 s one: every event acknowledged is delivered, once (341 of 341; 886 of 886); `attempts = written + failed + dropped`, queue empty; the short outage dropped none and the long one dropped 380 and said so; the table has at least what was written and at most that and the failed |
| 3 | a trigger holds an insert of an endpoint (and then of a schedule) on the wire and the proxy cuts: **504 "may have been stored" within a second**, not at the deadline; with the connection gone, 503 at once; after it is back both routes work. The row of unknown outcome is in the table (3 rows) and not in the service (2): the documented gap |
| 4 | a schedule due every second through a 4 s outage and through 16 backends ended in 12 s: 30 fires in the log, every `(schedule, second)` once and every key once; none made while it could not ask, and no errors counted for the cycles not started; it fires again, with no restart |
| 5 | the start: refused (listens at once; 503 on readyz and the routes; 202 on events; nothing delivered; the five events delivered once each after it loads, with no restart); black-holed (listens at once, 8 failed attempts in 5 s, the longest wait of a probe 4 ms); with a log that has cursors (the three events delivered before are not sent again, the three taken meanwhile are sent once); `pg-start-wait-ms 0` waits, a `SIGTERM` while it waits exits 0; a role that does not exist ends it at once with 20 |
| 6 | a role that may no longer log in, with its connections ended: 10 attempts in 6 s (the waits double), 0.01 s of CPU, the service stays up, answers, and is ready again within the longest wait after the role may log in |
| 7 | the loop never stalls: a probe asks `/healthz` on a new connection over and over (8,000 probes) through a steady state, a black hole of 7 s with events flowing, a cut, and six pairs of backends ended; the longest wait in any phase **23 ms** (first run), 9 ms (second); every event acknowledged delivered once |
| 8 | `pg-request-ms`: a connection that goes silent with a request on it is given up after 1.5 s, the request answered 503, and a new connection is made |
| 9 | backends ended while idle: both replaced in 0.07 s, no failed attempt, statements prepared again |
| 10 | the sleep bound includes the pool's: with waits of 2 ms, 1,700 attempts in 2 s (0.14 s of CPU), not the 80 of a 50 ms turn |
| 11 | a table whose answer is over the pool's 128 KiB slab ends the start with 20 `too large` (it was asked for again for ever until this stage found it) |
| 12 | a view that sleeps 2 s makes the read long: a connection is live and `/readyz` is 503 and `/endpoints` 503; the connection cut in the middle of the read is asked again and the endpoints load once |
| 13 | with a password (`HOOKS_PG_PASSWORD`; CI's server wants SCRAM-SHA-256): six pairs of backends ended, every login of the reconnects SCRAM, none failed, the longest wait of a probe 9 ms (20 ms in another run); a wrong password at the start is refused at once |
| 14 | with commands to stop and start the server (a private cluster of this machine's, not the shared one): the server stopped for four seconds and started again: ready 2.1 s after the start, 562 events taken, 562 delivered once each; 438 rows written, 0 failed, 124 dropped |

The suites that existed changed only where their meaning did: `history_test` 4 (the refusal is after `pg-start-wait-ms`, and the service listens first), 6 (the rows wait in the queue), 11 (`--pg-request-ms 0`: the stage is about the service's five seconds and slots, and with the default the pool gives up the connection at ten); `roster_test` 6 and 8 (the same, and a silent database no longer holds the start); `ready_test` 2 (it asserted that `/readyz` stays 503 after the database is back: it asserts 200 now); `config_test` (the new settings); and every harness that starts a service with a database waits for `hooks: endpoints loaded` after `listening` (`tests/pgwait.py`, `opslib.Service.start`). All the other steps of `ci.yml` are unchanged and green (the whole list, `FULL=1` for idempotency and schedules).
