**Mutants: 30 run, 30 killed** (each applied to a saved copy of one file in a scratch tree, built, run against the test that should fail, and the file restored from the copy and compared with `cmp`; none was left on disk): the ring dropping a row when no connection is live; `sync` keeping no live count; readiness not asking whether the endpoints are read; the endpoints always known; `enable` saying they are read; `/config` giving the first setting for the second; failures counted as reconnects; losses as failures; the seed of the SCRAM nonces empty (survived the trust-authenticated runs, killed by stage 13); the two waits swapped; the attempt and request times swapped; a longest wait equal to the first refused; its default changed; the limit of `pg-request-ms` off by one; class 28 not for good; the limit not reached at the limit; a limit of 0 a limit; a refused statement a refused login; the sleep bound ignoring the pool; the pool not revived; the first read skipping the replay and placing (the first version did not build, redone to compile); `DELETE /endpoints` not gated; a change lost in flight a 503 (by the status and by skipping the check, for endpoints and for schedules); the cron cycle started with no connection; the wait counted from time 0; a verdict not ending the run; a read lost in flight counted as read; the slab overrun asked again. One mutant is equivalent and was not counted: dropping the `pool.live > 0` test before the first read is submitted (`submit` answers -3 and the read is asked again next turn).

### 37.8 Measured

* **The loop does not wait.** Longest wait for `/healthz` by a probe: 4 to 8 ms with the database black-holed (frozen connections, dials that hang) at the start; 4 to 13 ms in a 7 s black hole with events flowing; 5 to 9 ms in a cut; 6 to 23 ms across six pairs of backends ended and their logins; 3 to 20 ms with SCRAM-SHA-256 logins (the key derivation is in pieces of 128 iterations a turn, `reconnect.md` 5.2). Four cores, load average 3 to 5 from other work.
* **How fast it is back.** Idle cut 1.5 s and restored: `/readyz` 200 1.66 s after. Backends ended: both replaced in 0.07 s. A real server stopped 4 s and started: ready 2.1 s after the start. A login refused for 6 s: 10 attempts, 0.01 s of CPU.
* **The hot path.** `scripts/bench/run.py`, base (`4ee5543`) and this build alternated, six rounds of `ten_pg` (10 endpoints from the table, every attempt a row, 50,000 deliveries): CPU per delivery base 91, 86, 84, 67, 88, 77 us (median 85), new 91, 87, 82, 87, 81, 86 (median 86.5): not different on a machine whose load average was 4. Three rounds of `ingest64` (50,000 events), `one`, `ten` and `ingest64_pg` (a database named, the pool idle): 10-11 against 8-12 us an event, 86-88 against 86-94 us a delivery, 68-70 against 69-79; the pool's turn costs nothing the bench can see. (`ten_pg` and `ingest64_pg` are new scenarios of the script.)

### 37.9 Not verified, and what the libraries lack

* **A host name for `pg-host` stalls the loop.** `tcp_connect_start` resolves a name with `getaddrinfo`, which waits. Reproducer (this machine, as root): bind a UDP socket on `127.0.0.1:53` that never answers, `unshare -m` and bind-mount a `resolv.conf` with `nameserver 127.0.0.1` and `options timeout:2 attempts:2` over `/etc/resolv.conf`, and start `hooks --pg-host db.nowhere.example ...` with a probe on `/healthz`: **the longest wait was 5,006 ms, and 5 answers in 25 s**: the resolver's time on every attempt. With an address, 4 ms. The runbook, the README and `hooks.conf.example` say to give an address; `deploy/hooks.docker.conf` shows `--pg-host db` for a compose service, which should be an address too until lex-sys has a resolver that does not wait (the work on `--dns-server` for https endpoints may be it).
* **A request lost with the connection is "unknown" even when it was never sent.** The pool answers a request that was queued and not yet written with the same status (1, 3, 6 or 11, 12) as one the server may have run; only `submit`'s -3 says "did not run". So the history rows on a connection that goes are counted `failed` and not queued again, although the insert is idempotent, and a change that never left the buffer is a 504 and not a 503. A status or a call that says "this one was never written" (or a `pool.requeue`) would let both be better.
* **The table is read once** (37.3) and a change of unknown outcome stays unknown to the running service.
* **Never run:** a real systemd and a container with the new health check; macOS; a server that accepts and answers `57P03` for a long time (the unit test of `dbup.verdict` has it, the pool's own mock test too, not this service); `pg-host` on IPv6.
* **Merge hotspots** (other changes to `src/hooks.ls`, `config.ls`, `metrics.ls`): the `run` signature and the head of its loop (`nap`, the pump block split around `pool.revive`), `handle`'s first lines (the gate), `/stats` and `/config`, `main` between the settings and `run` (the pool is configured there; `fetch` and the blocking history open are gone), `config.size()` and the last branch of `config.set`, `metrics.g_size()` and the cells after 36, `history.size()`, and `queries.ls`, which is generated: regenerate it with `pgen` after any change to `sql/`.

## 38. Retention: the logs are bounded by what is kept, not by history (`docs/production.md` 0.2)

*The design came first (`docs/retention.md`, committed before any code); this section is what building it found and measured. Where the two disagree the code is right, and `docs/retention.md` section 16 lists what changed.*

**What was wrong.** `events.seg` and `delivery.seg` were append-only and never shrank: disk grew with history, the start read both whole, and the idempotency index (65,536 keys, never forgetting one) was full after 45 days of a once-a-minute schedule.

**What it is now** (the full account is `docs/retention.md`; five lines here).

* The events log is a chain of **segments** (`events.seg`, `events-1.seg`, ...) with a manifest (`events.first`) and a header record in each (format 2, the segment's number, base offset, first id). Offsets are **logical** (header bytes excluded), so dropping the front rewrites nothing. A segment is deleted when it is sealed, every event in it is **final at every endpoint** (the least cursor, and no replay waiting below it), and the segment after it is older than `max(retention, window-ms)`. A paused, disabled or dead endpoint, or a waiting replay, **pins** its events at any age.
* The outcomes log is **replaced by a snapshot** (the shortest log that replays to the same state: a header, `created`, the final and waiting cells of each window, the flags, the replays) through `delivery.seg.tmp`, `fsync`, rename, directory `fsync`. Nothing in recovery changed: the snapshot is made of records recovery already knew.
* The idempotency index is two **FIFO rings** with open-addressing hashes (backward-shift deletion, no tombstones): a key leaves when its window has passed or its event is dropped; cron's keys have an index of their own. Event ids are **never reused**: the active segment's header names its first id.
* Every file begins with a **format version** and an unknown one is refused (status 40 events, 41 outcomes, 42 a hole in the chain); a directory from before retention is format 1 and is read as it was. Retention runs inside the loop, one small step a turn, under `compact.lock` (taken without waiting; the online backup holds it), and `--compact-now 1` does one pass and exits.
* `lexsys-log` was **not changed**: the chain, the staging, the manifest and the replacement are this repository's (`src/evlog.ls`, `src/store.ls`, `src/compact.ls`, `src/retain.ls`); `docs/lexsys-log-retention.md` is the proposal for what a library would own, and the lex-sys gaps found, with reproducers.

### 38.1 The gate, and what was measured

| gate | evidence |
|---|---|
| **millions of events through; disk bounded by retention, not by history** | `scripts/bench/retention_bench.py through` (retention 20 s, window 5 s, segments of 32 MiB, outcomes limit 16 MiB, so that a month happens in seconds; one endpoint, the C sink answering every delivery; the poster waits for the deliveries between chunks of 100,000, because an event not delivered is not droppable and a backlog is not what retention bounds). **Two runs, on two builds, and they must not be mixed up.** *On the merged build (main `4ee5543` + this work, compiler `4c27593`): 3,000,000 events,* all delivered (7,775 a second end to end, the service's CPU 230 s) on a machine others were using; the data directory peaked at **95.3 MB** while the history would have taken about 1,002 MB (257 bytes an event now that the type is stored, 77 an outcome), **24 segments sealed and 24 dropped, 13 snapshots, 0 errors**, the longest step of the loop **30 ms** (`maintenance_ms_max`), and resident memory **2.7 MB at the start, 9.7 MB from the 96th second to the end** (flat; it was 172 to 182 MB before the compiler allocated lazily). *On the build before the merge (this work on `5543bfe`, the earlier compiler): 10,000,000 events,* all delivered (5,274 a second, CPU 681 s), the directory **between 15 and 93 MB** against about 3,180 MB of history (34 times smaller), 100 segments sealed and dropped, the outcomes log replaced 45 times, 0 errors, the longest step 68 ms, resident memory 172 to 182 MB flat. **The ten-million run was not repeated on the merged build** (it takes about 30 minutes at the measured rate): the code that bounds the disk did not change in the merge, and 3,000,000 events at 20 s of retention take the same path 24 times. |
| **start time at 1M retained events** | `scripts/bench/retention_bench.py start --events 1000000`: 1,000,000 events (257 MB in 8 segments of 32 MiB) posted with retention off, the service killed, the time from `exec` to `listening` measured three times with the files in the page cache, in three separate runs, on the merged build. **No endpoint: 0.89 to 0.95 s** (3 MB resident). **One endpoint that has been sent all of them, with 128,460 records (9.9 MB) in the outcomes log: 2.12 to 2.22 s.** The machine was shared (load average 3 to 4 on 4 cores) and these are not the figures of the build before the merge (0.69 to 0.77 s and 1.50 to 2.11 s, 241 MB of events): part of the difference is the 16 bytes a record of the stored type and the filter state main's scan keeps, part is load; they were not separated. The second figure is a range, not a distribution. |
| **start time at 10M** | **not measured.** The scan is one pass over the retained events at about 0.75 to 0.95 microseconds an event (1,000,000 events in 0.72 to 0.77 s before the merge, 0.89 to 0.95 s after), so 10,000,000 retained events would start in about 7.5 to 9.5 s plus the replay: an extrapolation, linear by construction, not a measurement. Retention bounds it: R is the events inside the retention, and with a month of events at 100 a second R is 260 million; the retention is the knob. |
| **a crash during compaction loses nothing** | `kill -9` as a power cut at each of the **19 steps** (a roll is 1 to 6, a drop 7 to 11, the snapshot of the outcomes log 12 to 19), by the loop and by `--compact-now`, with every event final and with events that are not (a dead endpoint pins them: steps 7 to 11 do not happen there): **66 kills, 68 checks, all passing**. The kill is made by the service itself (`compact-kill-at` writes `killpoint` and waits), so the test waits for the **observed** step and not a time. After each: nothing lost (every event not legitimately dropped is there, byte for byte, read with the independent reader of `tests/chaos.py`), nothing sent again that was final, ids go on, the state looked at on a service that does nothing to it, then finished by `--compact-now` twice, and a second restart changes nothing. |
| **an event pinned by a paused endpoint, a disabled endpoint, a waiting replay** | one test each (`pins`): 1,500 events, retention passed three times over, 3 segments, **none dropped**, across a snapshot, a power cut and a restart; when the pin goes (the endpoint enabled and caught up, the replay delivered) the segments go. A fourth, `dormant`: an endpoint whose row is gone does not hold the log back, and when it returns after its events were dropped it starts at the oldest retained and says so. |
| **keys expire as designed** | `keys`: a key inside the window answers the first event's id and stores nothing, **across a compaction, a power cut and a restart**; after the window the same key and the same event are a **new event** (documented in README and `docs/retention.md`); a key leaves the index when its window has passed or its event is dropped; a key that expired while the service was down is not read back; `--idem-keys 200`: the 200 are taken and the 201st is a `507`, and room comes back with the window; **66,000 keys** (past the old 65,536) are held, restart rebuilds them in 0.06 to 0.16 s. A client key beginning `cron:` is a `400` (the schedules' own). |
| **ids are never reused** | `bounded`, `compactnow`: after every event is dropped the next is the next number, across a power cut and a restart (the active segment's header names its first id). |
| **a format version in the first record of each file; an unknown one refused** | `formats`: a new `events.seg` and `delivery.seg` begin with a header; version 3 is a refusal, status 40 (events) or 41 (outcomes), a line on stderr, **and the directory is byte for byte as it was**; a record that is not this program's header, a hole in the segments, a segment that does not begin where the one before ends, a manifest that names a missing file: 42 (and an `events.seg` that is there and cannot be opened is still 10); a format 1 directory is read as it was, nothing is rewritten at the start, `--compact-now` upgrades it. The header is shaped so that the previous build refuses it (status 16 and 15) instead of misreading it. |
| **the stall is bounded and stated** | The largest snapshot there can be (62 endpoints, 63,488 records, 4.9 MB, one turn): the loop was held **34 to 70 ms** (`maintenance_ms_max`; six runs of the `stall` stage before the merge; **32 ms** in the run on the merged build, where a request waited 33 ms at most), the slowest request beside it took 63 to 97 ms and p99 of 5 to 15 ms, and the snapshot is exactly the 63,613 records it should be (the test counts them). Before the fix of 38.5 it was 483 ms, and once, on a loaded machine, 960 ms. The gate is 300 ms. Every other step is a few milliseconds: over 100 rolls, 100 drops and 45 snapshots of the ten-million run the longest was 68 ms (30 ms over 24 rolls, 24 drops and 13 snapshots of the merged build's run). |
| **backup and restore under the new layout** | `tests/backup_test.py`: stages A to C unchanged and passing, **D** (online backups while segments are dropped and the outcomes log replaced, 43 `kill -9`) and **E** (the lock, and a directory of the previous format) new. Every backup holds every acknowledged event or one that was dropped after being final at both endpoints; restored samples (including one taken after every event had been dropped) serve what is retained, `404` what is not, and deliver each retained event not final in the backup once and never again one that was. |
| **the whole suite** | Every test step of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (as merged with main's work up to `4ee5543`) passes on this build, with the compiler at the pin (`4c27593`) and the database `hooks_g`, as root: fmt, build, the authority report, the 13 sets / 150 unit tests, cron, sign, config, authz, history, roster, production, layout, manage, slots, patch, delete, filter, rotation, headers, saturation, ssrf, replay, gone, attempt, retry, isolation, scan, breaker, chaos, backup (73 checks), **retention (160 checks, 333 s)**, shellcheck, delivery, idempotency, schedules, stop, corrupt (113 checks), metrics, ready, reason. A second run as the unprivileged user `nobody` (the tests that touch the file system, the permissions and the lock: config, layout, chaos, stop, idempotency, delivery, production, backup, corrupt, metrics, ready, retention) passes too, and found one defect in this work, fixed (38.7). Not run: the `Keep the service binary` step (an upload) and the Docker build. |


### 38.2 What the measurements said

* **The scan is cheap and the replay is not free.** 1,000,000 retained events (241 MB, 8 segments) start in 0.69 to 0.77 s with no endpoint; with one endpoint that has been sent all of them and an outcomes log of 9.9 MB (128,460 records) the start is 1.5 to 2.1 s (three separate runs, 1.50 to 2.11 s, on a machine others were using), so a record of the outcomes log costs 6 to 8 microseconds to replay and an event 0.75: the outcomes log, bounded by `delivery-log-bytes` (32 MiB, about 435,000 records at the default, 2.6 to 3.5 s), is the larger term at the default, and the one an operator can shorten.
* **Memory.** On the compiler this work was written against, the idempotency index at 262,144 keys cost 38 MB of resident memory (176 MB in all against the previous build's 138 MB) and one million keys cost 153 MB (291 MB in all): `box_slice` was resident from the moment it was made (`docs/lexsys-log-retention.md` gap 4), and the default was set to 262,144, not the 1,048,576 the design started with, for that reason. **The compiler at the pin (`4c27593`) allocates a large `box_slice` lazily and the gap is closed:** the service is **2.5 MB resident right after `listening` whatever `idem-keys` is** (16, 262,144, 1,048,576 and 4,194,304 measured) and holds 200,000 distinct keys in 30 MB (21.5 MB after the first 50,000, which touch the hash arrays at random, then 3 MB per 50,000). The default (262,144) and the limit (16 to 4,194,304) are unchanged; they are no longer a memory decision.
* **The snapshot is not a slow step any more.** The largest snapshot there can be (62 endpoints with full windows, 63,488 records, 4.9 MB, built in one turn) held the loop for **0.48 s** on its first measurement and **0.96 s** once on a loaded machine, and the design had no answer but to state it. It was one function losing its memory on every call (38.5): with that fixed the same state holds the loop for **34 to 70 ms**, and `--compact-now` on it takes 0.42 to 0.50 s including the start and the replay of 63,488 records. The stall gate is 300 ms.
* **A segment is not dropped for the age of its newest event** but for the time the next one was created (no event in the sealed segment is younger), so at the real default a segment of 64 MiB leaves up to retention + the time it takes to fill one: with the default 30 days and a segment that takes a day to fill, 31 days of events are on disk. The disk bound is `retention + one segment + the pinned events + delivery-log-bytes`.
* **What an operator sees:** `GET /stats` now says what retention did (`events_first_id`, `events_last_id`, `events_segments`, `events_bytes`, `segments_dropped`, `segments_sealed`, `snapshots`, `delivery_bytes`, `maintenance_ms_max`, `maintenance_errors`, `maintenance_lock_skips`, `events_skipped`, `cron_keys`), and the start says on stderr what it repaired (a roll or a drop a crash cut) and which endpoints missed events that were dropped.


### 38.3 Tests

`tests/retention_test.py`: 160 checks in ten stages (`bounded`, `pins`, `dormant`, `snapshot`, `formats`, `keys`, `killmatrix`, `compactnow`, `stall`, `memory`), 6 minutes; `STAGES=` picks some and `QUICK=1` runs four of the 19 steps. `tests/retain_test.ls` (the rules of retention, the header of a segment, the names of segments: pure, each at its edge), `tests/idem_test.ls` (the two rings, backward-shift deletion, eviction, the floor), `tests/state_test.ls`, `tests/config_test.*`, `tests/backup_test.py` (stages D and E), and the existing suite: every step of `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, plus the new step for `retention_test.py`. Unit tests are in `lex-sys.toml`: `lex-sys test` runs 13 sets, 150 tests, on the merged build.

**Races found by running the suite on a machine that other work was using, all in tests and all fixed:** `tests/retry_test.py` stage 3 killed the service "once the failure is durable" but counted the failure-streak record written beside the first failure as an outcome, so the second attempt was killed before it was recorded and retried at the restart (the gap 0.148 s, not 1.0 s); it passed on this branch and on the previous build when the machine was quiet. In `tests/retention_test.py` itself: the replay stage posted with 8 threads, so event 3 was not `n = 3` and the receiver's answer went by the wrong number; the `keys` stage posted `{"type":"t"}` and compared with `{"type": "t"}`; a kill-matrix case waited 60 s for a flaky receiver to catch up and needed more under load; the stall bound was first written for a leak (38.5). `backup.sh` had two races of its own that stage D found at once: it read `events.first` before taking the lock (a drop in between left it with no first segment, or made it exit 2 as if the directory were wrong), and `logcheck.py` called a tail damage when a power cut had spoiled the ends of two small records.

**What the tests do not pin** (said so that nobody thinks they do): the cron-in-flight guard of `rt_maintain` (`sched.tick_state(sg) == 0`) has no test that races a fire against a drop, and so a mutant that removes it survives; the flush of the outcomes before a drop (step 7's precondition) is not observable by a `kill -9` test, because a `fsync` the shim honours only matters at a power cut that lands between two steps, which the matrix does not make; a real power cut was not made (the shim stands in for it); the first compaction of a very large old directory (a year of history) was not made, only reasoned about.


### 38.4 Mutants

*Made on the build before the merge with main (the unit tests and the stages named are those of this work; the merge changed the code around them but not these functions, and the stages pass on the merged build; the mutants were not run again).* 34 mutants of the new code and scripts, each applied alone to a saved copy of its file, built, run against the unit tests and then the stage of `tests/retention_test.py` or `tests/backup_test.py` that should catch it, and restored and `cmp`-checked (none was ever left on disk): **30 killed, 4 survive.** *Killed by the unit tests:* `may_drop` one above the floor, the age ignored, retention `0` not keeping everything; `should_roll` at `>` instead of `>=`, and for an empty segment; `snapshot_due` at 2x instead of 4x; `age_needed` ignoring the window; the events header accepting a newer format, and a first event with id 1 not read as format 1; the eviction of a key at the floor, past its window, and past its budget; the lower limit of `segment-bytes`. *Killed by the retention test:* a waiting replay not pinning; slot 0 ignored by the floor; a paused endpoint written into the snapshot as merely disabled; the last cell of a window left out of the snapshot; a failure streak left out; the outcomes header accepting format 3; skipped events not counted; the base of a new segment wrong (the chain is refused at the restart); the wrong segment deleted; the snapshot, and `events.first`, not `fsync`ed before their rename (the kill matrix at steps 10, 11 and 17 to 19; the second needed the shim to learn about `events.first`); and three regressions of the leak of 38.5 (`request_for`, `put_outcome`, `flush` leaving their region by `return`: the `memory` stage). *Killed by the backup test:* a `logcheck.py` that calls a tail with no valid record after it damage; a backup that does not take the lock; a restore that does not restore `events.first`. **The four survivors, and why each is an equivalent mutant and not a gap I could close:** (1) the loop's drop not moving the floor of the key index: a segment is dropped only when older than `max(retention, window)`, so the keys of its events have left by age already, and the floor is belt and braces; (2) the roll not `fsync`ing the new segment's header: the first flush of an event `fsync`s the same file, and a header that never became durable in a file with no event is a roll the start deletes; (3) the `slot_known` guard on the replay of an outcome: it can only matter for an outcome of a slot that has no `created`, which I could not make a log this program writes contain, and the slot is reset by its `created` if it ever comes; (4) `store.seg_path` leaving its region by `return`: measured not to leak (the `memory` stage passes with it), which is itself a finding about the compiler fault: it does not lose the memory in every function. Several first versions were wrong, not the tests: they did not build, or changed a value the code does not compare (the failure streak is a time, so `> 100000` was true); and two survived until a test they needed existed (the record count of the largest snapshot; an `events.first` that the shim could cut). The mutants are scratch work and, as in the earlier sections, are not committed; each is a one-line change that this paragraph describes.

### 38.5 A bug in the service found on the way, and fixed (it was not retention's)

**What happened.** The first ten-million-event run had to be killed: the service's resident memory grew about 70 MB a second and was 2.8 GB after 300,000 events. Two measurements separated the causes. **Ingest alone** grew by 36 MB per 100,000 events on this branch and not at all on the build before it (134 MB flat): that was this work's own draft. **Delivery** grew by about 11 KB per delivery attempt **on the build before retention too**: 100,000 deliveries took the process from 2 MB to 1,075 MB resident. So a service delivering a million events a day to one endpoint would have been 11 GB larger every day, and at the measured 7 to 10 thousand deliveries a second it would have been out of memory within minutes; the 24-hour soak that `docs/runbook.md` says was never run would have found it.

**The cause** is a compiler fault, not a mistake of logic: **a region that is left by a `return` is not given back** (one left by falling out of its last statement is). `gdb` on `malloc` during a run showed 64 KiB allocations from `evlog.flush`, `store.seg_path`, `state.put_outcome` and `hooks.request_for`, each in a function whose `region { ... return x; }` returned from inside. `docs/lexsys-log-retention.md` gap 6 has a 40-line reproducer (396 MB after 100,000 calls, against 9 MB for the same function written to fall out of its region).

**The fix** is to leave every region by falling out of it, in the four functions (`state.put_outcome` and `hooks.request_for` are older than this work; the others were written for it) and in the functions of this work that a roll, a drop or a snapshot call. After it: 100,000 deliveries take the process from 2 MB to **172 MB, flat** (the previous build, 1,075 MB), 10,000,000 events and deliveries in 172 to 182 MB (both on the compiler before `4c27593`; on it the same flat line is 3.4 MB, the lazy `box_slice` having taken the rest), and `tests/retention_test.py` has a `memory` stage that posts and delivers 100,000 events and requires the resident size not to move (3.4 MB and 3.4 MB after 60,000 more; on the earlier compiler 173 MB and 173 MB, and on the previous build 144 MB and 417 MB). As a side effect the snapshot stall fell by an order of magnitude (38.2), because a snapshot is 63,488 calls of `put_outcome`.

**The same fault came back with the merge, and the `memory` stage found it at once:** main's `wire.request` (the HTTP client of `epx`/`ops`) left its region by a `return` and so did `ops.probe_write`; with the merge the first full run showed 963 MB then 1,323 MB resident in the stage (a leak per delivery, because the delivery goes through `wire.request`). Both leave their regions by falling out now. This is the argument for keeping the stage in the suite: a leak is not a thing a reviewer sees.

**Mended later** (the soak's finding 3, `docs/soak.md`): `finish_create`, `finish_patch`, `fill_change`, `change_request`, `dead_replay`, `manage.make_secret`, `sched.request_for`, `cron.parse` (every schedule a fire reads), the early exits of `fire_cron`, `epx.set_spec`, `hdrs.check_spec`, `hdrs.count_of` and `endpoints.parse` leave their regions by falling out of them now: a function whose region returned from several places has its body in a second function that is given the room it works in. Measured with `tests/leak_test.py` (two rounds, the second may keep at most 1 KiB a call): on the build before, a `PATCH` kept 18 to 36 KB, a create and a delete 28 KB, a bulk replay of dead letters 9 KB, a schedule's create and delete 15 KB, a fire 13 KB; after it, 0 to 102 bytes for each, and 68 bytes a fire. What the soak saw as memory growing with keyed events is not a leak: the hash slots of an idempotency index (2 x `idem-keys` + 1 integers, 4 MiB at the default) are resident a page at a time, each new key landing on a page of its own until all are, and then a key costs about 104 bytes up to the index's size (30 MB for 200,000 keys, as measured in section 38). **Still losing 64 KiB a call, once a start:** `evlog.open`, the park of `evlog.step`, `hooks.open_log`, `read_endpoints_file`, `load_config`, `start_tls` and the two functions of `logguard` (a start and `--compact-now`).

**What still loses 64 KiB a call** (as it was written before the mending above), none on the path of an event or a delivery: `evlog.open` (once per start) and the park of `evlog.step` (never returns); `hooks.open_log`, `read_endpoints_file`, `load_config` (once per start); `finish_create`, `finish_patch`, `manage.make_secret` and `sched.request_for` (once per administrative request or schedule statement); the two early exits of `fire_cron` (a key that is already there, an index that is full). They are listed so that the next person need not find them again, and are worth fixing as the compiler is not.


**A full disk and delivery (the soak's finding 2, `docs/soak.md`).** With the data directory full the outcomes log breaks (a write fails, and by design it is not retried); an attempt could then not be recorded, so it counted for nothing and was due again at once: the same events were sent as fast as the receiver answered (`tests/fulldisk_test.py` on the build before: 7,565 deliveries for 218 events in four seconds, one event 925 times). `start_attempts` now starts nothing while the outcomes log is broken, as it starts nothing while the service is stopping. A broken log is cleared only by a restart (`/readyz` says so), and the restart sends once more what was not recorded: on this build 211 deliveries for 211 events while full, at most two of an event in all after the disk is freed and the service restarted.

### 38.6 Not verified, and open

* **Not verified:** a real power cut (the `fsync` shim stands in: it cuts each `.seg` file and `events.first` to what its last `fsync` covered plus a random part of the rest, and zeroes a random tail); a real month (the retention is 20 s in the bench and 1.2 to 3 s in the tests: the code takes the same path, the arithmetic of a month was not run); ten million **retained** events (the start at that size is extrapolated); the first compaction of a directory with a year of history (one snapshot of at most 5 MB, but the events it would drop at once are many segments, one drop a turn, each with two `fsync`s: not made); two services on one directory (nothing prevents it, as before: `compact.lock` is a lock against a backup, not against a second service); a file system without `rename` atomicity or without directory `fsync`; the Dockerfile and the unit file with a retention setting (the settings are the same keys as before; the image was not rebuilt here).
* **Open, and the cost of each:** (1) the first compaction of a very large old directory is a long burst of drops; `--compact-now` run stopped is the way to take it at a chosen time, and it is documented, not made gentler. (2) A dormant endpoint's waiting replay cannot pin its event (nothing in the table says the replay exists); it is skipped at the start and counted in `events_skipped`. (3) `GET /events/:id` of a dropped event is a `404` with no tombstone: "dropped" and "never existed" read alike. (4) `attempts` in PostgreSQL is not pruned (it grows a row per attempt). (5) The segment table is 4,096 segments: at 64 MiB that is 256 GiB retained, beyond which a roll is refused and counted. (6) `lexsys-log` still has no version in its own header (a proposal). (7) Six lex-sys gaps are listed in `docs/lexsys-log-retention.md`; the one that matters is gap 6.
* **Conflicts with the work that went to main in the meantime** (`4ee5543`, health/stop/corrupt-log/signals/tokens/filter/headers/reason), and how they were resolved: this section is **38** because main has 30 to 36 and 37 is taken by the reconnecting pool. `src/hooks.ls` (the log handle is `evlog.Ev` through `run`, `prepare`, `main` and `handle`; main's `signals_watch`, readiness, metrics, `filter` and `epx` code is kept and talks to the log through `evlog`; `open_tmp_log` is the one place that opens a log of its own, for the compaction's temporary file), `src/config.ls` (both sides: cfg[40..46] are retention's, size 48), `src/state.ls` (**the header record of the outcomes log is kind 15, because kind 14 is main's reason of a failed attempt**), `src/logguard.ls` (main's preflight, `--repair-logs` and the pair check, made to work on the chain: the preflight judges the **last segment** of the events log, since a sealed one cannot end in a torn tail and the start checks that each ends where the next begins; the pair check is deferred until the log is open when the last segment holds no event; the header record is not counted as a record in what it reports), `scripts/logcheck.py` (main's `classify_tail`, `record_size` and the report, with the chain and the header: ids strictly increase within a log, a record that goes backwards is where it stops), `lex-sys.toml` (pin `4c27593`), `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (one step) and `tests/chaos.py` (`read_log` skips headers). **Statuses**: retention's are **40** (a format of the events log that is not known), **41** (the same for the outcomes log), **42** (a hole or a break in the chain of segments, a manifest that names a missing segment, a record that is not the header), **43** (`--compact-now` found the lock held) and **44** (`--compact-now`: a step failed); main's 10 to 20, 30 to 35 (production) and 18 and 19 (the pair, damage) are unchanged. **The authority report** (`scripts/check-authority.sh`, `docs/authority.json`) is unchanged by this work: retention adds no foreign authority (the segments are files in the one directory the service already holds), so the report was not regenerated.


### 38.7 Found by running the suite as an unprivileged user

CI runs as root, and `tests/corrupt_test.py` skips the case that needs a file the user cannot read (root reads anything). Run as `nobody`, case A7 failed: an `events.seg` with mode 000 made the start answer **42** (a hole in the chain) where the service had always answered **10** (a file that cannot be opened). `evlog.open` took any negative size of the first segment but "not there" for a broken chain. It answers 10 for anything but "not there" now. The same twelve test programs that touch the file system and the lock (config, layout, chaos, stop, idempotency, delivery, production, backup, corrupt, metrics, ready, retention) pass as `nobody` on the final binary. What this does not cover: CI does not run as an unprivileged user, so A7 is still skipped there.

## 39. Dead letters you can list and replay in bulk, cancelling a replay, retry jitter, concurrency and rate per endpoint (`docs/production.md` P1.2 and P1.4, and the "no way to cancel a waiting replay" of section 23)

*Written with the code, like sections 20, 22, 31 and 35: the rules were taken from production.md and the task, the gates were written with the tests, and the mutants were run once the tests passed. Where a decision was taken so that work could start it says so.*

### 39.1 Dead letters

**What is one.** An event is a *dead letter* at an endpoint when the last word about it there is `dead` (record kind 3: the schedule ran out, or the receiver answered `410`) or a replay's `replay_dead` (9), and no `replay_delivered` (8) has come since. The truth is `delivery.seg`; finding them there means reading the whole log, so a request cannot ask it.

**Where they are kept.** `src/dead.ls`: a table per endpoint slot of the `cap()` = 2,048 dead letters with the **largest event ids**, sorted by event id, four integers an entry (the event id, `attempts * 65536 + reason`, when it died, and where the event starts in the events log plus one). 62 x (4 + 2,048 x 4) integers: 508,152, 4,065,216 bytes, touched only where an endpoint has dead letters. A `put` is a binary search and a shift (at most 2,048 entries of 4 integers), a page is a binary search and a walk, and nothing reads a log. **Why 2,048:** an endpoint has at most one window of 1,024 events not final, a window's worth dies per schedule (about 76 hours with the default one), and the breaker pauses an endpoint after 5 days: about a window and a half per outage. The table is built by the same replay of the log as the rest of the state (`dead_note`, called from `replay` for kinds 3, 8, 9, 14 and 17), and kept by `finish_attempt` and `finish_replay` while the service runs.

**What the record had to gain: when it died.** A dead record's fifth field (`next_at`) was 0. It is now the Unix ms of the death (a replay's `replay_dead` too). It is harmless to everything that reads it: `state.apply` stores it in a cell that is final, and the previous binary ignores it. A log from before has 0, and the list says `died_at: 0`. The reason is the kind-14 record right behind the outcome (section 34.3): recovery takes it for the entry that has none, so a later failure of a replay is not mistaken for it. At run time the entry gets the reason directly.

**The bound is not a loss, and a plain replay of the log cannot honour that.** An entry that does not fit is dropped (the smallest id goes, or the new one is not entered if it is smaller than all), and the largest id so dropped is the table's **floor**: every dead letter above it is in the table. `GET /endpoints/:id/dead` says `truncated` and `complete_above`. The first version dropped the old ones while newer ones were there and counted them, and a restart *rebuilt the same table*: after replaying the newest 2,048 of 2,200 the 152 oldest were still dead in the log and could be neither listed nor replayed, because the fold that builds the table is lossy (a delivered replay removes an entry the fold had already evicted for room). The test of stage 7 found it. The fix: when the floor is above 0 and the table has room (`dead.wants_refold`), the log is read once more for the events at or below the floor (`dead_fold`, `dead_complete`), repeated while the floor falls; it runs when the endpoints have been read (`settle_endpoints`, section 37.2: at the start with no database, else when the database first answers) and, with the loop paused for the length of the read, the first time a list or a bulk replay finds the condition. **Cost:** one read of `delivery.seg` per round, at about the cost of the start's own replay of it (8 us a record: the start of the 6.2 MB, 130,000-record log of the measurement below takes 1.0 s; the fold does less for each record and was not timed alone). It happens only for an endpoint with more than 2,048 dead letters, once room appears, and once more at each start for such an endpoint.

**Retention (section 38) and the table.** Three rules, taken when this section was put on top of retention. **(1) A dead letter expires with its event.** Retention deletes an event only once it is final at every endpoint, dead letters included, and after that `GET /events/:id` and its replay are `404`; a dead letter whose event is gone could be listed but never replayed, so its entry goes with the event (`dead.expire`, called for every slot after each drop, before each snapshot, at the start and by the two routes: an entry below the first event that is left, and a floor below it). With the default `retention-days` of 30 a dead letter is listable for 30 days after its event arrived, at the least. **(2) The snapshot keeps the table.** `delivery.seg` is replaced by the shortest log that replays to the same state, and the snapshot used to write each final cell of a window as a `dead` record (final is all a window needs); a replay of it would have made every delivered event a dead letter. It writes them as `delivered` now, and the dead letters as **kind 17 `dead_entry`**: one for each entry of the table (the endpoint, the event, `attempts * 65536 + reason + 1`, when it died) and, if the table has a floor, one with `attempts` 0 whose event is the floor. Recovery puts each entry back and raises the floor; the fold of `dead_complete` takes them like any other record. What the table left out for room (the dead letters at or below the floor) is in the old log only, so **a snapshot makes the bound real**: after it those are neither listed nor replayed by this route (the event can still be replayed by id while retention keeps it), and the list keeps saying `truncated`. `dead_complete` marks a table `settled` once a fold below the floor found nothing to add (after a snapshot it never does), and does not read the log for it again until another dead letter is left out. **(3) Nothing is completed or listed before the endpoints are known** (section 37.2): the completion is part of `settle_endpoints`, which runs when the table has been read, and the four routes answer `503` until then like the other routes that name an endpoint; retention itself drops nothing and snapshots nothing before it (`rt_floor`), so no event whose dead letter is not yet known can go. The largest snapshot there can be grew with the entries (62 endpoints x 2,048 dead letters: 127,000 records, 9.8 MB more, `rt_snapshot_room` 16 MiB); its stall is in 39.8.

**Where an event's type comes from.** A list entry has the event's type, which is in the events log, not in `delivery.seg`. Entries made while the service runs know where their event starts (the endpoint's ring of offsets); entries built from the log do not (-1). The first list page, or bulk replay with `types`, that needs a place for an entry finds all of them in one merged pass over the events log from the last known place (`resolve_offsets`); after that a page reads at most `limit` records. **Cost:** 7.7 us for each event of the events log walked (155 ms for 20,000 events, 1.5 MB), from the last place known, with the loop paused: a million events is about 8 s, once after a start, by the first list or typed bulk replay that needs it (the start itself pays nothing for it).

**The list.** `GET /endpoints/:id/dead?limit=&order=&after=` (read scope). The cursor is an event id and the table is sorted by it, so a page is stable while entries come and go: a test pages through 40 entries in sevens while three more die and sees no event twice and none missing. `next` is the id to pass as `after`, `null` at the end. A page is built from at most `limit` (1,000) entries and as many reads of the events log. **Cost:** 6.0 ms for a page of 1,000 entries (1,000 reads of the events log) and 0.2 ms for 10, measured with 2,200 dead letters; the work is proportional to `limit`, not to the table or the log.

**Bulk replay.** `POST /endpoints/:id/replay-dead`, admin scope, body optional: `limit`, `types`, `after`, and nothing else (a misspelt member is a `400`: `{"limt": 5}` would otherwise replay everything). It walks the table **oldest first** past `after`, skips an entry that has a replay waiting (`replaying`), skips one whose type the patterns do not match, takes the first `room` of the rest and counts the others (`remaining`). The decision about the bound of 32 (section 23): **a call takes at most as many as the table of waiting replays has room for and no more than `limit`, writes one `replay` record for each, flushes once, and only then puts them in the table; it answers `taken`, `remaining`, `waiting` and `next` (the last id taken). Nothing is dropped and nothing is refused for being too many**: the caller calls again. `202` when something was taken, `200` when nothing was (including "nothing to do" and "the table is full"); a loop that goes on until `remaining` is 0 ends, because a dead letter that is replaying is not counted again. A replay that dies again is a dead letter again (new attempts, time and reason) and *is* counted by the next call: a caller that must not go round again passes `after` (the `next` of the last call). The replay machinery is the one of section 23, unchanged: same `webhook-id`, same schedule, a `410` disables the endpoint.

**What it does not do.** A dead letter older than the 2,048 is listed and replayed only after room appears (above). No filter but the type. No "replay everything that died between two times". The list is not in the history database (it is a view of the log).

### 39.2 Cancelling a waiting replay

`DELETE /events/:id/replay/:endpoint` and `DELETE /endpoints/:id/replays` (admin). A new record kind, **16 `replay_cancelled`** (15 is retention's header of the log) (endpoint, event), written and **flushed before the answer**, then the entry leaves the table; recovery ends the replay without an outcome, as `replay_dead` does, and the event stays what it was. So a cancel is durable (killed right after the answer, and cut to what the last `fsync` covered: `tests/cancel_test.py` stages 3 and 4) and exact (a cancel that was not acknowledged is not promised; one that was is never undone). **A replay with an attempt on the wire is not cancelled**: its outcome must be recorded and a new request for the same pair could meet the old attempt's answer, so the single route is a `409` and the bulk one counts it in `busy`; the caller asks again within `deadline-ms`. Cancelling does not recall a request that has been sent. A previous binary refuses a log with a kind-16 record (status 15; `state.outcome_at` of that binary knows kinds 1 to 15), like every kind added since section 22.

### 39.3 Retry jitter

**Decision (taken on the owner's behalf, reversible): `retry-jitter` is a percent, 0 to 50, default 10, symmetric.** Each delay of the schedule is `base + d` with `d` uniform in `[-base * p / 100, +base * p / 100]` (`src/jitter.ls`). Why symmetric and not "full jitter" (a delay uniform from 0 to the base): the published schedule (5 s, 5 min, ...) stays what a receiver's operator reads in the documentation, a retry is never sooner than 90 percent of what was promised, and the total time to a dead letter changes by at most 10 percent. Why 10 and not 20 or 25: 10 spreads events that failed in one instant over a fifth of the delay (a minute of failures retries over a minute at the 5-minute step and over 4 hours at the 24-hour one), which is enough to take a thundering herd apart, and it moves nothing a person sets a watch by. Why on by default: a retry storm is what a service of this kind is for avoiding, and an option nobody turns on does not avoid it; the cost is that a test of the schedule's exact gaps passes `--retry-jitter 0` (`tests/retry_test.py` does, and nothing else in the suite measures a gap). `0` is exactly the schedule: `jitter.delay` returns `base` before it computes anything.

**A function of (endpoint, event, attempt) and of nothing else.** No clock, no random source, no state: a 31-bit mix (Lehmer multipliers modulo 2^31 - 1, three rounds with a shift-xor between, every product under 2^47 so nothing overflows) of the endpoint's id, the event's id and the attempt's number, scaled onto the `2s + 1` values (scaling, not a remainder, so a wide span is not biased toward its low end). The same triple always gets the same delay; two services given the same endpoint id and events agree; another endpoint gets other delays (`tests/jitter_test.py` 3). **The time of the next attempt was already written with the outcome** (`failed`'s fifth field, section 15), so the jitter is applied where that time is computed, in `finish_attempt` and `finish_replay`, and **nothing is recomputed at a restart**: a retry waiting through a `kill -9` keeps the time it was given, even if the restart is with `--retry-jitter 0` (stage 5: 20 events, delays of 4,200 to 7,800 ms, two kills, the second restart with 0: the records are byte for byte what they were and every second attempt arrives at its recorded time). A replay's retries are moved the same way. The number of attempts is the schedule's, always.

### 39.4 Concurrency and rate per endpoint

**Settings and overrides.** `endpoint-concurrency` (1 to 8, default 8: the constant `per_endpoint()` it replaces) and `endpoint-rate` (attempts started a second, 0 to 100,000, default 0 = no limit) for every endpoint; an endpoint's own `"concurrency"` and `"rate"` (`POST` and `PATCH /endpoints`: an integer in range, or `0`/`null` to follow the setting again; `endpoints.conf` words `concurrency=` and `rate=`, which must be in range; two columns `concurrency int not null default 0 check (between 0 and 8)` and `rate int not null default 0 check (between 0 and 100000)` added by `alter table ... add column if not exists`, so `psql -f sql/schema.sql` upgrades an old table: tested against a table made by the old definition, in a schema of its own, with rows in it). The values are two integers at the end of the endpoint's row in `epx.ls` (stride 2,660 to 2,664), shifted by a delete like the rest, and two members of the change that waits for the database (`xg[4]`, `xg[5]`; masks 8 and 16). `GET /endpoints` shows the limits **in force** (the endpoint's own or the service's). 0 cannot mean "unlimited for this endpoint under a limit for the rest": use 100,000. The ceiling of 8 in flight is the attempt machinery's (64 connections, the per-slot flags), not a policy.

**The rate limit is a token bucket in the loop (`src/lim.ls`).** Tokens in thousandths, so a rate of 3 a second is exact in integers (elapsed ms x rate); an attempt costs 1,000; the bucket is `max(1, ceil(rate / 10))` tokens deep and starts full. The depth is a tenth of a second because the loop sleeps at most 50 ms: a bucket one token deep at 100 a second would be refilled in 10 ms and looked at every 50, and the limit would be undershot five-fold. It is the **monotonic clock the loop already reads** (`clock_ms`), called once a turn. `start_attempts` asks `admit` just before it starts an attempt of an endpoint's window (and of a replay); when the bucket is empty it **stops looking at that endpoint's window for the turn and goes on to the next endpoint**, so a limited endpoint neither stalls the loop nor the others (stage 4: beside an endpoint at 2 a second, the unlimited one is sent 300 events in 0.4 s). `admit` also notes the time until the soonest token, and `run` passes the shortest of them (never more than 50 ms, never less than 1) to `server.wait`: the limit is reached and not undershot (stage 3: 60 events at 10 a second, 59 intervals of 100 ms, took 5.9 s within 15 percent).

**A throttled event is not a failure.** No attempt is made, no record is written, the retry counter does not move, the event stays in its window (the cursor does not pass it, as for any event that waits) and is looked at again on the next turn: the at-least-once contract is what it was. `hooks_endpoint_throttled_total{endpoint}` counts the attempts that were held back: the first refusal after an admitted attempt counts once however many turns it waits. `hooks_endpoint_dead_letters{endpoint}` is the second series added to the endpoint's nine.

**The bucket is memory.** A restart starts every bucket full (the log records when an attempt *ended*, not when it began), so a limit can be exceeded by one bucket across a restart; stage 7 (six `kill -9` while 120 events are held back at 20 a second) measures a second of arrivals within the rate plus a bucket for each restart in it.

### 39.5 Memory and records

* The delivery state grows by `lim.size()` = 318 integers and `dead.size()` = 508,152 integers (4.07 MB, lazily touched), and each of the 62 rows of `epx` by 4 (stride 2,664). The block of `lim` and the table of dead letters are the last regions of the state (`off_lim()`, `off_dead()`); `tests/layout_test.py` passes.
* `delivery.seg`: two new kinds (16, a cancelled replay; 17, a dead letter of a snapshot, 39.1; retention's header took 15), and the fifth field of kinds 3 and 9 carries a time. `state.outcome_at` accepts kinds 1 to 17, and `scripts/logcheck.py` too. A log written by this version is refused by the previous binary (status 15, nothing lost) once a cancel has been recorded or the outcomes log has been replaced by a snapshot that holds a dead letter (kind 17); the dead records alone are read by it.
* `sql/schema.sql`: two columns of `endpoints`; `queries.ls` regenerated by `pgen` against a database that has them (the diff against the committed file is the added accessors and parameters only).

### 39.6 Tests

**Unit tests** (`lex-sys test`, 18 sets, 197 tests; new: `jitter` 8, `dead` 10, `lim` 11, `bulk` 4, and `epx` +2, `endpoints` +2, `config` +2 (the three settings, and that no two settings share an index), `state` kinds 16 and 17): the bounds, mean and spread of the jitter over the whole schedule and a short delay's two ends, the same delay for the same triple, a different one for another endpoint or attempt, 0 exactly the schedule; the table of dead letters in order, find, below/upto, an entry that dies again, remove, the reason only for its attempt, a full table keeping the newest and its floor, `wants_refold`, `clear_below`, and **300 inserts into a full table at every position checked for order, duplicates and the entry dropped**; the token bucket (the first attempt at once, refilled at the rate, a bucket a tenth of a second deep, an hour idle buys one burst, 20 s at 30 a second is 600 attempts plus a bucket and no second holds more than rate + depth, buckets apart, the held-back count, the sleep shortened to the soonest token, a rate changed while the bucket is half empty); the limits in an `epx` row, shifted by a delete; the request naming them or refusing each wrong value; the words of an `endpoints.conf` line; the page of the list and the body of the bulk replay with each refusal's code.

**Harnesses** (new; they share `tests/endpoint_kit.py`; each but `jitter_test.py` needs the database and empties `endpoints` and `attempts`):

* `tests/dead_test.py` (99 checks, 50 to 60 s): the list of 40 dead letters (type, attempts, reason, time of death between the first post and the last wait) against what the test made fail and against **the delivery log read apart from the service** (`log_dead`: its own fold of kinds 3, 8, 9, 10, 11 and 14); the healthy endpoint has none; pages of 7 in both orders put together are the whole list with none twice, `after` strictly past its event, a cursor that holds while three more die, every refusal (a limit of 0, 1001, `x`, empty, negative; an order; an after) a 400, an unknown endpoint a 404; **`kill -9` at three points while 300 events are dying: the list is exactly the 300, 2 attempts each, with their types and reasons, equal to the log, and the same after one more kill**; a `410` is a dead letter at once with the reason `gone`; **bulk replay of 100 dead letters** with the receiver mended: `limit` 10 takes 10, a second call never takes the same ones, a loop to `remaining` 0 takes all 100 exactly once, in calls of at most 32 with `waiting` never above 32, each sent once, the list and the log empty; `types`, `after` and `limit` together; an empty `types` is every type; every wrong body (13) a 400; a replay answered `410` is a dead letter again with 1 attempt, the reason `gone` and a later time; with the long schedule 5 replays wait and say `replaying`, the next call takes the other 7 and the one after takes nothing and says nothing remains; **`kill -9` six times in the middle of a bulk replay of 200: all 200 are sent, none more than once beyond what was in flight**; **the table of 2,048**: 2,200 dead, the newest 2,048 listed, `complete_above` 152, the same after a kill and a restart, the replay of what is above 152 sends 2,048 events once each, the next look at the list (no restart) holds the 152, a restart holds the same 152, and replaying them empties the list and the log; a log whose dead records end in 0 (rewritten by the test) is read with `died_at` 0 and replays. **With the database away at the start** (through `tests/pgproxy.py`, cut): the four routes about dead letters answer 503 and the service listens, and when the database comes the list is the whole 152 at once, with no restart. **Retention (stage 9):** 400 events dead at one endpoint and delivered at another with `delivery-log-bytes` at its minimum: the outcomes log is replaced by a snapshot, the list holds the 400 with their attempts, reason and time of death before and after a kill and a restart, the log read apart holds the kind-17 records, and the endpoint that delivered everything has no dead letter; a table of 2,048 with a floor snapshotted while it filled is the same after a restart, and a second look does not read the log again; with small segments, the dead letters of the events retention dropped are gone from the list and no floor is left for them.
* `tests/cancel_test.py` (64 checks, 12 to 14 s): three replays wait (sent twice, then ten minutes), one is cancelled (the table has two, the receiver is sent nothing, the event is still a dead letter with its attempts, reason and time as they were, a kind-16 record is in the log), **`kill -9` and a second one: still cancelled, the two others still waiting**; cancel-all; another endpoint's replays are not touched (10 wait, A's 5 cancelled, B's 5 wait across a kill); a cancelled replay asked for again is sent once; **under the `fsync` shim, two cancels acknowledged and the log cut to what the last `fsync` covered: still two waiting; the same for a bulk replay that took 4**; an attempt on the wire (a receiver that holds the answer 1.2 s): `409` for one, `busy` 1 for all, cancelled when asked again after it ended; the 404s (already cancelled, no replay, no endpoint, for both routes), the 400s (event 0, `x`), the 405s (six method and path pairs), a wrong token 401, and that the refusals wrote nothing; the bound of 32: the first call takes 32 and 68 remain, a call with the table full takes none and says 68, a single replay is a `507`, cancel-all frees the table, the rest go through in calls of at most 32, the 32 cancelled are still dead letters and a last call takes them: nothing was lost. **Retention (stage 10):** with two replays waiting and one cancelled, a thousand more events make the outcomes log be replaced by a snapshot; read apart from the service it holds the six dead letters as kind 17, a `replay` record for each of the two that wait and nothing for the cancelled one (and `scripts/logcheck.py` accepts the directory, here and with the kind-16 records of stage 1); after two kills and restarts the list, the replays that wait and the cancelled replay are what they were, and the receiver is sent nothing for the cancelled event.
* `tests/jitter_test.py` (21 checks, 35 s, no database): 400 events failing at one instant with a delay of 2,000 ms at 20 percent: every recorded delay within 1,600 to 2,400 ms (the record read apart from the service), the mean within 2 percent, all ten tenths of the range occupied and none holding a third, a standard deviation near a uniform range's, **the second attempt of every event arriving at the recorded time, none early**; `--retry-jitter 0`: 200 delays all 2,000 ms; the default (10 percent) within 1,800 to 2,200 and spread, `GET /config` says 10; two services apart with one endpoint id agree to 100 ms on each of 100 events and another id gives other delays; the later steps (300, 600 and 1,200 ms at 50 percent) each within its range and spread, every event tried 4 times and dead; **20 events waiting through two `kill -9`, the last restart with `--retry-jitter 0`: the 20 recorded times are the same, and every second attempt arrives at its recorded time**; 30 replays at 40 percent.
* `tests/limits_test.py` (92 checks, 32 to 40 s): **concurrency observed at a receiver that holds each request 0.3 s**: 2 for an endpoint following `--endpoint-concurrency 2` (and reached), 4 for one with 4 of its own and not 5, 1, 3 after a `PATCH` with no restart, 2 again after `null`, 8 by default and not 9; `GET` shows the limits in force; **rate observed in every window of one second**: 60 events at 10 a second take 5.9 s within 15 percent and no second holds more than 12; a rate of 9 (111 ms, not a multiple of the loop's 50 ms) takes its 3.2 s within 12 percent; 20 replays at 5 a second obey it; no attempt failed, none repeated, the retry counter did not move; **a limited endpoint (2 a second) beside an unlimited one that is sent 300 events in 0.4 s**, the metric counts the first and not the second, the held-back events are all delivered later; the service's `--endpoint-rate 5` is the default and an endpoint's own 100 and 1 override it either way; **six `kill -9` while 120 events are held back at 20 a second**: all arrive, repeats at most what was in flight, and a second inside a run never holds more than rate + depth + 1; every wrong value (13) a 400 at `PATCH` and at `POST` naming the member, the edges accepted, nothing stored by a refusal, the table's columns, a restart, `endpoints.conf` words in either order, `--import-endpoints`, 13 bad words refused at start naming the line; the settings from a file and flags and 13 refusals; **`psql -f sql/schema.sql` over a table made by the old definition, with rows in it**: the rows get 0 and 0, a second run changes nothing, the table refuses a concurrency of 9 and a rate of -1 itself.
* `tests/authz_test.py` (256 checks, 226 before): the four routes have a row each (read, admin, admin, admin) and an entry in `src/authz.ls`; 4 x 5 identities x 5 configurations more requests.

**Existing tests that had to change** are in 39.9.

Two things the tests found about themselves: `Probe` in `limits_test.py` first counted a request as in flight until its thread had finished writing the answer, so the next attempt of a limit of 3 was seen as the fourth (it failed once, as `nobody`, which is slower): the count now ends when the answer is sent; and the first `cancel_test` stage waited on a baseline taken after the replays were asked for.

### 39.7 Mutants

**48 mutants of the new code, 47 killed, 1 survives and is equivalent in behaviour** (each applied to a saved copy of the file, built, run against the test that should kill it with `FAIL_FAST`, restored from the copy and compared with `cmp`; nothing was left on disk). Killed: in the table, the shift of a full table (the entries above the place moved as well: the bug of 39.9), the binary search `<` for `<=`, the floor kept as the smallest, `wants_refold` true for a full table, a removal that does not count, a reason set for any attempt, a replaced entry keeping its old time, `clear_below` one too many; a dead letter not entered, a delivered replay that stays a dead letter, a dead record with no time, recovery that skips the final records, the completion folding at the floor and not above it, the list's descending page one late, `next` always null, bulk replay taking what is replaying, ignoring the room (31 calls and 713 taken for 100), not flushing (killed by the power cut of `cancel_test` 4b; first run against the wrong test and survived), ignoring `types`; cancel that cancels an attempt on the wire, does not flush (killed by the power cut), recovery that ignores kind 16, cancel-all that takes another endpoint's; jitter span doubled, upwards only, the event ignored, the attempt ignored, not applied to a window retry, not applied to a replay's, the setting ignored; the bucket that does not consume, unbounded, the sleep never shortened (first survived: rate 9 test added), an endpoint's own concurrency or rate ignored, the held-back count at every look, concurrency fixed at 8, a held-back event that goes on to start, replays not limited by the rate (first survived: 20 replays at 5 a second added), the loop's sleep fixed at 50, concurrency 9 allowed, a `PATCH` that does not apply the concurrency, a `POST` that does not apply the rate, the roster that drops the rate word, `endpoints.conf` ceiling 9, the metric not set, `retry-jitter` ceiling 51. **The survivor**: not completing the tables at the start. The first list or bulk replay completes the same table from the same log (stage 7 checks a restart and sees the 152 either way), so the only difference is when the log is read: before the service listens, or at the first look.

### 39.8 Measured

**The suite**, every step of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` in order (the four new ones after `breaker`) against a PostgreSQL of its own, `FULL=1` for idempotency and schedules: all 39 harness steps pass as root, and so do `fmt --check src tests`, the unit tests (197 in 18 sets), `scripts/check-authority.sh` (`docs/authority.json` is unchanged) and shellcheck. **The harnesses that touch the file system and the lock** (config, layout, chaos, stop, production, corrupt, delivery, metrics, ready, backup, idempotency, retention) **and the four new ones also pass as the unprivileged user `nobody`**, from a copy of the tree. One flake turned up there and is not in this change: stage `compactnow` of `tests/retention_test.py` failed in 1 run of 8 with the binary of the retention change itself, as `nobody` and as root alike. The events were final and the log still held the last few: with `retention-ms 1` an event must be 1 ms old to go, and the segment the `compact-now` run seals itself can be younger than that when it looks at it, so a second run drops it. The test runs a second one when the first left something.

**The hot path** (`scripts/bench/run.py`, four rounds, base = the `origin/main` of the time built by the same compiler, runs alternated base, new; the machine was shared, load average 3 to 4, so a few percent is noise; measured before this change was put on top of retention and the pool, and not repeated after):

| | base | this change |
|---|---|---|
| ingest only, 64 connections: CPU an event | 10.5 us (9 to 13) | 11 us (10 to 12) |
| 1 endpoint: CPU a delivery | 90.5 us (86 to 97) | 92 us (86 to 99) |
| 1 endpoint: deliveries a second, end to end | 5,986 | 6,126 |
| 10 endpoints: CPU a delivery | 72 us (68 to 77) | 74 us (64 to 82) |
| 10 endpoints: deliveries a second | 10,284 | 9,977 |

No cost is visible above the noise: a delivery gains one `admit` (no limit: a compare and a store), one `conc_of` and one `rate_of` a turn for each endpoint, and a `dead.put` at a death, which is not on the path of a delivery.

**The new structures** (this machine; 2,200 dead letters unless said): a page of 1,000 entries 6.0 ms, of 10 0.2 ms; 20,000 dead letters at start: the start of the 6.2 MB log takes 1.0 s with the table and 0.9 s with the dead-letter code switched off (100 ms, 5 us a dead letter; four to five starts each); after that restart a first page that needs places resolves them in 155 ms (7.7 us for each of the 20,000 events of the log, once); bulk replay of 2,048 dead letters, a loop of calls with the receiver mended, took 2.05 s. Resident size is unchanged until an endpoint has dead letters (the region is zero-filled lazily); the state is 4.07 MB larger and each `epx` row 32 bytes.

**Both directions of the log, with the real previous binary** (the binary built from `origin/main`, retention and the pool included): a log it wrote (five dead letters, no time) is read by this one: five entries, 2 attempts each, `status_5xx`, `died_at` 0, and the five replay; a log this version wrote with dead letters only is started by the previous binary, and one with a cancelled replay (kind 16) or with a snapshot's dead letters (kind 17) is refused by it with status 15.

### 39.9 Found along the way

* **`dead.put` into a full table corrupted it** (unit test written before the service test): the entries *above* the new one's place were moved down as well as those below it, overwriting the entry that should have moved up, so an id appeared twice and its neighbour vanished. The first unit test only inserted at the end of a full table; a test against a model (300 inserts at every position, checked for order, duplicates and the dropped entry) was written after the service test for 2,200 events showed it (`event 2154` twice, `2195` missing), and is a mutant killer (39.7).
* **The fold of the log cannot restore what it evicted**: see 39.1 (the test of stage 7 asked for the 152 oldest after replaying the 2,048 newest and the list was empty). The floor and `dead_complete` are the fix.
* **A boundary of one**: the completion folded the ids *below* the floor and the floor is itself one of the ids left out; the first list after a replay of 2,048 had 151 of the 152.
* **Existing tests that had to change, and why.** `tests/retry_test.py` passes `--retry-jitter 0` (`chaos.Service` has a `more` argument for flags; nothing else in the suite measures a retry gap, and the whole suite was run with the default). Four whole-object reads of `GET /endpoints` name the two new members (`manage_test` twice, `delete_test` twice) and `config_test.py` names the three new settings; `tests/ops_test.ls` and `metrics_test.py` count nine families an endpoint instead of seven; `tests/authz_test.py` has four rows and `authz_test.ls` four entries (the unit test that route 20 had no scope now uses 24); `backup_test.py` puts a concurrency and a rate in a row and compares the table row for row with its two new columns; `state_test.ls` allows kinds 16 and 17; `tests/endpoint_kit.py`'s `start` can be told not to wait for the endpoints to load (`wait_loaded`), for the test that starts with the database away; `tests/retention_test.py` runs a second `compact-now` when the first left a segment younger than the 1 ms retention (39.8).

### 39.10 Not verified, open, and merge conflicts

* **Not verified:** a log of millions of events with dead letters (the costs above are measured at 20,000 and extrapolated linearly); a table of 62 endpoints each with 2,048 dead letters; the rate limit across a restart on a machine under load (the bound of one bucket is argued and measured at 20 a second); that a rate above what one core delivers (about 10,000 a second) is reached; `retry-jitter` against a receiver that counts delays over days (the recorded time is checked, the schedule is not run for hours); the hot-path and cost figures of 39.8, which were measured before this change was put on top of retention and the pool and not repeated; the compiler is the one `lex-sys.toml` pins (`4c27593`), built from that commit locally and not by CI; CI's PostgreSQL (a container with a port mapping and `scram-sha-256`) was not available, the tests ran against a local server with trust authentication.
* **Open:** the table keeps 2,048 dead letters an endpoint and finding the rest reads a log; a ring would avoid the shift of a full table (about 8,000 integer moves for each death of an endpoint that is full), which costs 5 us a death in the measurement and was not worth the complexity; the places of the events (`resolve_offsets`) could be found by the start's own walk of the events log and are not; a list of dead letters has no filter by time or reason; `rate` cannot say "unlimited for this endpoint" under a limit for the rest except by 100,000; the throttle metric counts the first refusal after an admitted attempt, not events; a cancelled replay of an attempt that was on the wire is refused, not recorded for later; there is no way to cancel by event type.
* **Merge hotspots** (other changes to `ops.ls`, to the signal handling in `run` and `main` and to the compiler pin will meet these): `src/hooks.ls` — the imports (three lines), the layout after `ex` (`off_lim`, `off_dead`, `dv_size`), `clear_slot` (two lines), `finish_attempt` and `finish_replay` (the jittered `next_at`, the death time, the dead-letter table), the branches of `replay` (`dead_note` calls), `settle_endpoints` (the completion loop after `place_new`, and `dead_expire_all`), `start_attempts` (limits and `admit`), `run` (the argument of `server.wait`: one expression), `main` (one `lim.init` line after `rotation_grace_ms`), `handle` (one `if id >= 20 && id <= 23` block, and ids 20 to 23 in the `503` gate of section 37.2) and `routes`; `src/state.ls`, `src/compact.ls` and `scripts/logcheck.py` (the kinds: 15 is retention's header of the outcomes log, 16 and 17 are this change's; the snapshot writes a final cell as `delivered` and the table of dead letters as kind 17; the state's reader and the restore's check accept 1 to 17); `src/config.ls` (the settings are `cfg[28]` to `cfg[30]`, after the pool's `cfg[23..27]` and retention's `cfg[40..46]`; `size()` stays 48; **two branches that add settings collide on the index, and `tests/config_test.ls` has a test that no two settings share one**); `src/metrics.ls` (`row()` 11) with `tests/ops_test.ls` and `tests/metrics_test.py` counts; `src/authz.ls`, `tests/authz_test.py`, `tests/authz_test.ls` (routes 20 to 23); `src/epx.ls` (stride 2,664); `src/queries.ls` is generated: on a conflict regenerate it with `pgen` from `sql/queries.sql`; `lex-sys.toml` (four `[[test]]` sets), `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (four steps), `tests/chaos.py` (`more` in `flags` and `Service`).

## 40. `https` endpoints, names, and the destination rule at every attempt (`docs/production.md` P1.7)

Three slices, one change: **T1** the TLS phase of an attempt, **T2** host names and the SSRF rule applied to the address of every attempt, **T3** TLS sessions kept per endpoint. The design is
the lex-sys spike's (`docs/tls-nonblocking.md` in lex-sys, `examples/tls_nb/`: measured, with the gaps it found) built into the service; this section is what was decided here, what was
changed from the spike and why, what was measured, and what is not verified.

### 40.1 What was decided

* **OpenSSL, in the process** (production.md P1.7, the default taken): `libssl` and `libcrypto` through `Ffi`, 32 functions, each declared under its own library (section 40.7). The TLS of lex-sys
  itself (`packages/tls`, lex-sys epic #197) replaces it when it can verify a certificate chain (#206: today it accepts a leaf only if it is pinned byte for byte) and has been reviewed by
  someone else (#209: "not independently reviewed"). Nothing in the service outside `src/tls.ls` knows it is OpenSSL.
* **Names are resolved by the service itself, on the poller** (`src/dns.ls`, the DNS client's bytes; the exchange is a phase of `src/attempt.ls`), over TCP, to one name server. Not `getaddrinfo` (it
  blocks the loop for as long as the answer takes: 302 ms for a 300 ms answer in the spike; and the connect resolves a second time, so a check before it is not a check of what is dialled). Not a thread:
  the spike built that too, and what it cost in the language (a payload of one pointer, no way to wait for a thread on a poller, four named workers, a fixed loopback port) is more than a resolver of
  our own costs. The resolver does not need a thread, so it does not meet the rule of lex-sys `Signals` (`docs/signals.md`: watch first, then `spawn`; a `signals_watch` while a spawned thread has not been joined answers
  `EBUSY`): there is no thread in this service, and the resolver, like everything else, runs in the loop.
* **The scheme is part of the stored host** (`https://hooks.example.com`), and the API takes it as `scheme` or in a `url`. See 40.2 for why.
* **An `https` endpoint needs a host name**, not an address: the certificate is checked against the name, and a name is also what SNI carries. (An address with a certificate that has an IP address
  in its alternative names is not supported: `X509_VERIFY_PARAM_set1_ip_asc` is not declared.)

### 40.2 The endpoint

`endpoints.conf` and the `endpoints` table's `host` column hold `https://name` for a TLS endpoint and what they always held for the others (an IPv4 address, or now a name). The port is the port. **No
column was added**: `psql -f sql/schema.sql` is unchanged, a backup and a restore carry the scheme because they carry the host, and `--import-endpoints` copies it. The reason is a failure mode:
a previous version reading a table with a new `scheme` column would ignore it and deliver, in clear, to port 443 of an endpoint that was written down as `https`. A previous version reading
`https://hooks.example.com` in the host refuses the line (status 13), which is "refuse, don't downgrade".

Over the API (`POST /endpoints`, `PATCH /endpoints/:id`, admin token):

| | |
|---|---|
| `{"url": "https://hooks.example.com:8443"}` | scheme, host and port at once; no port means the scheme's own (443, 80); one final `/` is allowed and nothing else (no path, query or user); not with `host`, `port` or `scheme` (400) |
| `{"host": "hooks.example.com", "port": 443, "scheme": "https"}` | the same in members; `scheme` is `"http"` or `"https"` (anything else is a 400), left out is `"http"` on a `POST` and **what the endpoint has** on a `PATCH` |
| `PATCH {"host": "other.example.com"}` | keeps the scheme; `{"scheme": "http"}` alone changes only the scheme; an address under `https`, whichever of the two members brings it, is a 400 and the endpoint is as it was |
| `GET /endpoints`, `GET /endpoints/:id` | `"scheme": "https"` or `"http"` beside the port; still not the host and not a secret. The answers of `POST` and `PATCH` name the host without the scheme, and the scheme |

A `host` that starts with `https://` in a request is refused: the scheme is a member of its own, so there is one way to write it.

### 40.3 The TLS phase

`src/attempt.ls` gained states (the earlier three are unchanged for an address and `http`):

```
                                      +--(http)--------------------------------------------> sending -> reading
resolving (the name server) -> redial +                                                            ^
   |                                  +--(https)--> connecting -> handshake ----------------------+
   +-- the host is an address ---------------------> connecting --(http)------------------------>
```

Each state waits for the poller under the same token (`token0 + slot`), with the one deadline of the attempt (`deadline-ms`) over all of it. TLS is OpenSSL as a state machine over **two memory BIOs**
per connection: the bytes read from the socket go into one, the bytes OpenSSL wants sent come out of the other, and the sockets stay lex-sys `Conn`s in the attempt's `std.conns.Table`. So OpenSSL
never touches a descriptor (`conn_write` is `MSG_NOSIGNAL`: a peer that closed is an error code and not a `SIGPIPE`), the one place that touches the kernel is the one the type system governs, and
a slot is watched writable only while ciphertext waits for the kernel and readable otherwise. The verification, in `src/tls.ls`:

* `SSL_CTX_set_verify(SSL_VERIFY_PEER)`, **no way to turn it off** (the spike had one for comparison; it is not here), the minimum protocol TLS 1.2, `SSL_MODE_ENABLE_PARTIAL_WRITE | RELEASE_BUFFERS`, the library's
  own session cache off.
* The trust store, loaded **once, at start**, never per attempt (about 25 ms for the system's, a few for a file): the system's default locations (OpenSSL's rules: the compiled-in directory, and
  `SSL_CERT_FILE` / `SSL_CERT_DIR` if they are set in the service's environment), or **exactly** the PEM file `tls-ca-file` names, not both. A store that cannot be loaded is a refusal to start
