  (**status 21**, one line on stderr), never a client that does not verify.
* The host name: `X509_VERIFY_PARAM_set1_host` with the endpoint's name (no partial wildcards), and the same name in SNI. The `Host` header of the request is the name and the port (the port left out when
  it is the scheme's own), for an `https` endpoint and for a plain one that has a name; an endpoint at an address says `Host: receiver` as it always did.
* Revocation (CRL, OCSP, stapling) is **not checked**: OpenSSL does not unless it is given a source, and there is none. A certificate that is revoked and otherwise valid is accepted. No client certificate.

What changed from the spike's `tls.ls` (lex-sys `examples/tls_nb/`): per-library scopes (the spike had `Ffi("tls")` for both libraries and libc's `signal`; the new `foreign-authority.md` of lex-sys makes a
scope a set); no direct-socket transport and no `signal`; verification cannot be off; the connection's state is nine integers in the attempt's own array and its buffers are in the slot's bytes
(request, host name, 4 KiB read, 20 KiB ciphertext: 95,488 bytes a slot, 6.1 MB at 64 slots, from 4.5); a failure at the socket is `-1000 - errno` in the detail so that it is not mistaken for a
verification result (found by the first test of a receiver that closes: `ECONNRESET` is 104, and 104 would have read as an `X509_V_ERR`).

### 40.4 Why an attempt failed: ten new reasons

`reason.ls` numbers 17 to 26 (on disk, never renumbered), `attempt.ls` codes -14 to -23, the coarse `status` of the history as in the last column:

| reason | how an attempt gets it | `status` |
|---|---|---|
| 17 `dns_failed` | no name server known or reachable, an error answer (no such name, server failure, refused), bytes that are not DNS, an answer to another question, or a name with no IPv4 address | -1 |
| 18 `dns_timeout` | the deadline passed while the name was being resolved | -3 |
| 19 `ssrf_refused` | the name (or `localhost`) resolves to an address in the ranges of section 26; no connection was made | -1 |
| 20 `tls_handshake` | the peer closed or spoke badly during the handshake, offered nothing the client accepts (TLS before 1.2), or sent an alert | -1 |
| 21 `cert_untrusted` | the chain does not lead to a trusted root: unknown issuer, self-signed, a leaf whose issuer was not sent (`X509_V_ERR` 2, 18, 19, 20, 21, 27) | -1 |
| 22 `cert_expired` | expired or not yet valid (9, 10) | -1 |
| 23 `cert_hostname` | the certificate does not name the host (62, 63, 64) | -1 |
| 24 `cert_invalid` | any other verification failure: a purpose that does not fit, a bad signature, a CA that may not sign | -1 |
| 25 `tls_timeout` | the deadline passed during the handshake | -3 |
| 26 `tls_error` | TLS could not be started, or failed after the handshake | -2 |

Each is retried on the schedule and dead after it, like any failed attempt (a `410` over TLS is `gone`, dead at once, and disables the endpoint). The counters of `/metrics` have a series for each
(`hooks_attempt_failures_total{reason=...}`), `hooks_endpoint_last_failure` names the last, and the delivery log's kind 14 holds the number. The array of counters in `ops.ls` had room for 17: the
reasons now come **last** in it (`o_reasons() = 110`, `size() = 110 + reason.count()`), found by the first test that counted a `cert_expired` and read it as the probe's round. `/metrics` also has
`hooks_tls_handshakes_total{result="full"|"resumed"}`.

### 40.5 Names, and the destination rule at every attempt

**At the write** (`destination.host_ok`; the file, the table, `POST` and `PATCH` all call it): an IPv4 literal that is public (unless `allow-private-hosts 1`), or a **name** (`destination.name_ok`:
labels of letters, digits, `-` and `_`, 1 to 63 bytes each, 253 in all, no leading or trailing dot, and a last label that is not all digits). The last rule is what keeps `127.1`, `2130706433`, `0x7f.0.0.1`
and `1.2.3.4.5` out: a person reads them as addresses, a C library as other addresses, and the service as nothing. `allow-private-hosts 1` no longer accepts what is neither an address nor a name
(it used to accept any printable text, because the connect resolved whatever it was given; the changed assertions of `tests/ssrf_test.py` are marked CHANGED). A name's address cannot be known at the write,
so the write is a weaker guarantee than it was; the attempt is where the guarantee is.

**At every attempt** (`attempt.begin`, `advance`, `redial`):

1. an address (or `localhost` and `x.localhost`, which are the loopback by RFC 6761 and are not asked of a name server) is judged by `destination.is_public` and dialled; refused it is `ssrf_refused`;
2. a name is asked of the name server over TCP (the query is built by `dns.build_query`, the answer read as it arrives into the slot's buffer, `dns.parse` total on any bytes: a million damaged answers
   in `tests/dns_test.ls`), on the attempt's own connection: **the slot's connection is the one to the name server until the answer is in**, so the token, the deadline and the buffers are the attempt's
   throughout; `redial` then closes it and puts the connection to the endpoint in the same slot (the table gives back the slot it just freed; `redial` checks);
3. **every** A record of the answer is judged, and one private address refuses the whole answer, wherever it is (a name that has a private address in its answer is one resolver change from being
   sent there); an answer with no A record is `dns_failed`;
4. the connection is made to the first address as a literal, with the name used afterwards for SNI and the certificate and for nothing else. **No lookup happens between the check and the
   connection** (there is no second `getaddrinfo`; `tcp_connect_start` is given a dotted quad), so there is nothing for a rebinding name to change; and the name is asked again at the next attempt
   (no cache: the answer's TTL is ignored; the cost is about 0.1 ms of CPU and one round trip to the server, 40.9).

Which name server: `dns-server` (`ip` or `ip:port`) or the first IPv4 `nameserver` of `/etc/resolv.conf` (read once, at start; `resolve.ls`). **What this does not do**: no `/etc/hosts` (except `localhost`), no search
list (a name is used as written), no second server if the first is down, no UDP, no IPv6 name server, **no IPv6 at all**: AAAA records are not asked for, so a name that only has an IPv6 address is
`dns_failed`, and the destination rules are for IPv4. The name server must answer over TCP, which every recursive resolver does (systemd-resolved's stub, Docker's embedded server, dnsmasq, unbound,
the cloud resolvers) and a stub that is UDP-only does not (a resolver that does not is `dns_failed` and the reason says so). The query id is a counter mixed with the deadline: not unpredictable, which
matters to an off-path attacker against UDP and little against a TCP connection to a resolver on the same host or network. The service trusts the name server for names and **not for addresses**: whatever it
answers, an address that is not public is refused, so a hostile resolver can send the service to any public address and to no private one.

The rule is all-or-nothing, as `allow-private-hosts` always was: an operator whose receivers are on a private network and are named in a private DNS sets it to 1 and gives up the protection for every
endpoint (a per-name allow-list would be the next piece, and is not built). It does not limit the port, and a public address that is itself a proxy to the inside is outside what a service can know.

### 40.6 Sessions, and the keep-alive question

**The service does not keep connections.** Every attempt dials, sends the request with `Connection: close`, reads the status line, and closes: 1 connection a delivery, today as before this change (the
receiver of the tests counts them). With `https` that is a TLS handshake a delivery: 0.65 ms of CPU in the spike's measurement, about 0.9 ms here. Keeping the connection open per endpoint
(a request on an established session costs 0.01 ms) would remove it and is a larger change than this one (a pool in the table, deadlines for an idle connection, what a retry means when the connection
was closed by the receiver, and the retry accounting): not built. *Since then: designed in section 53, with its open questions.*

**What is built is resumption.** After the status line of an attempt over TLS, the connection's session (the ticket of TLS 1.3, or the session of 1.2) is kept for the endpoint (`attempt.keep_session`);
the next attempt to the endpoint offers it, and the handshake is an abbreviated one if the receiver still accepts it (a receiver that does not, because it restarted or the ticket expired, gets a full handshake
and the delivery is made: no failure). One session per endpoint slot, **in memory only**, 62 at most, each replaced by the newest, so the memory is bounded by the endpoints and not by the deliveries
(the test of 3,200 deliveries over TLS grows no faster than one without TLS). A session is kept under the **name and port** it was made for and is offered only to them, and is **dropped** whenever the
endpoint changes: a `PATCH` (of anything: host, port, scheme, secret) and a `DELETE` call `attempt.drop_session` for the slot, so the next attempt makes a full handshake and verifies the chain again.
`tls-resume 0` keeps none. A resumed handshake does not verify the certificate again (that is what makes it cheap, and what TLS means by resuming): it resumes a session whose peer was verified,
for this name, in this process.

### 40.7 The authority: what the service may now call

`lex-sys authority` was `UNBOUNDED` already, by one libc symbol (`statx`; the four signal functions went in section 36). It is, and the list is now:

```
before   unbounded_by: libc:statx                                                                                                  (1 pair; labels: ffi("libc"), signals INT,TERM)
after    unbounded_by: libc:statx, and
           libcrypto: BIO_new  BIO_read  BIO_s_mem  BIO_write  ERR_clear_error  ERR_get_error  X509_VERIFY_PARAM_set1_host  X509_VERIFY_PARAM_set_hostflags
           libssl:    SSL_CTX_ctrl  SSL_CTX_free  SSL_CTX_load_verify_file  SSL_CTX_new  SSL_CTX_set_default_verify_paths  SSL_CTX_set_verify  SSL_SESSION_free  SSL_SESSION_is_resumable  SSL_ctrl  SSL_do_handshake  SSL_free  SSL_get0_param  SSL_get1_session  SSL_get_error  SSL_get_verify_result  SSL_new  SSL_read  SSL_session_reused  SSL_set_bio  SSL_set_connect_state  SSL_set_session  SSL_shutdown  SSL_write  TLS_client_method
                                                                                                                                (33 pairs; labels: ffi("libc"), ffi("libcrypto"), ffi("libssl"), signals INT,TERM)
```

(The diff of `docs/authority.json` against the report before this change is exactly these 32 lines in `unbounded_by`, the same 32 in `foreign_symbols`, and the two new `ffi` labels, `libcrypto` and `libssl`, beside `libc`; the effects (`args`, `clock`, `conn_*`, `file_*`, `fs_*`, `heap`, `net_*`, `poll`, `err_write`) and the signals claim are unchanged.) `bounded` stays `false`, as it was. The 32 are the
whole of what the service may call in OpenSSL: the context (`TLS_client_method`, `SSL_CTX_new`, `SSL_CTX_free`, `SSL_CTX_ctrl` for the minimum protocol, the partial-write mode and the cache mode,
`SSL_CTX_set_verify`, `SSL_CTX_set_default_verify_paths`, `SSL_CTX_load_verify_file`); one connection (`SSL_new`, `SSL_free`, `SSL_set_bio`, `BIO_s_mem`, `BIO_new`, `SSL_set_connect_state`,
`SSL_ctrl` for SNI, `SSL_get0_param`, `X509_VERIFY_PARAM_set1_host`, `X509_VERIFY_PARAM_set_hostflags`); its steps (`SSL_do_handshake`, `SSL_read`, `SSL_write`, `SSL_shutdown`, `BIO_read`,
`BIO_write`, `SSL_get_error`, `ERR_clear_error`, `ERR_get_error`, `SSL_get_verify_result`); and the session (`SSL_session_reused`, `SSL_get1_session`, `SSL_set_session`,
`SSL_SESSION_is_resumable`, `SSL_SESSION_free`).

`scripts/check-authority.sh` regenerates the report from the sources and the pinned libraries and fails CI on any difference from `docs/authority.json`; a new foreign symbol is one added line, and
committing the file is the approval. Each function takes only the part of the capability it uses (`Ffi("libssl")` for the context and the sessions, `Ffi("libcrypto")` for the BIOs, both for a handshake;
`main` narrows once, `narrow(ffi, "libc,libcrypto,libssl")`, and lends down). The scope is a claim and the symbol is the fact (lex-sys `docs/foreign-authority.md` section 3): nothing checks that `SSL_new` is in
`libssl`; `-lssl -lcrypto` is on the link line only. **OpenSSL is C code in the process**; what the report cannot say is what it does with its memory and with the bytes it is given. The functions
declared are those a TLS client needs and no more (no function that opens a file other than the trust store, none that reads the environment: OpenSSL does that itself in
`SSL_CTX_set_default_verify_paths`).

Building: the project file has no linking options for a program, so `scripts/cc-ssl.sh` (a `cc` that adds `-lssl -lcrypto`) is the `CC` of `lex-sys build` (`scripts/build.sh`, `scripts/release.sh`, the
Dockerfile). OpenSSL **3.0 or later** is needed (`SSL_CTX_load_verify_file` is its one-string form of the call that loads a file; the two-string form cannot be declared: lex-sys gap 4), at run time
(`libssl3` on Debian and Ubuntu) and with its development files to build (`libssl-dev`).

### 40.8 Tests

Every test starts the service for real; the TLS receiver is Python's `ssl` behind a throwaway authority made with the `openssl` command, and the name server is the test's own (DNS over TCP), so what
a name resolves to and how often it was asked is known.

| file | what it shows | checks |
|---|---|---|
| `tests/https_test.py` | a good chain is delivered and its signature verifies with the reference library; SNI, `Host`, TLS 1.2 or 1.3; each certificate that is not good has its reason and **nothing is delivered** (expired, not yet valid, another name, another authority, a self-signed one, a purpose that does not fit), with and without `tls-ca-file`, and the controls that show the check and not the setup failed (a self-signed certificate that *is* the file is trusted; the same certificate for another name is delivered to when the endpoint has that name; the file replaces the system's store even where `SSL_CERT_FILE` trusts the authority); the handshake: a receiver that closes (FIN), resets (RST), speaks something that is not TLS, offers TLS 1.1 only (and a control that it does offer it, and that the service's own minimum is what refuses it: the OpenSSL configuration of the test allows TLS 1.0 at security level 0), never answers (at the deadline, `tls_timeout`); after it: silent, 500, 302 (not followed), 410 (dead, endpoint disabled); a status line in two records, a 60,000-byte event to a receiver reading 2 KiB at a time, **partial and refused I/O** (`tests/io_shim.c`: `send` at most 700 bytes, `recv` at most 300, every third call `EAGAIN`, on the receiver's and the name server's connections); the retry schedule (a certificate mended between attempts is delivered by the retry, one that never is makes three failures and a dead letter); the trust store (`SSL_CERT_FILE`; an unreadable `tls-ca-file`, or a file with no certificate, is status 21); **`kill -9` in the middle of a handshake and the event is delivered after the restart**; **64 handshakes the receiver never answers hold nothing** (the longest wait of a request to the service while they are pending: 0.6 to 1.3 ms, idle 0.9 to 3.2 ms) and all end at the deadline; a dead letter of a reason above 16 is named by `scripts/logcheck.py`, kept by a snapshot of the outcomes log (`--compact-now 1`) and listed with its reason after it (retention, 40.12) | 94 |
| `tests/names_test.py` | 21 unsafe addresses behind names (loopback, 10/8, 172.16/12, 192.168/16, 169.254/16 with the metadata address, 100.64/10, 0/8, multicast, reserved, broadcast, documentation, benchmarking, protocol assignments) are each `ssrf_refused`, with a receiver on loopback **never connected to** (by a count of TCP connections, not of requests) and each name asked once; four mixed answers (a public and a private address in either order, and four addresses) are refused whole; `localhost`, `app.localhost`, `LocalHost` are refused and not asked of the name server; under `allow-private-hosts 1` the same kinds of name are delivered to, with the name and the port in `Host`, and an `https` endpoint behind a name that resolves to loopback is delivered to there and refused under the default (the receiver's connection count shows no handshake started); a public address is dialled; **rebinding**: a name that answers a public address and then 127.0.0.1 is refused from the second attempt on, the loopback receiver is never connected to and the name is asked once per attempt; a name that alternates between 127.0.0.1 and 127.0.0.2 is delivered to each receiver in turn, one lookup per delivery; NXDOMAIN, SERVFAIL, REFUSED, an answer with no A record, bytes that are not DNS, an answer to another question, and no name server listening are each `dns_failed`; a name server that takes 3 s: `dns_timeout` at the deadline; **64 lookups waiting on a 2 s name server hold nothing** (longest wait 1.1 to 2.2 ms) and 64 lookups of 300 ms are delivered in 0.34 s; the lookup under partial and refused I/O (the query out five bytes at a time, the answer in three) | 37 |
| `tests/sessions_test.py` | resumption seen from the receiver (TLS 1.3 and 1.2: six deliveries, six handshakes, the first full and five resumed; `tls-resume 0`: none); two endpoints behind one receiver each have their own; a `PATCH` of the host, of the secret and of the port, and a `DELETE` then a new endpoint behind the same name and port, each make the next delivery a full handshake; a receiver that restarted (new ticket keys) refuses the session and the delivery is made with no failure; **no leaks**: against a control that does everything but TLS, 500 more events with three failing handshakes an event and 3,000 more deliveries over TLS cost no more memory (within 1.5 MiB; measured 0.6 and 0.1 MiB more) and leave the same descriptors | 32 |
| `tests/https_api_test.py` | `POST` with a `url` (with and without a port, `http`, a final slash), with `host`, `port` and `scheme`; the table holds the stored form; `GET` says the scheme and no host; 21 requests that are not an endpoint are each a 400 and store nothing; `PATCH` of the scheme alone, the host alone (keeps the scheme), a `url`, an address under `https` in each order (400, the row as it was); the file (an `https://` line is read and delivered to, an address under `https`, another scheme, a bare scheme and an upper-case one stop the start with 13 naming line 1); `--import-endpoints` copies the scheme; **a backup and a restore** keep it and the restored service delivers over TLS again; the history of attempts (the API and the table) says `cert_untrusted`, `cert_expired`, `cert_hostname`, `ssrf_refused`, `dns_failed` and `dns_timeout` with the coarse status each always had | 57 |
| unit | `tests/dns_test.ls` (6: a known answer, the errors by name, a name with no address, the query builder, dotted decimal, **a million damaged answers**), `resolve_test.ls` (2), `manage_test.ls` (3: the `url`, the stored host), `destination_test.ls` (11: 5 new: names, 63 and 253 bytes, the host in the form it is stored, `localhost`, the scheme), `reason_test.ls` (the ten reasons, their numbers and coarse statuses), `config_test.ls` (the three settings), `ops_test.ls` (every reason in a cell of its own: the test that found the overlap) | 6, 2, 3, 11, 6, 20, 13 |

Existing tests whose assertions changed, each marked CHANGED or explained in the file: `tests/ssrf_test.py` (names moved from refused to accepted at the write, the hosts that are neither an address nor a name are refused even under `allow-private-hosts`),
`tests/destination_test.ls` (a name in a file is accepted; `127.1` is refused open or not), `tests/patch_test.py` (a `PATCH` to a name is a 200, a number form is a 400), `tests/config_test.ls` (the blob has the trust store's path after the tokens), `tests/reason_test.py` and
`tests/metrics_test.py` (26 series of failures, not 16), and the exact answers of `GET /endpoints/:id` and `PATCH` in `tests/manage_test.py`, `tests/patch_test.py` and `tests/delete_test.py` (they have a `scheme`). Nothing was weakened: each
assertion was either made about the new rule or kept.

### 40.9 Measured

`scripts/bench/https_cost.py`: the CPU time the service itself uses (user + system, from `/proc`) per delivery, over 600 deliveries, the median of 5 runs with the least and the most; the service on core 3 of a shared 4-core VM
(Intel Xeon 2.10 GHz, the same family as the spike's; other work was running on it), the receivers (Python) and the name server on cores 1 and 2. The ingest of the event, the logs and everything else is in every row.

| | 1 endpoint (an event a delivery) | 10 endpoints |
|---|---|---|
| plain HTTP, an address | 267 us (183 to 300) | 100 us (67 to 117) |
| plain HTTP, a name (one lookup a delivery) | 283 us (267 to 317) | 167 us (150 to 217) |
| `https`, a full handshake every time (`tls-resume 0`) | 1,433 us (1,400 to 1,533) | 1,033 us (983 to 1,317) |
| `https`, the session resumed | 833 us (767 to 983) | 683 us (650 to 817) |

* **A name costs about 70 us** (the lookup: a TCP connection to the name server, a query, an answer, `dns.parse`; the spike measured 100 us) and a round trip to the name server, and no cache spares it.
* **A full verified handshake costs about 0.9 ms of the service's CPU (0.87 ms at 10 endpoints, 1.15 ms at 1), a resumed one about 0.5 ms**, a third less per delivery. The spike measured 0.65 ms and 0.31 ms for the handshake alone in a client that did
  nothing else, 64 in flight; here the same work shares a core with the loop, the logs and the ingest, and with a machine that others were using. One core therefore does about 970 `https` deliveries a second with full handshakes and
  1,460 resumed, against 10,000 plain (at 10 endpoints).
* **Plain HTTP did not get slower**: `scripts/bench/run.py` on the commit before and after (the CPU a delivery, 4 alternated runs each): 10 endpoints 62 to 83 us before (median 76), 66 to 77 after (73); 1 endpoint 72 to 92 before (79), 64 to 97 after (85). The difference is inside the noise of the runs.
* **The loop is not held.** A request to the service while 64 handshakes are pending (the receiver never answers): the longest wait over 100 requests 0.6 to 1.3 ms, against 0.9 to 3.2 ms with nothing pending; while 64 lookups wait on a name server that takes 2 s: 1.1 to 2.2 ms (idle 0.7 to 5 ms). A lookup that blocks would hold the loop for the 2,000 ms.
  64 lookups of 300 ms each are delivered in 0.34 s in all.
* **Memory**: the slots' buffers grew from 4.5 MB to 6.1 MB (95,488 bytes a slot, of which 70,656 are the request); OpenSSL's own state is 26 to 48 KiB a connection while it lasts (the spike's measurement; the memory test above bounds it in use);
  the process is about 5.7 MB larger with OpenSSL mapped and the system's trust store loaded (9.7 MB against 4.0 MB after 100 deliveries to one endpoint on the commit before; the slope per event is the same).
* **The connection is not kept** (40.6): the receiver of the tests counts one connection per delivery before and after, and one handshake per delivery without sessions.

### 40.10 Mutants

47 mutants of the new code were killed and 2 were not: **49 in all, run on a copy of the tree, each built and run against the test files it names, the file restored and compared with `cmp` after each** (`scripts/mutate.py`, the list in `tests/mutants/https.py`; it has 49 entries). The first run killed 40 of 45; the four that survived were each a hole in a test, and were closed by tests, not excused (marked below). One does not compile (the checker's row check: a function that
declares `ffi("libssl")` and no longer performs it is refused), which is the foreign-authority rows doing their job; its replacement is a mutant of `tls.drop` itself. One survives and is redundant by design.

| area | mutants (killed by) |
|---|---|
| the address policy | judged addresses never refused (names); only the first address judged (names); an answer with no address taken as an address (names); the literal check at the attempt removed (names); `localhost` sent to the name server (names) |
| the pin | the connection made to the name server's address, not the answer's (names); the "resolving" flag kept after the redial (names) |
| failure codes | not-yet-valid not told from expired, the host-name codes, the unknown-issuer code, the "other verification failure" code, the handshake deadline's code, the lookup deadline's code, a refused connection to the name server read as a connection failure (https, names) |
| TLS | verification off; host name not set; SNI not set; trust file ignored; system store added to the file's; **minimum protocol TLS 1.0 (survived the first run: the system's configuration refuses TLS 1.1 as well; killed by a test that runs the service under a configuration that allows it)**; **a write that would block reported as done (survived: loopback never blocks; killed by the partial-I/O test)**; **the peer's end of stream in the handshake taken as "wait" (survived: the test reset the connection and never ended it; killed by a receiver that closes with a FIN)**; a write that waits reported as a failure (killed by the partial-I/O test); a lookup that stops sending after a partial write, one that parses half an answer (killed by the same, on the name server's connection) |
| sessions | never saved; the old session not freed on replacement (3,000 deliveries); `drop_session` a no-op; the drop on `PATCH`/`DELETE` not called (a secret change then resumes); `tls.drop` replaced (the SSL objects leak: 134 MiB more); **the key (name and port) not compared: survives** (a `PATCH` of the host or port drops the session as well, so the second check is never the only one; it is there for the day that is forgotten) |
| names and hosts | the last label may be all digits; an address under `https` allowed; any address allowed when open; the guard against a scheme prefix in `host`; the stored host's length; the scheme lost on a `PATCH` of the host; the scheme of a `PATCH` ignored; `Host` always `receiver`; the scheme of the table always 0 (the unit tests and the API test) |
| reasons, settings, bytes | the SSRF reason mapped to another; the deadline statuses; the counters' offset in `ops.ls` back to 24 (the overlap this work found); a damaged answer's length not checked (traps in the fuzz); a name server of `0.0.0.0` taken (twice); `tls-resume`'s default |

### 40.11 Not verified, open, and what lex-sys lacked

* **Not verified.** Nothing was connected to a public host: this environment reaches the internet only through a proxy, so a real public chain (an intermediate sent by the server, a root of the system's store, a wildcard, an SNI-routed host) was
  not exercised; the system's store was exercised through `SSL_CERT_FILE`, and the default locations were opened under `strace` (below) but not matched against a real certificate. The container image was not built here (no Docker), and the unit was not run under
  systemd: `strace` over an `https` workload (names, a good and an untrusted receiver, with and without `tls-ca-file`) shows 42 distinct system calls, **all inside the unit's `@system-service` less `@privileged` and `@resources`** (checked with
  `systemd-analyze syscall-filter`), `AF_INET` only, no mapping that is writable and executable, OpenSSL reading `/usr/lib/ssl/openssl.cnf` and, without `tls-ca-file`, the directory of trusted certificates. The Debian/Ubuntu OpenSSL 3.0 of the test machine only; OpenSSL 3.2 or later (where
  `SSL_CTX_load_verify_file` and the rest are as here) and LibreSSL, which has no `SSL_CTX_load_verify_file`, were not tried. The tests ran on the LLVM backend only.
* **Open.** No keep-alive (40.6). No revocation. No client certificate. No IPv6, no `/etc/hosts`, one name server over TCP only, no cache of lookups (a name server that is slow slows every attempt to its names). A per-name allow-list for private
  addresses (today `allow-private-hosts` is all or nothing). An `https` endpoint at an address with a certificate for that address. `GET /config` does not show the three new settings. A query id that is not unpredictable.
  The last unverified claim of the spike that this repeats: handshake CPU depends on how busy the core is kept, so a figure is only good with the concurrency it was measured at.
* **What lex-sys lacked or got wrong (reproducers in the report of this change):** (1) a project cannot say what a program links with (`[[bin]]` has no `links`): `scripts/cc-ssl.sh` is a `CC` that adds `-lssl -lcrypto`; the same gap as the spike's 11. (2) A
  local binding named like a function of an imported module breaks the qualified call: with `let bare = ...` in scope, `destination.bare(x)` is "`bare` is a local binding, not a function". (3) `std.conns` has no way to put a connection in a slot that is free
  on purpose: `redial` closes one and puts the next, and relies on the table giving back the slot it just freed ("newest freed first" is in the comment of the module and in no test of it); a `replace(table, slot, conn)` would say what is meant. (4) The
  declared scope of a foreign function is not checked against what is linked (the follow-up 5 of lex-sys `foreign-authority.md`): `libssl` can be claimed with no `-lssl`, and this change's CI pin is of the claim and the symbol, not of the library. (5) Nothing
  catches an array region that overlaps another by arithmetic: the 17 reasons' counters and the probe's round were one cell apart, and 27 reasons wrote over the round and the last-reason cells; only a test that counted caught it (a hazard of the hand-laid arrays of
  `hooks.ls`, not of the compiler, but a layout check like `lex-sys layout` for `[int]` regions would have). (6) `c_ptr` still cannot be named in a signature, so a handle is an `int`, and a foreign function with a string that is not last still cannot be
  declared (the spike's 1 and 4): `SSL_CTX_load_verify_file` and `X509_VERIFY_PARAM_set1_host` are the one-string forms for that reason.

### 40.12 Put on top of the signals, the pool, the retention and the delivery controls

This section was written and tested on the base of section 35. It was then moved onto sections 36 to 39 (the signals claim, the pool that reconnects, retention, dead letters and per-endpoint limits), and what that changed is recorded here.

* **Nothing of the others was changed to make room**; what collided moved on this side. The settings `dns-server`, `tls-ca-file` and `tls-resume` are `cfg[31]` to `cfg[34]` (the pool's are 23 to 27, the delivery controls' 28 to 30,
  retention's 40 to 46; the table is still 48 wide), and `tests/config_test.ls` sets all of them to values of their own and reads each back, so two settings on one index fail it. The counters of the handshakes are `g[44]` and `g[45]` of the metrics
  (`g_size` is 46); the cells of `ops` (24 and 25, and the reasons at 110) did not move. The reasons 17 to 26 are on disk and are as they were.
* **The signals claim and the TLS context.** `main` narrows `ffi` once, to `libc,libcrypto,libssl`, and `run` is lent only `libcrypto,libssl`; `statx` is lent `libc` alone. The context is freed by `attempt.close_tls` at the end of `run`: a first version of
  this merge freed it in `main` as well, which is a double free that aborts the process at every stop (`free(): double free detected`), found at once by the one test that reads the whole of stderr after a stop (`tests/authz_test.py`, stage 4: it
  wants `listening` and the two lines of the drain, and nothing else). The context is freed in `main` only when `run` was never entered (the listener could not be opened).
* **The table is read after the service listens (section 37)**, so an `https` endpoint of the table is not delivered to before `hooks: endpoints loaded`, as no endpoint of the table is; the tests that start a service with a database wait for that line (`opslib.Service.start`).
  A row of the table that is not a valid endpoint (an address under `https`, for one) is now the exit status 13 *after* `listening`, with the message naming the row; before, the service did not start at all. The test changed to say that (`tests/https_api_test.py`, stage 4).
* **The limits of an endpoint (section 39.4) are those of an `https` endpoint too**: an attempt holds its slot, and so counts against the endpoint's concurrency, from the lookup through the handshake to the end of the response. A rate limit holds an attempt back before
  its lookup. (This is how the code reads and the existing tests of the limits pass; there is no test of the limits over TLS.)
* **Retention (section 38).** A reason is stored in a record of kind 14 and, packed with the attempts, in a dead letter (`attempts * 65536 + reason + 1`: a reason up to 65,534 fits). A snapshot of the outcomes log does not write kind 14 (it writes the state, not its history),
  but it does write every dead letter the table holds, with its reason; so after a snapshot the reason of a *dead letter* of names or TLS is kept, and the reason of an event that is waiting for its next attempt is not (as for every reason). `scripts/logcheck.py` knows the names
  of 17 to 26 (it printed `unknown` for them) and `tests/https_test.py` stage 9 checks both: the checker's histogram of reasons names `cert_untrusted` three times before the snapshot, accepts the directory after it with the dead letter as a record of kind 17, and the listing of
  the dead letters after a restart still says `cert_untrusted`, three attempts.
* **Authority.** The pinned report is the base's (`bounded: false`, `unbounded_by: ["libc:statx"]`, signals `INT,TERM`) plus exactly 32 pairs and two labels: the diff of `docs/authority.json` is the 8 `libcrypto:` and 24 `libssl:` symbols of 40.7, the same 32 in `foreign_symbols`, and the two `ffi` labels.

## 41. More than 62 endpoints (`docs/production.md` P1.6), and `--compact-now` with a database

*The design, written and committed before any code of the change. Where building it found something different, the later subsections say so (41.9 onward); this part is not edited to look right afterwards.*

### 41.1 Where 62 comes from

`state.max_endpoints()` is 62 because the disabled set is one integer (a bit a slot, 63 usable). Everything else that was sized for 62 followed it, and most of those sizes are not obvious from the name. What scales with the limit, and what each endpoint costs today (measured on `origin/main`: one endpoint, 8.9 MB resident; 62 endpoints, 15.3 MB, so **about 105 KB resident for each endpoint**, all of it written at start by code that stores zeros over zeros):

| what | per endpoint | where |
|---|---|---|
| four bit-sets in the control block: disabled, paused, draining (a deleted endpoint's attempt is still on the wire), tripped (the breaker said so on stderr) | 1 bit each | `hooks.ls` `c_disabled()` ... |
| the window: the cells (final, attempts, next attempt; 3 x 1,024 integers), the place of each event in the events log (1,024), the in-flight flags (1,024) | 40 KiB | `state.ls`, `hooks.ls` `off_cells()`, `off_offs()`, `off_flight()` |
| the extras row (subscription, headers as sent, previous key, two limits), one byte to an integer | 21 KiB | `epx.ls` `stride()` 2,664 |
| the table of dead letters (2,048 entries of four integers) | 64 KiB | `dead.ls` `block()` |
| twelve small integers: cursor, in flight, slot id, scan position (2), run of failures, last failure reason, rate bucket (5) | 96 B | `hooks.ls`, `ops.ls`, `lim.ls` |
| a saved TLS session and the name and port it was saved for | 16 B (and the session itself, 1 to 2 KB, only for an `https` endpoint that has been used) | `attempt.ls` `most_sessions()` is **64**: a slot above it is never resumed, without saying so |

and what is sized for the text of the table and not for the endpoints, but holds 62 of them: the text limit (32 KiB: both the file, the table written as one, and the blob of hosts and keys), the pool's input slab (128 KiB, which holds the reply to the read of the table: a reply that does not fit is `status 8`, "the table is too large"), the 64 KiB arena of a region (`main` allocates the text there), the room of a snapshot of the outcomes log (16 MiB, built in one buffer), and, the one nobody wrote down, **the server's output queue: 64 KiB for a connection** (`http-server` `output_size()`: one whole answer and a little more; a connection that cannot take it is closed). `GET /metrics` for 62 endpoints is 24.9 KB and `GET /endpoints` about 15 KB; neither could be 16 times larger.

Two more places use the limit as a *sentinel*: the format header of `delivery.seg` is an outcome of kind 15 whose endpoint field is `max_endpoints()` (62), "which is no slot", and `scan_slots` takes a slot first met in any outcome for its endpoint's own id. With 62 a valid slot number, the header would give slot 62 an owner.

### 41.2 The limit: 1,024

**1,024 endpoints; ids stay up to 999,999.** Why this number and not another:

* The **cost of using it is CPU, not memory**: a delivery is 63 to 78 microseconds of CPU (measured on main with 1, 10 and 62 endpoints: 76, 63 and 63 us), so one event to 1,024 endpoints is about 65 ms of one core and the service takes about 15 such events a second at saturation. That is a fan-out of a real subscriber list, and a point where a second service on its own database is the next step. At 4,096 one event to everyone is a quarter of a second and the largest snapshot (one record for each of 4,096 x 1,024 cells) is 320 MB: not a number to state without a reason.
* **Lazy by construction, not by a new allocator.** The delivery state is one zero-filled heap block (`calloc`: pages that are never written are not resident, which is why one endpoint is 8.9 MB and not 8 MB more). The rule this change adds is that **nothing at the start, on a restart, or when an endpoint is created writes a zero over a region that is already zero**: a window is cleared by reading it and writing only what is not zero, an extras row and a dead-letter table clear only what they used (they keep the invariant that everything past their length is zero), and the arrays that are written at the start are dense (one integer a slot). So an idle endpoint costs its table row, its host and key, and about twelve integers (a few hundred bytes); a window becomes resident a page at a time as events pass through it; a dead-letter table only where it has entries. The idle resident cost per endpoint, the busy one, and the worst one (every window and table full: 1,024 x (40 + 21 + 64) KiB = 125 MiB) are measured, not predicted (41.8).
* The **virtual** size is 16.5 times the block of 62 (131 MB for the state, plus what the text limits below add). No reservation larger than a snapshot's 16 MiB buffer is made at once (41.4).
* **Why not "only for the endpoints that need them", as a second allocator.** The slot of an endpoint is chosen once and stays (design 25); an arena of windows handed out on first use would need its own free list, its own record in the log and its own recovery, to save what laziness at the page already saves. Lazy pages are free, fall out of the existing layout, and are a rule a test can state (41.8): the resident size after a start with 1,024 idle endpoints is a few MB.

### 41.3 What the loop does each turn, and what it must not

`start_attempts` visits every endpoint of the table each turn, in a rotation, and for each reads its limits from the extras row (21 KiB apart: a cache miss each), reads the clock, and then looks at the window from the cursor up. With 62 endpoints that is nothing; with 1,024 it is 1,024 of each per turn, and a turn happens whenever a request arrives, an attempt ends, or 50 ms pass. The decision, with the rule that makes it safe:

* **An endpoint that cannot have work is skipped by two comparisons, before anything else is read.** It cannot have work when (a) it is not disabled, and (b) it has looked at every event it may (`scan_id(e)` is at least `min(newest event, cursor + window)`: nothing in the log is left for it to read), and (c) the earliest time anything of its window can be tried again (`wake(e)`, in Unix ms) has not come. `wake(e)` is computed by the pass that looked at the whole window and nothing else: it is the smallest next-attempt time among the events that are not final and not on the wire, or "never" if there is none. A pass that stopped early (the turn's budget of starts, the endpoint's own concurrency, its rate limit, the skip budget) does not set it: the endpoint is looked at again next turn, as today.
* **Everything that can create work for a skipped endpoint clears `wake(e)`**: an attempt of it ends (`finish_attempt`, `finish_replay`: a cursor moved, a time was set), the slot is given, freed or reset (`clear_slot`), the endpoint is enabled. A new event is not in that list on purpose: it shows as `scan_id(e)` below the newest event, so the endpoint is looked at without anyone remembering to tell it. A mistake in this list is a lost wake-up (an event that waits until something else wakes the endpoint), which is why the test for it is the worst one I know how to write for it: kill -9 chaos over many endpoints with receivers that fail, checking that every event reaches every endpoint, and a mutant for each line of the list (41.7).
* The cost of a turn is then **N comparisons for the endpoints with nothing to do, plus the work of the ones that have some**. `/stats` counts the turns and the endpoints looked at (`turns`, `endpoints_looked_at`), so a test can say "1,024 endpoints that have caught up: ten turns, no look". *(Corrected in 41.10: this sentence first went on "and 100 events to one subscriber are 100 looks, not 102,400", which is false: an endpoint whose subscription does not want an event still has to read it to know, so a new event is one look at every endpoint, and what a quiet endpoint saves is the looks of the turns in which no event came.)*
* What this does **not** bound: an event that every endpoint wants costs a delivery for each, and an event that none wants costs each endpoint a look at it (about 2.4 us a pair, measured in section 35.8; 2.5 ms an event at 1,024). That is the price of a subscription filter being read by the endpoint and not indexed, and an index by type is the next step for it, not part of this change.

### 41.4 The data structures

* **The four bit-sets become one array of flags**, a word a slot (disabled 1, paused 2, draining 4, tripped 8). `is_disabled(dv, e)` and the others keep their names and meaning, so no caller changes; `c_draining` and `c_tripped` become counts, so "is there any" is still one comparison.
* **The window** is unchanged in shape (cells, places, in-flight flags, 1,024 each, indexed `id % window`); its offsets are computed from the limit as they always were. `state.reset` writes only what is not already zero. `state.max_endpoints()` is the one number; 1,024 and 62 appear nowhere else.
* **The extras** (`epx.ls`): the six small integers an endpoint's turn reads (subscription length, headers length, previous key length and validity, concurrency, rate) move to a dense block of eight integers a row at the head of the array; the bytes (subscription 512, headers 2,048, previous key 96) stay in rows of 2,656 after it and are touched only when set. Setting a shorter value clears the tail (a header value is a credential: deleting or shortening it used to leave it behind in the part of the row past the length, and `clear_row` was the only thing that erased it), so a row is all zero past its lengths and **clearing or moving a row costs what it holds, not 21 KiB**. A `DELETE` at index 0 of 1,024 endpoints moves 1,023 rows of a few integers each.
* **The dead letters** (`dead.ls`): the table is sorted and already zero past its count (every removal clears the entry it vacates); `clear` zeroes the entries it holds and not 8,192 integers.
* **TLS sessions**: `attempt.most_sessions()` is the limit (it was 64), the arrays in the environment grow with it.
* **Text.** The average allowance is what it was (528 bytes an endpoint: 32 KiB for 62): **the text limit is 528 x the limit = 540,672 bytes**, the blob likewise. The text of the table is read into a heap box, not into the arena of a region (64 KiB: the trap that section 25.1 hit once). The pool's input slab for the database is **1 MiB** a connection (zero-filled, so resident as it is used): the reply to the read of the table is the text plus about 40 bytes a row. A table that does not fit is what it was: `status 8`, then status 20 with reason 6 (now "the table is too large (the service reads at most 540,672 bytes of it)"); `POST /endpoints` has its `507` when the text would not fit.
* **Answers are bounded by the 64 KiB queue, and so are made in pages.** `GET /endpoints` takes `limit` (1 to 256, default 64) and `offset` (default 0), in table order, and stops early if the page would pass 40 KiB; it answers the same JSON array as before, and says what it left out in headers (`X-Total-Count`, and `X-Next-Offset` when more remain). With 64 endpoints or fewer, with ordinary members, nothing is different. **`GET /metrics` takes `page`** (0 by default): page 0 is the service's own series and the per-endpoint series of the first 64 endpoints of the table; page k is the endpoints 64k to 64k + 63 and nothing of the service's (so a scrape of every page counts nothing twice); a page past the last is `404`; `hooks_metrics_pages` says how many there are. A Prometheus with more than 64 endpoints scrapes one job per page (`params: {page: ["1"]}`). The 64 are what fits in 64 KiB with seven-digit values and six-digit ids (about 690 bytes of series an endpoint); the longest page is tested, not computed.
* **The snapshot** of the outcomes log is built and written **in chunks of 62 slots**, in the same turn and with the same steps, so its buffer stays 16 MiB however many slots there are (a buffer for the worst case of 1,024 would be 277 MB of address space): a state with 62 slots or fewer is one chunk and the file is byte for byte what it was. The stall of the step grows with what the snapshot holds (it is one turn, as before), and that is a measured gate (41.7).
* **Small things that scanned all slots each turn** (`finish_drains`, the report of tripped slots) return at once when their count is zero.

### 41.5 What does not change, and how an old directory and an old build are treated

* `endpoints.conf`, the `endpoints` table, `sql/schema.sql`, the `attempts` table, every API member: unchanged. A deployment with 62 endpoints or fewer starts, delivers and snapshots **as before**; the format header of `delivery.seg` still carries 62 in its endpoint field (written by `state.format_slot()`, a constant, and no longer by the limit), so the file is byte for byte what main wrote, and `scan_slots` now skips a record of kind 15 explicitly.
* **A log that uses a slot of 62 or above must not be read as if it did not.** A build from before this change reads a record with an endpoint above 61 and does nothing with it, silently: an endpoint in slot 500 would start again from the slowest cursor and be sent events again (or miss some). So the first time the service gives a slot of 62 or above (and when a snapshot finds one in use), it writes **a record of a new kind, 18 (`wide`)**, flushed before the first record about that slot. A build that does not know kind 18 reads it as "not an outcome" and **refuses the log with status 15**, which is what that status means (section 5 of the runbook), and nothing is changed. A snapshot writes the marker if and only if a slot of 62 or above has an owner, so a directory that is back to 62 slots or fewer after a snapshot is readable by the old build again. Not a format version: the header is the first record of a file and cannot be written later, and a log that has not used a wide slot has no reason to say it is format 3.
* `logcheck.py` (the backup's checker) learns kind 18; `restore.sh` and `backup.sh` copy bytes and need nothing.

### 41.6 What happens at the limit

* `POST /endpoints` at 1,024: **`409`**, "the service has as many endpoints as it can (1024)", and nothing is written (as at 62: it was a 409, and a 507 is the text not fitting). A delete frees a slot at once unless an attempt of the deleted endpoint is on the wire (then `409`, "every slot is taken: a deleted endpoint is still finishing an attempt").
* A table or `endpoints.conf` with 1,025 endpoints: **status 13** naming the row or the line, nothing delivered (the message says "more than 1024 endpoints"). `--import-endpoints` refuses it before writing a row.
* A table whose text is over the limit: status 20, reason 6, as it was.

### 41.7 The gates, stated before the numbers

| gate | what passes |
|---|---|
| **memory, idle** | a start with 1,024 idle endpoints (file, and database): resident size within 25 MB of a start with 1; the cost of each endpoint is reported |
| **memory, busy** | 1,024 endpoints each with a window of failing events: resident size reported against the 40 KiB a window |
| **memory, worst case** | every window full and every dead-letter table full: reported, against 125 MiB |
| **hot path** | CPU a delivery at 1, 10 and 62 endpoints against `origin/main`, alternated runs: within noise (about 5 percent) |
| **per-turn cost** | 1,024 idle endpoints: CPU of the loop at idle within noise of one endpoint; one event to 1 of 1,024 subscribers costs a few looks, not 1,024 (`/stats` counts them); an outage of every endpoint with full windows: the longest turn under 50 ms |
| **the loop is not stalled** | the longest step (a snapshot of the largest state, a scrape of `/metrics`, `GET /endpoints`, a delete at index 0) reported for 1,024 endpoints; the snapshot of 1,024 full windows is reported and not hidden if it is long |
| **start time** | 1,024 endpoints idle; and 1,024 x 2,048 dead letters (2,097,152 records in `delivery.seg`): reported |
| **every event reaches every endpoint** | 1,024 endpoints, kill -9 as a power cut at random, receivers that fail some of the time: every event at each endpoint at least once, the cursors at the end, the log the same after a restart |
| **disabled, paused and replays in every slot** | slots 0, 61, 62, 63, 64, 127, 128, 511, 1,023 and the rest, by a log written by hand and by the API: the flags survive a restart, a disabled endpoint is sent nothing, enabling resumes it |
| **the limit** | 1,024 created through the API, the 1,025th a `409` with nothing stored; a table of 1,025 rows is status 13 |
| **old log, new log** | a log from main (no wide slot) read as it was and no kind 18 written; a log with a wide slot refused by a build without kind 18 (checked with the binary of `origin/main`), and not refused after a snapshot back at 62 |
| **answers fit** | `GET /endpoints` and `/metrics` at 1,024: every page under 64 KiB with the largest ids and values; the pages together are every endpoint once |
| **backup, restore, retention, the pool** | with 1,024 endpoints: backup and restore keep every cursor; an event pinned by a paused endpoint in slot 1,000 is not dropped; a start with the database away answers 503 and reads the 1,024 when it comes back |
| **mutants** | each line of the new code with a mutant that a test kills, or an argument that it is equivalent |

### 41.8 `--compact-now` with a database (the follow-up of section 37)

`--compact-now 1` refuses (status 44, nothing changed) when a database is named, because the service stopped waiting for the endpoints table (section 37) and without the table there are no cursors, so every event is "final everywhere" and a snapshot would be empty. The fix is not to give up the refusal's reason but to meet it: **connect through the same pool code, wait for the read of the table bounded by `pg-start-wait-ms`, and only then do the pass.** The decision `dbup.verdict` already makes (a refusal for good ends at once, a connection that cannot be made or a server that does not answer ends after the wait; 0 waits for ever) is the one used, and its statuses are the service's own: **status 20 with the reason on stderr** (the endpoints could not be read from the database), distinct from 43 (the lock is held) and 44 (a step failed). Nothing is changed in that case: no segment sealed, nothing dropped, `delivery.seg` untouched. The retention gate stays what it was: `rt_floor` answers 0 until `history.endpoints_known`, and `compact_once` checks it again after the wait, so a path that skips the load cannot drop or snapshot. The tests (`tests/pgre_test.py` stage 15, `tests/retention_test.py`) asserted the refusal; they now assert the pass, the refusal with the database unreachable, and that nothing was touched in that case.

### 41.9 What was built, and where it differs from the design above

The design above is unchanged; this is what building it showed.

* **Reading a page nobody wrote is not free on every kernel.** The design said a window is cleared "by reading it and writing only what is not zero". On the kernel of the machine this was built on (a Firecracker guest, 6.18) a *read* of an untouched anonymous page makes a real page resident (a program that only read 100 MB of fresh `mmap` had a resident size 100 MB larger; on the kernels most hosts run the read maps the shared zero page and costs nothing). A resident size that depends on that is not a rule a test can state, so nothing reads a region it has no reason to: a per-slot flag (`off_wused`) says whether the cells of a window may hold something, and `reset_window` reads them only then; the headers of the dead-letter tables moved to a dense block like the extras' (`dead.ls`: `hb(e)` for the count, the floor and `settled`, the entries after all of them); the snapshot and `/metrics` read a window only up to the newest event (a caught-up endpoint has nothing to read). With those, a start with 1,024 endpoints is 0.28 MB resident more than a start with one on that kernel, which is the worst case for it.
* **The wake time and the skip test are as designed**, plus one thing the design did not have: an attempt that ends *while the pass is still going* (a connection that cannot even begin is recorded at once, in `start_one`) sets the endpoint's next time after the ones the pass had looked at, so the pass does not say anything (`c_attempts` is compared before and after).
* **A bug found by measuring, in the service before this change, that 1,024 endpoints made large:** an event that an endpoint's subscription does not want is *passed over*, and a turn passes over at most `most_skips()` of them (4,096, so that a backlog of them does not hold the loop). When that budget ran out the loop waited for its timer, up to 50 ms, so a backlog of events nobody wants drained at 82,000 pairs a second: 80 events a second at 1,024 endpoints (`scripts/bench/run.py many_idle`: 20,000 events did not finish in 8 minutes). A turn that stops for that reason now makes the next wait 0 (`ex_again`): the same 20,480,000 pairs take 21 s (1.0 us a pair). `/stats` has `waits_skipped` (how many waits were left out), which is what `tests/many_test.py` reads: a backlog of 1,500 events x 1,024 endpoints is 375 turns, each of which is followed by no wait. (Timing alone could not tell the build with the fix from the one without it on that test: the loop's waits are shorter than 50 ms when anything else wakes it, and the unfixed drain took 2.9 s against 0.7 s.)
* **The per-turn rule had a hole, found by watching the loop from outside.** The gate in 41.7 says the longest turn of an outage with every window full is under 50 ms. The first build passed the gate by the measure the bench used (a probe thread of the same Python process that posted the events: 582 ms, which was put down to the snapshot), but a probe in a process of its own, with the snapshot switched off (`many_bench.py busy_nosnap`: `delivery-log-bytes` of 4 GiB), saw turns of 100 to 180 ms and a longest `/healthz` of 635 ms. Cause: an endpoint's pass reads every cell of its window once to learn when to look again (`off_wake`), and after a start 1,024 endpoints with 1,024 failed events each are 1,048,576 cells, which the first turn walked whole (about 100 ns a cell). The quiet test does not help with the first walk, only with the ones after it. A turn now walks at most `most_walk()` = 65,536 cells in all (checked before an endpoint's pass, so a turn may walk one window more), stops there with `step < count`, and makes the next wait 0 (`ex_again`, as for the skip budget); the endpoints it did not reach have `off_wake` 0 and are the next turn's. 62 full windows are 63,488 cells, so a deployment of 62 endpoints or fewer never meets the bound and loops as before. With it the same run has a longest `/healthz` of 33 ms (41.12). Tested by `quiet` (a restart over 1,024 x 100 failed events: `waits_skipped` >= 1, the walk ends, nothing is looked at afterwards) and four mutants (Q13 to Q16).
* **`GET /endpoints` with `limit=256` is not 4 pages**: a page also ends where its text passes 40 KiB (the endpoints with the longest lists of types and headers are 1.5 KB each), so it is 5.
* `/stats` has `max_endpoints`, `turns`, `endpoints_looked_at` and `waits_skipped`. (`max-endpoints` was put in `GET /config` first and taken out: it is a property of the build, not a setting, and `tests/config_test.py` compares that answer whole.)
* `--compact-now` with a database is **status 20** (the endpoints could not be read) after `pg-start-wait-ms`, with the message of the service's own start (`cannot connect`, `cannot log in`, `the database did not answer in time`, ...), and no file touched. It waits on a poller of its own for the pool's connections; nothing else runs. It reads the table as the service does (`load_late`), so a row the log does not know yet is given a slot and a `created` record, which the next start would have written.

### 41.10 Tests

`tests/many_test.py` (with `tests/manykit.py`: one receiver that is every endpoint's, by the address a connection came to, verifying each signature with that endpoint's own key), ten stages, each against a PostgreSQL of its own; `STAGES=name,name` runs some.

| stage | what it checks |
|---|---|
| `limit` | 1,024 endpoints created through `POST /endpoints` (1.2 s), the 1,025th is a `409` that says 1024 and stores nothing; `GET /endpoints` in pages that follow `X-Next-Offset` (16 of 64, every endpoint once, none over 40 KB), `limit=257` and `offset=-1` are `400`; a `created` record for each of the 1,024 slots, the marker (kind 18) once and before the first of slot 62, the header's 62 unchanged; one event reaches every endpoint exactly once, signed with its own key; the 16 pages of `/metrics` (a 17th is `404`, the longest under 56 KB, the service's series on page 0 only, every endpoint once, 9 families each); a restart reads the 1,024, writes no slot record and no second marker, sends nothing again; a delete frees a slot and a `POST` takes it; a table of 1,025 rows ends the service with status 13 naming the row |
| `chaos` | five starts and four `kill -9` as a power cut (the `fsync` shim) while events are posted to 1,024 endpoints whose receivers fail the first attempt of three events in ten (a `500`, a reset, a close); every acknowledged event reaches every endpoint, every cursor is at the last, no signature is another endpoint's, the events are in the log, one more restart sends nothing again |
| `flags` | disabled (a `410` from the receiver, to the 174 of 214 slots that are not also paused; the 214 include 61 to 64, 127, 128, 255, 256, 511, 512, 1000, 1023), paused (the breaker's records, written by hand into a stopped service's log, in 158 slots), then enabled: nothing is sent to them, `GET /endpoints`, `/stats` and `/metrics` say the same, the flags and cursors are the same after a restart, `POST .../enable` on one wide slot of each kind resumes it and only it, with what it missed once each; a backup (`scripts/backup.sh`, which now reads kind 18) and a restore keep every flag and cursor |
| `replay` | a type that three endpoints (ids 3, 600, 1023) subscribe to: 1,021 pass it over and nothing is sent to them; `POST /events/:id/replay` goes to those three, a named replay to 1022, which does not subscribe; a `410` at endpoint 1000 makes a dead letter listed with its reason, `replay-dead` takes it and it is delivered; a replay to every endpoint of 1,021 is a `507` and stores nothing |
| `quiet` | after the start every endpoint is looked at once; with nothing to do turns go by and no endpoint is looked at; an event is one look at each and none again; 1,024 endpoints whose attempts fail are not looked at each turn while they wait, and their retries are made when due; 100 events refused by 1,024 endpoints and a restart (102,400 cells to walk, more than a turn's bound of 65,536): `waits_skipped` is at least 1, the walk ends, and nothing is looked at while the retries wait; three endpoints whose attempts end at once (a multicast address, refused by the kernel while the turn is still looking at the endpoint) are retried to the last attempt with no event to wake them; 1,500 events nobody subscribes to, found in the log by 1,024 endpoints (`endpoints.conf`, no database, no request to wake the loop), are passed over without a wait between the turns that stopped for the budget (`waits_skipped`) |
| `retention` | an event pinned by an endpoint in slot 1,000 that the breaker paused: 200 events of 2.5 KB (three segments) are not dropped across a snapshot of the outcomes log (made at the start: it has a `created` for each of 1,024) and a restart, the snapshot carries the marker once right after the header and the paused flag, `logcheck.py` reads the directory; enabled, the endpoint is sent the 200 once each and the segments go |
| `compactnow` | the database away: status 20 after about `pg-start-wait-ms`, the message, no file changed; with the database it reads the endpoints (3), does its pass, exits 0, and a second run drops what the first sealed; with one endpoint paused its events are not dropped |
| `pool` | the database away at the start (`PgProxy` cut): the service listens, `/readyz` is `503` naming the database, five events are taken, nothing is delivered; when the database is back the table of 1,024 (a reply of 215 KiB: each endpoint has a subscription of 125 bytes, so that the pool's input slab of 1 MiB and the table's text limit are exercised) is read and the five events reach every endpoint once; 1,500 events of a type nobody wants, taken meanwhile, are passed over without a wait between turns; `GET /endpoints?limit=256` of these long rows is pages that end at 40 KiB |
| `formats` | 62 endpoints write the log main wrote (the header, a `created` each, no marker) and a restart changes nothing; `--compact-now` with the database snapshots it with no marker; the 63rd endpoint takes slot 62 (the header's 62 owns nothing) and brings the marker; a record of kind 19 ends the start with status 15 and changes nothing; a table of 1,025 rows is status 13 naming row 1025; a table of 563 KB of text is status 20 `too large` |
| `oldbuild` | (`HOOKS_BIN_MAIN=` the binary of the build before; not in CI) that build's directory is read by this one with `delivery.seg` unchanged by a start and a stop; the build before refuses a log with a wide slot with status 15; after the wide endpoints are deleted and a snapshot is made it reads the log again |

The suites that assumed the limit were changed and are marked here: `slots_test.py` (stage 5: 1,024 slots taken before a newcomer reclaims a dormant one), `delete_test.py` (ids 98 to 150 moved to 5,098 to 5,150 so that an id is not also its own slot, stage 11, 12 and 16 at 1,024 endpoints and a slow endpoint that holds its attempt for 10 s under a deadline of 20 s, because a thread-per-connection receiver needs more than 1.5 s for 1,023 requests), `manage_test.py` (stage 10), `roster_test.py` (a table over the text limit is now 563 KB), `pgre_test.py` (stage 11: a table over the pool's slab is 2.4 MB; stage 15: `compact-now` with the database away is status 20 after the wait and with it there reads the table), `layout_test.py` (slots 0, 61 and 1,023). `sessions_test.py` (an `https` endpoint in slot 1,000 resumes its session: the sessions are kept for every slot, they were 64). Unit tests: 8 more (215 to 223: the page of the endpoints and of the metrics in `bulk_test`, the rows past their lengths, a delete's moves (the bytes of a moved row's headers too) and the last row of the block in `epx_test`, the last slot's table and the words `clear` leaves in `dead_test`, a page of 64 endpoints of the longest series in `ops_test`) and the changed ones (`endpoints_test`: 1,024 accepted and a 1,025th refused with its line, in boxes on the heap; `lim_test`, `state_test`).

### 41.11 Mutants

`tests/mutants/many.py` (60 mutants, `python3 scripts/mutate.py tests/mutants/many.py [ids]`: the tree is copied, each mutant built and run against the test sets it names until one fails, the file restored and compared byte for byte). **Run on the final code, under an emulated x86-64, after the merge (19 of the 60 when this was written):** 16 killed (Q01 to Q07, Q09, Q11 to Q14, Q16, F01 to F03; by `many:quiet`, `many:flags`, `limits`, `retry` and `delete`), Q15 not judged (the C compiler crashed under the emulation, not a verdict), and **two survive, both equivalent in behaviour**: Q08 takes away the reset of an endpoint's `wake` when a *replay's* attempt ends, and replays are started by their own loop, which `wake` does not gate (and a window pass that stopped at the endpoint's concurrency, which the end of a replay frees, has already set `wake` to 0); Q10 takes away the reset of `wake` when an endpoint is enabled, and while it was disabled its `wake` kept the earliest time any of its waiting events is due, so after the enable they are looked at when due, and a new event raises its reach whatever `wake` says. Both resets are kept: they make the skip safe without the argument. The whole list is run on CI's kind of machine by `.github/workflows/mutants.yml` (by hand, in shards); its result is recorded here when it has run.

### 41.12 Measured

One shared 4-core VM (Intel Xeon 2.10 GHz, Linux 6.18, 16 GB), other work on it; each figure is from `scripts/bench/run.py` or `scripts/bench/many_bench.py` of this change, and the build before is the binary of `origin/main` built the same way. Runs of the two builds were alternated. Which lex-sys built the measured binaries was not recorded. The idle figures need the zero fill of `box_slice` to be `calloc` (lex-sys #235, which the pin includes), so they were not taken on a compiler older than that; they were not taken again on the pinned compiler, and are to be re-measured on it, on a machine of its own, with the capacity page.

| gate (41.7) | result |
|---|---|
| **memory, idle** (`many_bench.py idle`, `endpoints.conf`, no database) | 1 endpoint 9.1 MB resident, 62 endpoints 9.2 MB, **1,024 endpoints 9.3 MB**: 0.15 MB more than one, so an idle endpoint costs about 0.15 KB of resident memory. Address space 325 MB whatever the number. The build before: 9.0 MB for one and **15.4 MB for 62** (about 105 KB each, written at the start), 205 MB of address space. |
| **memory, busy** (`busy`: 1,024 endpoints that nothing listens for and 1,024 events, every window full, 1,048,576 failed attempts) | **50 to 52 MB** resident (the 40 KiB a window is 41 MB of it), after 51 to 66 s; at rest afterwards 0 endpoints looked at a turn, 0.1 to 0.2 percent of a core |
| **memory, worst case** (`worst`: 1,024 endpoints and 2,048 dead letters each, written into `delivery.seg` by `scripts/bench/mklog.c`, 162 MB, 2,097,152 records) | **73 to 97 MB** resident and **12.9 to 16.0 s** to `listening` (three starts, 16.0, 12.9, 13.9; the first reads the file cold). Every window full **and** every table full at once (125 MiB by the sum) was not run together: the two halves were measured apart. |
| **hot path** (`run.py`, CPU of the service a delivery, two alternated rounds each) | 1 endpoint: **100 and 100 us** before, **69 and 67 us** now; 10 endpoints: 74 and 76 before, **47 and 45** now; 62 endpoints: 81 and 83 before, **50 and 52** now; 1,024 endpoints (100 events, 102,400 deliveries): **53 and 53 us** (the build before cannot). Not slower, and on this machine 30 to 40 percent less CPU (a delivery's share of the turn's per-endpoint reads is gone with the skip test; the reason was not isolated by a build of its own). The 40.9 and 39.8 figures (76 and 73 us at ten endpoints) were taken on another day under another load; the comparison that counts is the alternated one. |
| **per-turn cost, at rest** | 1,024 endpoints that have caught up: 0.1 percent of a core, the same as one endpoint (0.0 to 0.1) and the build before at 62 (0.2); no endpoint looked at in any turn (`tests/many_test.py quiet`) |
| **per-turn cost, an outage with every window full** (`busy_nosnap`, a probe in a process of its own asking `/healthz` every 2 ms) | before the bound of 41.9: turns of 100 to 180 ms (`/healthz` up to 635 ms); with `most_walk()`: the longest `/healthz` **33 ms** over a 51 s run of 1,048,576 failed attempts |
| **the loop is not stalled** | the **snapshot of the outcomes log with every window of 1,024 endpoints full holds the loop for 563 ms** (three runs: 473, 563 and 581 ms; 40 to 70 ms for 62). It is one turn, as it always was, and it is proportional to the state, not bounded below the limit: `maintenance_ms_max` says it, and it happens when `delivery.seg` has passed `delivery-log-bytes` (default 64 MiB, a run of 1,048,576 failures is 120 to 162 MB of log). A scrape of `/metrics` (every page): 0.3 to 3.1 ms a page, the largest 33.8 KB (short ids and values; the longest page tested is under 56 KB); `GET /endpoints` in pages: 0.2 to 0.4 ms, the largest 10.7 KB; a `DELETE` of the first of 1,024: 2.3 ms with the database's answer included |
| **start time** | 1,024 idle endpoints: 60 ms from exec to `listening` (34 ms for one); from the table: 63 ms from exec to `endpoints loaded`; the worst case above 13 to 16 s |
| **every other gate** | the tests of 41.10; their runs are in `docs/production.md` P1.6 |

What was not measured: CPU a delivery with a database (the history) at 1,024 endpoints; the snapshot with both the windows and the tables full; a limit above 1,024; the CI database (SCRAM-SHA-256 behind a mapped port) for the figures (the tests ran against it in CI, and here with the pool code against a trusting local server and through `tests/pgproxy.py`).

### 41.13 What the user-facing documents should say

**Applied before the merge**: every row of the table below is now what the pages say (`README.md`, `docs/status.md`, `docs/endpoints.md`, `docs/api.md`, `docs/index.html`; the counts of unit tests by set and of harness programs were taken from a run of this commit). The line numbers are those of when this was written.

The README and the page are the coordinator's; this change edits only the pages that state its own facts (`docs/api.md`, `docs/operating.md`, `docs/retention.md`, `docs/configuration.md`, `docs/testing.md`). The statements below mention the old limit or the old facts and are left for whoever reconciles the wording.

**The limit, in one statement.** At most **1,024 endpoints** (an endpoint's id is still up to 999,999). It is a constant of the build (`state.max_endpoints()`, shown as `max_endpoints` in `GET /stats`), not a setting. At the 1,025th: `POST /endpoints` is a `409` that says 1024 and stores nothing; a table or `endpoints.conf` with more than 1,024 endpoints ends the start with status 13, naming the row or the line. **What it costs** (measured, one shared 4-core machine; 41.12): a start with 1,024 idle endpoints is 9.3 MB resident (9.1 MB with one; address space 325 MB either way), so an idle endpoint costs about 0.15 KB; an endpoint whose window is full of failed attempts costs its window, 40 KiB (1,024 of them: 50 to 52 MB resident); one whose table of dead letters is full costs up to 64 KiB more (1,024 of them: 73 to 97 MB, and 13 to 16 s to start). Worst case, both full: 125 MiB (computed; the two halves were measured apart). A delivery costs 53 us of one core at 1,024 endpoints (47 to 69 us at 1, 10 and 62, against 74 to 100 us on the build before): one event to every endpoint is 1,024 deliveries. The snapshot of the outcomes log holds the loop for 0.5 s with every window full (40 to 70 ms for 62); every other turn of that outage is under 50 ms (33 ms longest).
